[p3 | prose | Asbestos removal]
Asbestos removal

[p3 | prose | KEY POINTS]
KEY POINTS

[p3 | prose | KEY POINTS]
Buildings built before 1 January 2000 are likely

[p3 | prose | KEY POINTS]
to contain asbestos-containing materials (ACMs).

[p3 | prose | KEY POINTS]
For buildings built after 1 January 2000, the risk

[p3 | prose | KEY POINTS]
of asbestos material being present is lower.

[p3 | prose | KEY POINTS]
The removal of asbestos is covered by the Health

[p3 | prose | the Health and Safety at Work (General Risk and]
the Health and Safety at Work (General Risk and

[p3 | prose | Workplace Management) Regulations 2016, and]
Workplace Management) Regulations 2016, and

[p3 | prose | the Health and Safety at Work Act 2015.]
the Health and Safety at Work Act 2015.

[p3 | prose | the Health and Safety at Work Act 2015.]
There are different classes of asbestos removal.

[p3 | prose | the Health and Safety at Work Act 2015.]
Each class has different regulatory requirements

[p3 | prose | the Health and Safety at Work Act 2015.]
depending on how much asbestos material is being

[p3 | prose | the Health and Safety at Work Act 2015.]
removed and the friability of the asbestos material

[p3 | prose | the Health and Safety at Work Act 2015.]
being removed.

[p3 | prose | the Health and Safety at Work Act 2015.]
Businesses involved in the removal of asbestos must

[p3 | prose | the Health and Safety at Work Act 2015.]
ensure the health and safety of their workers and any

[p3 | prose | the Health and Safety at Work Act 2015.]
other people that could be put at risk by the work that

[p3 | prose | the Health and Safety at Work Act 2015.]
they do.

[p3 | prose | the Health and Safety at Work Act 2015.]
These guidelines do not cover removal of asbestos

[p3 | prose | the Health and Safety at Work Act 2015.]
from contaminated land. For more information, see

[p3 | prose | New Zealand Guidelines for Assessing and Managing]
New Zealand Guidelines for Assessing and Managing

[p3 | prose | Asbestos in Soil]

[p4 | prose | Use of ‘must’ and ‘should’]
Use of ‘must’ and ‘should’

[p4 | prose | Use of ‘must’ and ‘should’]
The words ‘must’ and ‘should’ indicate whether:

[p4 | prose | Use of ‘must’ and ‘should’]
– an action is required by law, or

[p4 | prose | Use of ‘must’ and ‘should’]
– is a recommended practice or approach.

[p4 | table | Use of ‘must’ and ‘should’]
| TERM | DEFINITION |
| --- | --- |
| Must | Legal requirement that must be complied with |
| Should | Recommended practice or approach. |
| Alternative approaches may be adopted, if those provide for equivalent or greater levels of safety. |  |

[p15 | prose | Images]
Asbestos can harm people’s health if it is not removed safely. These guidelines help asbestos removalists to manage risks related to asbestos removal and comply with relevant regulations.

[p15 | prose | 1.1 What are these guidelines about?]
1.1 What are these guidelines about?

[p15 | prose | 1.1 What are these guidelines about?]
These guidelines provide good practice advice for the safe removal of asbestos from buildings and workplaces.

[p15 | prose | 1.1 What are these guidelines about?]
These guidelines will help PCBUs that conduct asbestos removal (called removalists in these guidelines) to comply with:

[p15 | prose | 1.1 What are these guidelines about?]
– the Health and Safety at Work Act 2015 (HSWA)

[p15 | prose | 1.1 What are these guidelines about?]
– the Health and Safety at Work (Asbestos) Regulations 2016

[p15 | prose | 1.1 What are these guidelines about?]
– the Health and Safety at Work (General Risk and Workplace Management) Regulations 2016

[p15 | prose | 1.1 What are these guidelines about?]
These guidelines do not cover removal of asbestos from contaminated land. For more information, see New Zealand Guidelines for Assessing and Managing

[p15 | prose | 1.2 Who should read these guidelines?]
1.2 Who should read these guidelines?

[p15 | prose | 1.2 Who should read these guidelines?]
These guidelines are for any PCBUs that carry out asbestos removal. There are two main types of asbestos removalist:

[p15 | prose | 1.2 Who should read these guidelines?]
– licensed asbestos removalists:

[p15 | prose | 1.2 Who should read these guidelines?]
those licensed to remove friable and non-friable asbestos (a Class A licence)

[p15 | prose | 1.2 Who should read these guidelines?]
those licensed to remove non-friable asbestos (a Class B licence)

[p15 | prose | 1.2 Who should read these guidelines?]
– unlicensed asbestos removalists:

[p15 | prose | 1.2 Who should read these guidelines?]
those who remove restricted quantities of non-friable asbestos as part of other work they do (for example, some tradespeople).

[p16 | prose | Friable and non-friable asbestos]
Friable and non-friable asbestos

[p16 | prose | Friable and non-friable asbestos]
Asbestos can be classified into two categories based on its condition:

[p16 | prose | Friable and non-friable asbestos]
– Friable asbestos is flaky or powdery. It can be crumbled or reduced to a powder without much pressure. Friable asbestos can easily release fibres into the air. Friable asbestos is sometimes referred to as Class A asbestos.

[p16 | prose | Friable and non-friable asbestos]
– Non-friable asbestos usually has asbestos fibres bonded into another material such as cement. Non-friable asbestos is less likely to release asbestos fibres into the air unless it is disturbed or has started to deteriorate. Non-friable asbestos is sometimes referred to as Class B asbestos.

[p16 | prose | Friable and non-friable asbestos]
For more information about asbestos types, see WorkSafe’s webpages:

[p16 | prose | Friable and non-friable asbestos]
– Asbestos in Aotearoa New Zealand

[p16 | prose | Friable and non-friable asbestos]
– Asbestos in the home

[p16 | prose | Friable and non-friable asbestos]
These guidelines may also help PCBUs working in other parts of the asbestos management contracting chain including:

[p16 | prose | Friable and non-friable asbestos]
– PCBUs that own, lease, or manage a building that has or could have asbestos in it

[p16 | prose | Friable and non-friable asbestos]
– PCBUs that do asbestos surveys

[p16 | prose | Friable and non-friable asbestos]
– PCBUs that do asbestos assessments (assessors)

[p16 | prose | Friable and non-friable asbestos]
– building surveyors.

[p16 | prose | Friable and non-friable asbestos]
These guidelines are not intended for people who are not a PCBU (such as homeowners and residential tenants).

[p16 | prose | 1.3 Where to find other information about asbestos and asbestos management]
1.3 Where to find other information about asbestos and asbestos management

[p16 | prose | 1.3 Where to find other information about asbestos and asbestos management]
This guide focuses specifically on good practice for removing asbestos from buildings and workplaces. There is guidance available for other aspects of the management of asbestos.

[p16 | prose | 1.3 Where to find other information about asbestos and asbestos management]
This guide should be read in conjunction with:

[p16 | prose | 1.3 Where to find other information about asbestos and asbestos management]
– Asbestos in New Zealand – information about what asbestos is, the risks of asbestos and why it should be managed

[p16 | prose | 1.3 Where to find other information about asbestos and asbestos management]
– Managing asbestos in your building or workplace – guidelines for PCBUs about how to manage asbestos in their building or workplace (including when to engage an asbestos surveyor to assist with this)

[p16 | prose | 1.3 Where to find other information about asbestos and asbestos management]
– Protective clothing and equipment for working with or near asbestos – guidance for PCBUs that carry out any work where there is a risk of exposure to asbestos fibres

[p16 | prose | 1.3 Where to find other information about asbestos and asbestos management]
– Conducting asbestos surveys – good practice guidelines

[p16 | prose | 1.3 Where to find other information about asbestos and asbestos management]
– Asbestos assessments – good practice guidelines

[p16 | prose | 1.3 Where to find other information about asbestos and asbestos management]
– Asbestos in the home – information for homeowners about how to manage asbestos in their home, and how to engage asbestos professionals for the safe management and removal of asbestos

[p16 | prose | 1.3 Where to find other information about asbestos and asbestos management]
– The Health and Safety at Work (Asbestos) Regulations 2016 – interpretive guidelines

[p16 | prose | 1.3 Where to find other information about asbestos and asbestos management]
– New Zealand Guidelines for Assessing and Managing Asbestos in Soil

[p17 | prose | 1.4 The Health and Safety at Work Act 2015 (HSWA)]
1.4 The Health and Safety at Work Act 2015 (HSWA)

[p17 | prose | 1.4 The Health and Safety at Work Act 2015 (HSWA)]
HSWA is the primary work health and safety legislation in New Zealand. HSWA applies to all work and workplaces unless specifically excluded.

[p17 | prose | Primary duty of care]
Primary duty of care

[p17 | prose | Primary duty of care]
All PCBUs (including removalists) have a primary duty of care under HSWA. The primary duty of care means that PCBUs must make sure, so far as is reasonably practicable, the health and safety of:

[p17 | prose | Primary duty of care]
– any workers that are influenced by their business (for example, their own workers, workers of other PCBUs, contractors, subcontractors, and apprentices)

[p17 | prose | Primary duty of care]
– other people that could be put at risk by their work (for example, tenants, visitors, customers, and passers-by).

[p17 | prose | Primary duty of care]
For removalists, this duty includes any risks a worksite may create while they have control over it for asbestos removal (from the start to the end of the removal process, not just while asbestos is actively being removed).

[p17 | prose | Primary duty of care]
More information about the primary duty of care (and other duties under HSWA) is available on the WorkSafe website Introduction to the Health and Safety at Work Act 2015 – special guide

[p17 | prose | Overlapping duties]
Overlapping duties

[p17 | prose | Overlapping duties]
When working with other asbestos professionals in a contracting chain (such as with building owners, surveyors and assessors) removalists are likely to have overlapping duties. Sometimes, multiple PCBUs share the same health and safety duties for an activity. This is called overlapping duties.

[p17 | prose | Overlapping duties]
Under HSWA, where there are overlapping duties, all PCBUs involved must, so far as is reasonably practicable:

[p17 | prose | Overlapping duties]
– consult each other

[p17 | prose | Overlapping duties]
– cooperate with each other

[p17 | prose | Overlapping duties]
– coordinate their activities.

[p17 | prose | Overlapping duties]
This is to make sure that all workers across all the PCBUs, (and other people) are not put at risk before, during, and after the asbestos removal process.

[p17 | prose | Overlapping duties]
For more information about managing overlapping duties, see WorkSafe’s webpage Overlapping duties

[p17 | prose | Overlapping duties]
More information about managing risk in a contracting chain, see WorkSafe’s webpage PCBUs working together: advice when contracting

[p17 | prose | 1.5 The Health and Safety at Work (Asbestos) Regulations 2016]
1.5 The Health and Safety at Work (Asbestos) Regulations 2016

[p17 | prose | 1.5 The Health and Safety at Work (Asbestos) Regulations 2016]
The Health and Safety at Work (Asbestos) Regulations 2016 specify how to manage asbestos risks. Complying with regulations made under HSWA is mandatory.

[p17 | prose | 1.5 The Health and Safety at Work (Asbestos) Regulations 2016]
Removalists have specific duties under the Asbestos Regulations. They cover (for example) requirements for:

[p17 | prose | 1.5 The Health and Safety at Work (Asbestos) Regulations 2016]
– licensing requirements for removalists

[p17 | prose | 1.5 The Health and Safety at Work (Asbestos) Regulations 2016]
– training and supervision

[p17 | prose | 1.5 The Health and Safety at Work (Asbestos) Regulations 2016]
– asbestos removal control plans (ARCPs)

[p17 | prose | 1.5 The Health and Safety at Work (Asbestos) Regulations 2016]
– notifying WorkSafe and others about removal work

[p17 | prose | 1.5 The Health and Safety at Work (Asbestos) Regulations 2016]
– signage

[p17 | prose | 1.5 The Health and Safety at Work (Asbestos) Regulations 2016]
– controlling access to removal areas

[p18 | prose | 1.5 The Health and Safety at Work (Asbestos) Regulations 2016]
– decontamination

[p18 | prose | 1.5 The Health and Safety at Work (Asbestos) Regulations 2016]
– disposal of asbestos waste and used personal protective equipment (PPE)

[p18 | prose | 1.5 The Health and Safety at Work (Asbestos) Regulations 2016]
– clearance inspections and certificates

[p18 | prose | 1.5 The Health and Safety at Work (Asbestos) Regulations 2016]
– air monitoring.

[p18 | prose | 1.5 The Health and Safety at Work (Asbestos) Regulations 2016]
For a detailed explanation of the asbestos regulations, see The Health and Safety at Work (Asbestos) Regulations 2016 – interpretive guidelines

[p18 | prose | 1.6 Health and Safety at Work (General Risk and Workplace Management) Regulations 2016 (The GRWM Regulations)]
1.6 Health and Safety at Work (General Risk and Workplace Management) Regulations 2016 (The GRWM Regulations)

[p18 | prose | 1.6 Health and Safety at Work (General Risk and Workplace Management) Regulations 2016 (The GRWM Regulations)]
The General Risk and Workplace Management Regulations covers general requirements for all workplaces and more specific requirements for high-risk work which includes asbestos removal. They cover (for example) requirements for:

[p18 | prose | 1.6 Health and Safety at Work (General Risk and Workplace Management) Regulations 2016 (The GRWM Regulations)]
– managing risks associated with substances hazardous to health

[p18 | prose | 1.6 Health and Safety at Work (General Risk and Workplace Management) Regulations 2016 (The GRWM Regulations)]
– PPE

[p18 | prose | 1.6 Health and Safety at Work (General Risk and Workplace Management) Regulations 2016 (The GRWM Regulations)]
– training

[p18 | prose | 1.6 Health and Safety at Work (General Risk and Workplace Management) Regulations 2016 (The GRWM Regulations)]
– exposure monitoring

[p18 | prose | 1.6 Health and Safety at Work (General Risk and Workplace Management) Regulations 2016 (The GRWM Regulations)]
– health monitoring.

[p18 | prose | 1.6 Health and Safety at Work (General Risk and Workplace Management) Regulations 2016 (The GRWM Regulations)]
For a detailed explanation of the GRWM Regulations, see:

[p18 | prose | 1.6 Health and Safety at Work (General Risk and Workplace Management) Regulations 2016 (The GRWM Regulations)]
– General risk and workplace management – part 1

[p18 | prose | 1.6 Health and Safety at Work (General Risk and Workplace Management) Regulations 2016 (The GRWM Regulations)]
– General risk and workplace management – part 2

[p20 | prose | 1.6 Health and Safety at Work (General Risk and Workplace Management) Regulations 2016 (The GRWM Regulations)]
Unlicensed asbestos removal can still expose people to harmful asbestos fibres. It is important to manage the risks effectively and follow good practice.

[p20 | prose | 2.1 What is unlicensed asbestos removal?]
2.1 What is unlicensed asbestos removal?

[p20 | prose | 2.1 What is unlicensed asbestos removal?]
Unlicensed asbestos removal involves the removal of up to 10m2 of non-friable asbestos. Unlicensed asbestos removal can be carried out by an asbestos removalist or another tradesperson (such as plumbers, electricians, or roofers).

[p20 | prose | 2.1 What is unlicensed asbestos removal?]
For asbestos removal to be unlicensed, all the following apply:

[p20 | prose | 2.1 What is unlicensed asbestos removal?]
– only non-friable asbestos or asbestos-contaminated dust may be removed (up to 10m2)

[p20 | prose | 2.1 What is unlicensed asbestos removal?]
– the 10m2 restriction applies cumulatively to the whole asbestos removal project for the site.

[p20 | prose | 2.1 What is unlicensed asbestos removal?]
Important: These requirements cannot be avoided by dividing a large site into sectors of 10m2 or less and removing asbestos from each sector.

[p20 | prose | 2.1 What is unlicensed asbestos removal?]
If it is unclear whether the area of asbestos material to be removed is over or under 10m2, the area should be carefully measured.

[p20 | prose | 2.1 What is unlicensed asbestos removal?]
Removal of more than 10m2 of non-asbestos material must be carried out by a licensed asbestos removalist.

[p20 | prose | 2.1 What is unlicensed asbestos removal?]
Removal of any amount of friable asbestos requires a Class A licence.

[p20 | prose | 2.1 What is unlicensed asbestos removal?]
See Section 3.0: Licensed asbestos removal for more information.

[p20 | prose | 2.1 What is unlicensed asbestos removal?]
Table 1 shows examples of unlicensed asbestos removal work.

[p20 | table | 2.1 What is unlicensed asbestos removal?]
| EXAMPLE | LICENCE CLASS REQUIRED |
| --- | --- |
| Removing a single non-friable asbestos cement sheet (area 2m?) to install an air conditioner | Licence is not required because the area of ACM is 10m? or less and is non-friable |
| Self-employed person has been engaged to remove a non-friable asbestos cement eave (1.6m?in total) to provide access for pipes | Licence is not required because the area of ACM is 10m? or less |

[p20 | prose | 2.1 What is unlicensed asbestos removal?]
Examples of unlicensed asbestos removal work

[p21 | prose | Recommended techniques for unlicensed asbestos removal]
Recommended techniques for unlicensed asbestos removal

[p21 | prose | Recommended techniques for unlicensed asbestos removal]
Unlicensed asbestos removal must still follow all applicable requirements of the Asbestos Regulations and HSWA, and removal must be done in accordance with good practices for safe removal of non-friable asbestos. PCBU's must make sure they have the right skills and experience to conduct unlicensed removal in accordance with the regulations.

[p21 | prose | Recommended techniques for unlicensed asbestos removal]
For detailed information on techniques for removing non-friable asbestos that does not need a licence see non-friable asbestos removal safe work practices

[p21 | prose | 2.2 Measuring non-flat surfaces]
2.2 Measuring non-flat surfaces

[p21 | prose | 2.2 Measuring non-flat surfaces]
Many objects containing asbestos are not flat. For these objects, it is acceptable to calculate the overall dimensions of the object’s surface. It is acceptable to ignore surface texture and holes when measuring asbestos.

[p21 | prose | How to apply the 10m2 rule to curved or uneven surfaces (such as asbestos piping)]
How to apply the 10m2 rule to curved or uneven surfaces (such as asbestos piping)

[p21 | prose | How to apply the 10m2 rule to curved or uneven surfaces (such as asbestos piping)]
Measure the external surface area of the pipe. This can be worked out by multiplying the outside circumference of the pipe by its length. This equals the surface area in square metres. Calculate the external surface area of a pipe using this equation:

[p21 | table | How to apply the 10m2 rule to curved or uneven surfaces (such as asbestos piping)]
| 10(m2) |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- |
| π x diameter (m) | = Max length (m) | No licence required |

[p21 | prose | How to apply the 10m2 rule to curved or uneven surfaces (such as asbestos piping)]
Example 1: For a 100mm (0.1m) pipe, 10m in length

[p21 | table | How to apply the 10m2 rule to curved or uneven surfaces (such as asbestos piping)]
| 3.14 x 0.1m x 10m | = 3.14m2 | No licence required |
| --- | --- | --- |

[p21 | prose | How to apply the 10m2 rule to curved or uneven surfaces (such as asbestos piping)]
For a 250mm (0.25m) pipe 15m in length

[p21 | table | How to apply the 10m2 rule to curved or uneven surfaces (such as asbestos piping)]
| 3.14 x 0.25m x 15m | = 11.77m2 | Licence required |
| --- | --- | --- |

[p21 | prose | How to apply the 10m2 rule to curved or uneven surfaces (such as asbestos piping)]
Example 2: Maximum length of 100mm (0.1m) pipe that can be removed without a licence

[p21 | table | How to apply the 10m2 rule to curved or uneven surfaces (such as asbestos piping)]
| 10(m2) | = 31.84m2 | Licence required |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 3.14 x 0.1 (m) |  |  |

[p21 | prose | How to apply the 10m2 rule to curved or uneven surfaces (such as asbestos piping)]
Maximum length of 250mm (0.25m) pipe that can be removed without a licence

[p21 | table | How to apply the 10m2 rule to curved or uneven surfaces (such as asbestos piping)]
| 10(m2) | = 12.73m2 | Licence required |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 3.14 x 0.25 (m) |  |  |

[p23 | prose | How to apply the 10m2 rule to curved or uneven surfaces (such as asbestos piping)]
Licensed asbestos removal involves higher-risk work that must be carried out by appropriately trained and authorised removalists.

[p23 | prose | 3.1 What is licensed asbestos removal?]
3.1 What is licensed asbestos removal?

[p23 | prose | 3.1 What is licensed asbestos removal?]
Licensed asbestos removal is the controlled and regulated activity of removing asbestos-containing material (ACM) from buildings or workplaces. Specific licences are required based on the type and amount of asbestos being removed.

[p23 | prose | 3.2 Classes of asbestos removal licences]
3.2 Classes of asbestos removal licences

[p23 | prose | 3.2 Classes of asbestos removal licences]
There are two types of licences available for conducting licensed asbestos removal.

[p23 | prose | 3.2 Classes of asbestos removal licences]
Class A asbestos removal licences

[p23 | prose | 3.2 Classes of asbestos removal licences]
A Class A asbestos removal licence permits the removal of any type or amount of asbestos and asbestos-contaminated dust or debris (ACD).

[p23 | prose | 3.2 Classes of asbestos removal licences]
A Class A asbestos removal licence is required for the removal of ACD unless the ACD is:

[p23 | prose | 3.2 Classes of asbestos removal licences]
– associated with and generated during Class B asbestos removal work, excluding ACD that was present before the work started

[p23 | prose | 3.2 Classes of asbestos removal licences]
– a ‘minor contamination’ not associated with friable or non-friable asbestos removal. See Section 4.2: What is minor contamination? for more information.

[p23 | prose | Class B asbestos removal licences]
Class B asbestos removal licences

[p23 | prose | Class B asbestos removal licences]
A Class B asbestos removal licence permits the removal of:

[p23 | prose | Class B asbestos removal licences]
– any amount of non-friable asbestos

[p23 | prose | Class B asbestos removal licences]
– ACD associated with and generated during the removal of non-friable asbestos or ACM, excluding ACD that was present before the work started.

[p23 | prose | Class B asbestos removal licences]
Table 2 shows the licence required depending on the type and quantity of asbestos material being removed.

[p24 | table | Class B asbestos removal licences]
| TYPE OF LICENCE | WHAT ASBESTOS CAN BE REMOVED? |
| --- | --- |
| Class A | Any type or quantity of asbestos material, including: = any amount of friable asbestos material - any amount of ACD = any amount of non-friable asbestos material. |
| Class B | Any amount of non-friable asbestos material. ACD associated with and generated during the removal of non-friable asbestos or ACM. This excludes ACD that was present before the work started. |
| No licence required | Up to and including 10m? of non-friable asbestos material, cumulatively over the whole course of the removal project for the site. Asbestos-contaminated dust (ACD) associated with and generated during removal of 10m? or less of non-friable asbestos material. This excludes ACD that was present before the work started. |

[p24 | prose | Class B asbestos removal licences]
TABLE 2:

[p24 | prose | Class B asbestos removal licences]
Licensed and unlicensed asbestos removal

[p24 | prose | Class B asbestos removal licences]
– Class B licensed asbestos removalist contracted by a building owner PCBU to remove 12m2 of non-friable asbestos cement sheets from a factory toilet block.

[p24 | prose | Class B asbestos removal licences]
– Class A licensed asbestos removalist contracted by a building owner PCBU to remove 0.5m2 of friable asbestos lagging from a pipe.

[p24 | prose | 3.3 Changes to friability and classification of removal work]
3.3 Changes to friability and classification of removal work

[p24 | prose | 3.3 Changes to friability and classification of removal work]
In most cases the friable and non-friable status of asbestos is determined by the condition of the asbestos before it is disturbed for removal.

[p24 | prose | 3.3 Changes to friability and classification of removal work]
However, in some cases the classification of friability of the asbestos can change based on the method of removal used. Table 3 outlines examples of where the method of removal may change the friability and therefore the conditions under which it can or must be removed

[p24 | table | 3.3 Changes to friability and classification of removal work]
| PRODUCT TYPE AND ORIGINAL FRIABILITY | CAN BE TREATED AS NON-FRIABLE AND BE REMOVED UNDER CLASS B CONDITIONS IF: | MUST BE TREATED AS FRIABLE AND REMOVED UNDER CLASS A CONDITIONS IF: |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Hot water cylinder with internal asbestos insulation Friable | Removed and disposed of as a complete unit and the asbestos inside remains fully contained | Cylinder is opened up or broken down in any way for removal or disposal |
| Fire door with AIB core Friable | Removed and disposed of as a complete unit and the asbestos inside remains fully contained | The core of the door is exposed in any way during removal or disposal. This includes where removed screws or hardware expose the core |
| Asbestos-containing paper backing attached to a non- asbestos vinyl Friable | Removed and disposed of as one piece with no disturbance to the paper backing (this will typically be limited to small rooms as the piece will need to fit through the door opening) | The paper backing is cut, torn or in any other way disturbed or separated from the vinyl it is attached to enable removal or disposal |
| Asbestos-containing vinyl flooring (the asbestos is present in the vinyl matrix itself) Non-friable | Removed and disposed of as a single piece and no asbestos-containing adhesive is left behind that cannot be reoved by solvent (this will typically be limited to small rooms as the piece will need to fit through the door opening) | The vinyl is cut or torn to enable removal, of if any remaining asbestos-containing adhesive requires power tools to remove |

[p25 | table | 3.3 Changes to friability and classification of removal work]
| PRODUCT TYPE AND ORIGINAL FRIABILITY | CAN BE TREATED AS NON-FRIABLE AND BE REMOVED UNDER CLASS B CONDITIONS IF: | MUST BE TREATED AS FRIABLE AND REMOVED UNDER CLASS A CONDITIONS IF: |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Asbestos rope inside a chimney Friable | Chimney removed and disposed of as one piece with no disturbance to the rope | The chimney needs to be broken down into pieces to enable removal and disposal |
| Textured coating Non-friable | Removed and disposed of while still attached to the substrate without disturbing the textured coating (this will typically be limited to small rooms as the piece will need to fit through the door opening) | The textured coating is disturbed via manual or mechanical scraping/lifting or cutting through the ceiling/wall |
| ‘Popcorn’ ceiling coating Friable | disposed attached to the substrate without disturbing the textured coating (this will typically be limited to small rooms as the piece will need to fit through the door opening) | ceiling or mechanical scraping/lifting or cutting through the ceiling |

[p25 | prose | 3.3 Changes to friability and classification of removal work]
TABLE 3: Examples of when method of removal may change asbestos friability

[p25 | prose | 3.3 Changes to friability and classification of removal work]
See Section 11: Controlled removal and repair techniques for more detailed information on some of the above removal techniques.

[p25 | prose | 3.3 Changes to friability and classification of removal work]
Any removal work that requires controls associated with friable asbestos removal should be treated as Class A asbestos removal work and only be done by a Class A asbestos removalist and subject to Class A asbestos clearance undertaken by an independent licensed asbestos assessor.

[p25 | prose | 3.4 Who can hold an asbestos removal licence?]
3.4 Who can hold an asbestos removal licence?

[p25 | prose | 3.4 Who can hold an asbestos removal licence?]
Only PCBUs can apply for and hold a Class A or Class B asbestos removal licence. WorkSafe administers Class A and B asbestos removal licences.

[p25 | prose | 3.4 Who can hold an asbestos removal licence?]
For more information about asbestos licensing, see WorkSafe’s webpage Asbestos licensing

[p25 | prose | 3.5 Asbestos removal licence register]
3.5 Asbestos removal licence register

[p25 | prose | 3.5 Asbestos removal licence register]
WorkSafe keeps a register of every currently licensed, cancelled and expired asbestos removalist. The register includes:

[p25 | prose | 3.5 Asbestos removal licence register]
– the licence holder name

[p25 | prose | 3.5 Asbestos removal licence register]
– licence status

[p25 | prose | 3.5 Asbestos removal licence register]
– licence number

[p25 | prose | 3.5 Asbestos removal licence register]
– licence class

[p25 | prose | 3.5 Asbestos removal licence register]
– licence expiry date

[p25 | prose | 3.5 Asbestos removal licence register]
– nominated supervisor name(s).

[p25 | prose | 3.5 Asbestos removal licence register]
For more information, see WorkSafe’s website Asbestos licence holder registers

[p27 | prose | Removal of ACD is treated separately under the regulations to friable and non-friable asbestos removal.]
Removal of ACD is treated separately under the regulations to friable and non-friable asbestos removal.

[p27 | prose | 4.1 Licence requirements for removing ACD]
4.1 Licence requirements for removing ACD

[p27 | prose | 4.1 Licence requirements for removing ACD]
Asbestos-contaminated dust or debris (ACD) means dust or debris that has settled within a workplace or home and is, or is assumed to be, contaminated with asbestos.

[p27 | prose | 4.1 Licence requirements for removing ACD]
Removal of asbestos containing dust (ACD) is treated separately under the regulations to friable (Class A) and non-friable (Class B) asbestos removal. Regulatory requirements for the removal of ACD depends on how the ADC was created and how much is present.

[p27 | prose | 4.1 Licence requirements for removing ACD]
ACD must be removed by a Class A asbestos removalist unless it is:

[p27 | prose | 4.1 Licence requirements for removing ACD]
– associated with non-friable asbestos removal, or

[p27 | prose | 4.1 Licence requirements for removing ACD]
– a ‘minor contamination’ not associated with friable or non-friable asbestos removal.

[p27 | prose | 4.1 Licence requirements for removing ACD]
See Table 4, further explanations and examples below.

[p27 | table | 4.1 Licence requirements for removing ACD]
|  | ACD ASSOCIATED WITH REMOVAL (any amount) | CONTAMINATION (that is not minor and not associated with removal) | MINOR CONTAMINATION (not associated with removal) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| ADC comes from a friable source | Class A | Class A | Class B |
| ACD comes from a non-friable source | Class B | Class A | Class B |
| ACD source was originally non- friable, but condition has deteriorated to become friable | Class A | Class A | Class B |

[p27 | prose | 4.1 Licence requirements for removing ACD]
TABLE 4: Licence requirements for different ACD removal scenarios

[p27 | prose | ACD associated with removal of non-friable asbestos]
ACD associated with removal of non-friable asbestos

[p27 | prose | ACD associated with removal of non-friable asbestos]
ACD associated with removal of non-friable asbestos means ACD generated by or discovered during asbestos removal activities. These activities include site set up/pre clean and non-friable asbestos removal. For example: when removing an asbestos cement roof and settled ACD is discovered after lifting roof material (this can be dealt with under the Class B licence).

[p28 | prose | ACD associated with removal of asbestos that was originally non-friable but condition has deteriorated to become friable]
ACD associated with removal of asbestos that was originally non-friable but condition has deteriorated to become friable

[p28 | prose | ACD associated with removal of asbestos that was originally non-friable but condition has deteriorated to become friable]
Where accumulated quantities of ACD have been generated by a non-friable asbestos source, and there is a sufficient quantity of ACD that it is identifiable before work has started, it should be assumed that the nature of the source material has changed due to deterioration or damage (accidental or due to an event such as fire). The source material should be reclassified as friable, and the removal of it, and the ACD generated by it, must be undertaken by a Class A removalist.

[p28 | prose | ACD not associated with removal]
ACD not associated with removal

[p28 | prose | ACD not associated with removal]
ACD not associated with removal includes where historically asbestos material was or might have been present. In this scenario ACD must be seen as a stand alone asbestos contamination and, unless a minor contamination, must be removed by a Class A removalist.

[p28 | prose | 4.2 What is minor contamination?]
4.2 What is minor contamination?

[p28 | prose | 4.2 What is minor contamination?]
There is no legal definition of minor contamination. To determine whether a contamination of ACD is a minor contamination, the asbestos removalist or competent person will need to carry out a risk assessment.

[p28 | prose | 4.2 What is minor contamination?]
Relevant considerations include:

[p28 | prose | 4.2 What is minor contamination?]
– the time it would take for a person to carry out the clean-up job

[p28 | prose | 4.2 What is minor contamination?]
– the size, area and extent of the contamination

[p28 | prose | 4.2 What is minor contamination?]
– the number of workers and persons who will be or are likely to be involved in or exposed to the work

[p28 | prose | 4.2 What is minor contamination?]
– the complexity of the work being undertaken

[p28 | prose | 4.2 What is minor contamination?]
– the knowledge and skills required to complete the work safely

[p28 | prose | 4.2 What is minor contamination?]
– the risks associated with the work and the complexity of the risk control measures.

[p28 | prose | 4.2 What is minor contamination?]
The amount of ACD cannot exceed what would, in other circumstances, be associated with safely removing 10m2 or less of non-friable asbestos.

[p28 | prose | 4.2 What is minor contamination?]
Example 1: Removing a minor ACD contamination from a small area, such as an electrical box lined with Bakelite

[p28 | table | 4.2 What is minor contamination?]
| Time to clean up | 10 minutes for one worker |
| --- | --- |
| Area | Small |
| Distribution | No further spread of ACD to adjoining areas |
| Rick of exposure | Very low |

[p28 | prose | 4.2 What is minor contamination?]

[p31 | prose | Example 5: A house asbestos cement roof has been cleaned using high- pressure water, leaving dried ACD spread over a large area]
Asbestos removal workers must be suitably trained and qualified to do the job safely. Provide clear instructions and make sure they are competent before any removal work begins.

[p31 | prose | 5.1 Workers must have the right training and information]
5.1 Workers must have the right training and information

[p31 | prose | 5.1 Workers must have the right training and information]
PCBUs must make sure that workers engaged in asbestos removal work or carrying out asbestos-related work, are trained in the identification and safe handling of, and suitable control measures for, removal of asbestos and ACM.

[p31 | prose | 5.1 Workers must have the right training and information]
WorkSafe strongly recommends regular refresher training for all people working with asbestos.

[p31 | prose | 5.2 Qualifications for asbestos removal workers]
5.2 Qualifications for asbestos removal workers

[p31 | prose | 5.2 Qualifications for asbestos removal workers]
Licensed asbestos removalists must make sure all their workers have the correct/ relevant certificate for the class of licensed asbestos removal work to be done.

[p31 | prose | 5.2 Qualifications for asbestos removal workers]
Table 11 shows required certificates for licensed asbestos removal workers.

[p31 | prose | CLASS OF LICENSED ASBESTOS REMOVAL WHAT CERTIFICATE IS REQUIRED?]
CLASS OF LICENSED ASBESTOS REMOVAL WHAT CERTIFICATE IS REQUIRED?

[p31 | prose | WHAT CERTIFICATE IS REQUIRED?]
WHAT CERTIFICATE IS REQUIRED?

[p31 | prose | WHAT CERTIFICATE IS REQUIRED?]
Class A

[p31 | prose | WHAT CERTIFICATE IS REQUIRED?]
Unit Standard 29766: For undertaking Class A work

[p31 | prose | WHAT CERTIFICATE IS REQUIRED?]
Class B

[p31 | prose | WHAT CERTIFICATE IS REQUIRED?]
Unit Standard 29765: For undertaking Class B work

[p31 | prose | WHAT CERTIFICATE IS REQUIRED?]
Workers who do less than a combined four weeks of Class B asbestos removal work a year (12-month period) do not have to meet the Class B requirements in Table 11 The four weeks counts across all jobs and removalists they may work for in that year.

[p31 | prose | WHAT CERTIFICATE IS REQUIRED?]
A worker with the Unit Standard for Class A removal cannot undertake Class B removal work unless they also hold the Class B unit standard.

[p31 | prose | WHAT CERTIFICATE IS REQUIRED?]
These qualification requirements apply to all asbestos removal workers, including fixed term contract workers and temporary labour hire workers.

[p31 | prose | WHAT CERTIFICATE IS REQUIRED?]
Required certificates for licensed asbestos removal workers

[p32 | prose | 5.3 Instructing asbestos removal workers]
5.3 Instructing asbestos removal workers

[p32 | prose | 5.3 Instructing asbestos removal workers]
The licensed asbestos removalist must give appropriate instructions to workers carrying out licensed asbestos removal work. The instructions must be specific for the:

[p32 | prose | 5.3 Instructing asbestos removal workers]
– location and type of workplace where the work is done

[p32 | prose | 5.3 Instructing asbestos removal workers]
– the work to be carried out.

[p32 | prose | 5.3 Instructing asbestos removal workers]
Get this information from the asbestos removal control plan (ARCP) for the site. For more information, see Section 7.0: Asbestos removal control plan (ARCP)

[p32 | prose | 5.3 Instructing asbestos removal workers]
Before the start of each asbestos removal job, instruct workers on:

[p32 | prose | 5.3 Instructing asbestos removal workers]
– the nature of the hazards and risks of asbestos

[p32 | prose | 5.3 Instructing asbestos removal workers]
– how asbestos can affect a person’s health

[p32 | prose | 5.3 Instructing asbestos removal workers]
– the risks from exposure to airborne asbestos

[p32 | prose | 5.3 Instructing asbestos removal workers]
– the control measures in place for that job

[p32 | prose | 5.3 Instructing asbestos removal workers]
– the methods and equipment to use for the job

[p32 | prose | 5.3 Instructing asbestos removal workers]
– choosing, using, and caring for PPE and respiratory protective equipment (RPE)

[p32 | prose | 5.3 Instructing asbestos removal workers]
– decontamination procedures

[p32 | prose | 5.3 Instructing asbestos removal workers]
– waste disposal procedures

[p32 | prose | 5.3 Instructing asbestos removal workers]
– emergency procedures

[p32 | prose | 5.3 Instructing asbestos removal workers]
– the importance of health monitoring and exposure monitoring

[p32 | prose | 5.3 Instructing asbestos removal workers]
– any other requirements from other laws, if applicable.

[p32 | prose | 5.4 Training records for asbestos removal workers]
5.4 Training records for asbestos removal workers

[p32 | prose | 5.4 Training records for asbestos removal workers]
A licensed asbestos removalist must keep a training record for each worker who does licensed asbestos removal work for them. These records must be:

[p32 | prose | 5.4 Training records for asbestos removal workers]
– kept while each worker is carrying out licensed asbestos removal work

[p32 | prose | 5.4 Training records for asbestos removal workers]
– retained for five years after the worker has stopped carrying out licensed asbestos removal work for the removalist

[p32 | prose | 5.4 Training records for asbestos removal workers]
– readily accessible at the asbestos removal area and available for inspection by a health and safety inspector.

[p32 | prose | 5.4 Training records for asbestos removal workers]
Note: Records can be kept in hardcopy versions or electronically.

[p34 | prose | Class A and Class B licensed asbestos removal work must be supervised by a nominated supervisor.]
Class A and Class B licensed asbestos removal work must be supervised by a nominated supervisor.

[p34 | prose | 6.1 Workers must have appropriate supervision]
6.1 Workers must have appropriate supervision

[p34 | prose | 6.1 Workers must have appropriate supervision]
Licensed asbestos removal work must be supervised by a supervisor nominated on the licence of the removalist undertaking the work. The licence must cover the type of asbestos work being done.

[p34 | prose | 6.1 Workers must have appropriate supervision]
For information on applying for a licence, see WorkSafe’s webpage Apply for an asbestos removal or assessor licence including:

[p34 | prose | 6.1 Workers must have appropriate supervision]
– nominate a supervisor for licensed asbestos removal work

[p34 | prose | 6.1 Workers must have appropriate supervision]
– qualifications to supervise licensed asbestos removal work

[p34 | prose | 6.1 Workers must have appropriate supervision]
– experience to supervise licensed asbestos removal work.

[p34 | prose | 6.2 Class A asbestos removal supervision]
6.2 Class A asbestos removal supervision

[p34 | prose | 6.2 Class A asbestos removal supervision]
For asbestos removal work that requires a Class A licence, the nominated supervisor must be present at the asbestos removal area whenever the work is carried out. This includes during:

[p34 | prose | 6.2 Class A asbestos removal supervision]
– asbestos removal area set up

[p34 | prose | 6.2 Class A asbestos removal supervision]
– enclosure erection

[p34 | prose | 6.2 Class A asbestos removal supervision]
– asbestos removal

[p34 | prose | 6.2 Class A asbestos removal supervision]
– clearance inspection

[p34 | prose | 6.2 Class A asbestos removal supervision]
– site demobilisation after clearance inspection.

[p34 | prose | 6.3 Class B asbestos removal supervision]
6.3 Class B asbestos removal supervision

[p34 | prose | 6.3 Class B asbestos removal supervision]
If the asbestos removal work requires a Class B licence, the nominated supervisor must be in the vicinity and readily available. The work must still be supervised effectively.

[p34 | prose | 6.3 Class B asbestos removal supervision]
It is important to consider these factors when deciding whether to be there:

[p34 | prose | 6.3 Class B asbestos removal supervision]
– the workers’ competence and experience

[p34 | prose | 6.3 Class B asbestos removal supervision]
– the work being carried out and the risks involved, what might go wrong, and the ability of the workers to immediately recognise and remedy risk

[p34 | prose | 6.3 Class B asbestos removal supervision]
– how the supervisor can be contacted and be readily available to return to the removal area.

[p34 | prose | 6.3 Class B asbestos removal supervision]
Table 12 below shows more examples of factors to consider when deciding whether to be on-site.

[p35 | table | 6.3 Class B asbestos removal supervision]
| FACTOR | WHAT TO CONSIDER |
| --- | --- |
| Worker competence and experience | Assess the skill level and experience of the workers performing the removal work. More experienced workers may require less direct supervision. |
| Nature of the work and risks involved | Evaluate the specific tasks and associated risks. Higher risk activities are more likely to require the supervisor to be present to make sure asbestos removal is carried out safely. |
| Potential issues and response | Consider potential problems that could arise during the removal process and the workers’ ability to recognise and address these issues promptly. |
| Communication and availability | Make sure the supervisor is easily contactable and can return to the site quickly if needed. |

[p35 | prose | 6.3 Class B asbestos removal supervision]
Examples of

[p35 | prose | 6.3 Class B asbestos removal supervision]
asbestos removal work

[p35 | prose | Example 1: Class B supervision]
Example 1: Class B supervision

[p35 | prose | Example 1: Class B supervision]
The supervisor is supervising workers that are competent, reliable and experienced in the type of removal being carried out. The supervisor decides that they need to check on the workers periodically during the day, but otherwise they will be working in a different part of the building. The workers are instructed to contact the supervisor by phone immediately if anything unexpected occurs, or if removal is not going according to plan. In these cases, the supervisor will return to the removal area.

[p35 | prose | Example 2: Class B supervision]
Example 2: Class B supervision

[p35 | prose | Example 2: Class B supervision]
The supervisor is supervising workers that are still training and are inexperienced. In this case, direct supervision is always needed.

[p35 | prose | What does ‘in the vicinity’ and ‘readily available’ mean?]
What does ‘in the vicinity’ and ‘readily available’ mean?

[p35 | prose | What does ‘in the vicinity’ and ‘readily available’ mean?]
‘In the vicinity’ means in the general area nearby, in person. The exact meaning depends on the situation and must be considered with the definition of ‘readily available’.

[p35 | prose | A supervisor is not ‘in the vicinity’ if they are:]
A supervisor is not ‘in the vicinity’ if they are:

[p35 | prose | A supervisor is not ‘in the vicinity’ if they are:]
– only available remotely

[p35 | prose | A supervisor is not ‘in the vicinity’ if they are:]
– not able to reach the workplace in a reasonable period.

[p35 | prose | A supervisor is not ‘in the vicinity’ if they are:]
‘Readily available’ means able to be reached quickly and easily. The exact meaning will depend on the situation and must be considered alongside the definition of ‘in the vicinity’.

[p35 | prose | A supervisor is not ‘in the vicinity’ if they are:]
A supervisor is not ‘readily available’ if they:

[p35 | prose | A supervisor is not ‘in the vicinity’ if they are:]
– can only be reached through a one-way channel (like a text message or email) – cannot easily access the site due to obstacles

[p35 | prose | A supervisor is not ‘in the vicinity’ if they are:]
– cannot be reliably reached due to issues with their method of contact/ travel. For example, commuting across a busy urban centre during rush- hour traffic.

[p35 | prose | A supervisor is not ‘in the vicinity’ if they are:]
When considering ‘in the vicinity’ and ‘readily available’, it is important to factor in scale and complexity of the removal. For example, asbestos cement cladding on a garage which is relatively simple to remove versus multiple non-friable ACMs across a school refurbishment project, with vulnerable people present.

[p35 | prose | A supervisor is not ‘in the vicinity’ if they are:]
When workers are recently trained, the licence holder may wish to ensure the supervisor is always on site to oversee proportionately more of the workers’ activities.

[p36 | prose | Example 1]
Example 1

[p36 | prose | Example 1]
If an incident occurred on a Class B licensed asbestos removal site (such as a cut needing minor first aid or the breakdown of a piece of equipment with competent removalist workers), the nominated supervisor is contactable by phone and able to arrive in person at the workplace within 30 minutes of being notified, WorkSafe would likely regard them as ‘in the vicinity’ and ‘readily available’.

[p36 | prose | Example 2]
Example 2

[p36 | prose | Example 2]
If the same incident occurred with recently trained removalist workers, and the nominated supervisor is away from site, contactable by phone and able to return to the site within 15 minutes of being notified that support is needed, WorkSafe would likely regard them as as ‘in the vicinity’ and ‘readily available’.

[p36 | prose | Example 3]
Example 3

[p36 | prose | Example 3]
If there are two licensed class B jobs happening at the same time but they are 30 minutes apart in light traffic, and both are supervised by the same person, managing a minor issue at one site might be tricky due to the distance. If a serious problem happens at the other site during busy traffic, it will take the supervisor longer to get there. In such a case, WorkSafe would likely regard the supervisor not to be ‘in the vicinity and readily available’ to manage the problem.

[p38 | prose | Use an ARCP to manage risks and protect workers and other people when removing asbestos that requires a licence.]
Use an ARCP to manage risks and protect workers and other people when removing asbestos that requires a licence.

[p38 | prose | 7.1 What is an ARCP?]
7.1 What is an ARCP?

[p38 | prose | 7.1 What is an ARCP?]
An ARCP is a document that identifies the specific control measures that will be used to make sure that workers and other people are not put at risk during licensed asbestos removal.

[p38 | prose | 7.1 What is an ARCP?]
An ARCP can help to plan an asbestos removal job effectively and make sure that asbestos material is removed safely.

[p38 | prose | 7.1 What is an ARCP?]
Include the following details in asbestos removal control plans:

[p38 | prose | 7.1 What is an ARCP?]
– how the removal will be done, including the method, tools, equipment, and personal protective equipment to use

[p38 | prose | 7.1 What is an ARCP?]
– information from an asbestos demolition/refurbishment survey – the asbestos to be removed, its location, type, and condition

[p38 | prose | 7.1 What is an ARCP?]
– a description of the asbestos removal area and any air monitoring points

[p38 | prose | 7.1 What is an ARCP?]
– how the asbestos waste will be transported and disposed of.

[p38 | prose | 7.1 What is an ARCP?]
While ARCPs are required only for licensed asbestos removal work, they can also be useful for planning unlicensed asbestos removal work, especially when being done near sensitive areas such as schools or hospitals.

[p38 | prose | 7.1 What is an ARCP?]
Note: While the structure of the ARCP can be generic, each plan must address the specific requirements of each job.

[p38 | prose | 7.1 What is an ARCP?]
A template ARCP is provided in Appendix 1.

[p38 | prose | 7.2 Who is responsible for preparing an ARCP?]
7.2 Who is responsible for preparing an ARCP?

[p38 | prose | 7.2 Who is responsible for preparing an ARCP?]
A licensed asbestos removalist must prepare an asbestos removal control plan (ARCP) for any licensed asbestos removal work they are commissioned to do.

[p38 | prose | 7.2 Who is responsible for preparing an ARCP?]
It is important the removalist prepares the ARCP before the removal work starts. When preparing it, the removalist is responsible for consulting with:

[p38 | prose | 7.2 Who is responsible for preparing an ARCP?]
– the PCBU that commissioned the work

[p38 | prose | 7.2 Who is responsible for preparing an ARCP?]
– any PCBUs that manage or control workplaces that may be affected by the asbestos removal work

[p38 | prose | 7.2 Who is responsible for preparing an ARCP?]
– workers and their representatives

[p38 | prose | 7.2 Who is responsible for preparing an ARCP?]
– the assessor that will be doing air monitoring and site clearance.

[p39 | prose | 7.2 Who is responsible for preparing an ARCP?]
If the licensed asbestos removal work is being carried out at a home, the removalist should also consult with the person who commissioned the removal work and the owner or occupier of the home (if different).

[p39 | prose | 7.2 Who is responsible for preparing an ARCP?]
If, after work has started, the scope of work changes or other factors mean the way the work will be done has to change, the ARCP should be updated in consultation with the commissioning PCBU. Any changes should be communicated to the same people listed above.

[p39 | prose | 7.3 Working with assessors during planning]
7.3 Working with assessors during planning

[p39 | prose | 7.3 Working with assessors during planning]
While removalists and assessors must remain independent of each other and work for the commissioning PCBU, they still need to work together to manage overlapping duties and to support each other to comply with their respective duties. This is particularly important during planning and creating the ARCP. Examples of where the removalist should engage with the assessor include:

[p39 | table | 7.3 Working with assessors during planning]
| WORK ACTIVITY | EXAMPLES WHERE REMOVALIST AND ASSESSOR SHOULD WORK TOGETHER |
| --- | --- |
| Pre-removal site visit | With the assessor do a walk through of the removal site during scoping and before planning. This can help assessors identify and deal with potential issues, avoiding unnecessary delays in completing the clearance. A walk through will also give assessors a better idea of how long they may need for the visual inspection and air monitoring during four-stage clearance. See Section 13: Clearance inspections for more information. |
| Asbestos removal control plan (ARCP) development | Consult with assessors in the following areas of the ARCP: - roles and responsibilities - timelines for task completion - emergency procedures decisions on positioning of monitoring equipment and positioning or viewing panels in enclosures when deciding when or how to do personal air monitoring use and availability of decontamination facilities expectations for dealing with remedial cleaning or failed clearances how the clearance inspection records, and clearance certificates will be provided/delivered. |
| During removal work | Agree with the assessor how they will make results of air monitoring available to the removalist and their workers - especially if the respirable asbestos fibre levels are found to be too high. This must be communicated to the commissioning PCBU and the removalist immediately so additional control measures can be put in place, or work stopped. See Section 10.6: Action to take if asbestos fibres are exceeded for more information. The assessor and the removalist must also cooperate during any incident response. |

[p39 | prose | 7.3 Working with assessors during planning]
TABLE 13: Examples of Removalists and Assessors working together

[p39 | prose | 7.3 Working with assessors during planning]
For more information about maintaining independence between removalists and assessors see Section 2.4: Independence and conflicts of interest in the Asbestos assessments – good practice guidelines

[p39 | prose | 7.4 Who needs access to the ARCP?]
7.4 Who needs access to the ARCP?

[p39 | prose | 7.4 Who needs access to the ARCP?]
Once the ARCP is prepared, the removalist must provide a copy of it to the person who commissioned the licensed asbestos removal work.

[p39 | prose | 7.4 Who needs access to the ARCP?]
A copy of the ARCP must be kept for two years after the work is completed. If there is an incident that needs to be notified to WorkSafe, the ARCP must be kept for five years.

[p40 | prose | 7.4 Who needs access to the ARCP?]
The ARCP must be readily accessible on site for the duration of the removal work and available for the following to read:

[p40 | prose | 7.4 Who needs access to the ARCP?]
– any PCBU at the workplace

[p40 | prose | 7.4 Who needs access to the ARCP?]
– workers and their representatives

[p40 | prose | 7.4 Who needs access to the ARCP?]
– the occupants of the home (if relevant)

[p40 | prose | 7.4 Who needs access to the ARCP?]
– WorkSafe as the regulator (under HSWA).

[p40 | prose | 7.5 Notifying WorkSafe about licensed asbestos removal work]
7.5 Notifying WorkSafe about licensed asbestos removal work

[p40 | prose | 7.5 Notifying WorkSafe about licensed asbestos removal work]
The licensed asbestos removalist must notify WorkSafe in writing at least five days before the licensed asbestos removal work starts.

[p40 | prose | 7.5 Notifying WorkSafe about licensed asbestos removal work]
This information must be included in the notification.

[p40 | table | 7.5 Notifying WorkSafe about licensed asbestos removal work]
| ITEM | DESCRIPTION |
| --- | --- |
|  | The name, licence number, and business contact details of the licensed asbestos removalist |
|  | The name and business contact details of the supervisor of the licensed asbestos removal work |
|  | The name of the asbestos assessor engaged to carry out a clearance inspection and issue a clearance certificate for the work |
|  | The name and contact details of the person for whom the work is to be carried out |
|  | The name (including registered business or company name) of the PCBU with management or control of the workplace where the asbestos is to be removed, including its address and the kind of workplace |
|  | If the workplace is large, the specific location in the workplace of the asbestos removal |
|  | The date of the notice |
|  | The date on which the asbestos removal work is to start and the estimated duration of the work |
|  | Whether the asbestos to be removed is friable or non-friable |
| 10 | If the asbestos to be removed is friable, how the area of removal will be enclosed |
| 11 | The estimated quantity of asbestos to be removed and the means of transport and disposal of the asbestos waste |
| 12 | The number of workers who are to carry out the asbestos removal work |
| 13 | A summary of the training for each worker carrying out the asbestos removal work |

[p40 | prose | 7.5 Notifying WorkSafe about licensed asbestos removal work]
The Notification of Licensed Asbestos Removal form is available from WorkSafe’s website.

[p40 | prose | 7.5 Notifying WorkSafe about licensed asbestos removal work]
TABLE 14:

[p40 | prose | 7.5 Notifying WorkSafe about licensed asbestos removal work]
WorkSafe notification

[p40 | prose | 7.5 Notifying WorkSafe about licensed asbestos removal work]
details for licensed

[p40 | prose | 7.5 Notifying WorkSafe about licensed asbestos removal work]
asbestos removal work

[p41 | prose | 7.6 Limited circumstances where removal work may start immediately]
7.6 Limited circumstances where removal work may start immediately

[p41 | prose | 7.6 Limited circumstances where removal work may start immediately]
Removal work may start immediately in either of these limited circumstances:

[p41 | prose | 7.6 Limited circumstances where removal work may start immediately]
– a sudden, unexpected event (such as equipment failure, fire, or flood) that may expose people to respirable asbestos fibres, or creates a risk of exposure (such as a burst pipe lagged with asbestos)

[p41 | prose | 7.6 Limited circumstances where removal work may start immediately]
– an unexpected breakdown of an essential service (such as gas, water, sewage, or telecommunications services) that needs urgent repair to keep the service running.

[p41 | prose | 7.6 Limited circumstances where removal work may start immediately]
If this happens, the licensed asbestos removalist:

[p41 | prose | 7.6 Limited circumstances where removal work may start immediately]
1. Must immediately notify WorkSafe by telephone and in writing within 24 hours after the verbal notice was provided.

[p43 | prose | 7.6 Limited circumstances where removal work may start immediately]
Warning signs and barriers are essential to clearly identify that asbestos removal is underway and protect people from exposure.

[p43 | prose | 8.1 Removal areas must be clearly indicated]
8.1 Removal areas must be clearly indicated

[p43 | prose | 8.1 Removal areas must be clearly indicated]
Asbestos removal area covers all the areas in which asbestos removal work is carried out and includes:

[p43 | prose | 8.1 Removal areas must be clearly indicated]
– decontamination facilities

[p43 | prose | 8.1 Removal areas must be clearly indicated]
– enclosures or areas from where asbestos is being removed

[p43 | prose | 8.1 Removal areas must be clearly indicated]
– transit routes

[p43 | prose | 8.1 Removal areas must be clearly indicated]
– any other area specified in ARCP.

[p43 | prose | 8.1 Removal areas must be clearly indicated]
The asbestos removal area should be clearly indicated. Use clear signage and appropriate barriers to prevent unauthorised access. For removal being carried out without an enclosure this should be done using a solid barrier or fence surrounding the immediate removal area.

[p43 | prose | 8.1 Removal areas must be clearly indicated]
A secondary buffer zone should be created in the general area to further restrict unauthorised people from getting near the enclosure or asbestos removal area especially in sensitive locations such as schools or hospitals and in occupied buildings.

[p43 | prose | 8.1 Removal areas must be clearly indicated]
Responsibilities for the security and access control to the asbestos removal site should be specified in the ARCP.

[p43 | prose | 8.1 Removal areas must be clearly indicated]
The asbestos removalist should consider not only the risk associated with asbestos removal, but also the general risks related to the work and work environment.

[p43 | prose | 8.2 Using warning signs]
8.2 Using warning signs

[p43 | prose | 8.2 Using warning signs]
Warning signs must be used to indicate that asbestos removal work is taking place. They effectively alert workers, visitors, and the public to the presence of asbestos and the associated risks.

[p43 | prose | 8.2 Using warning signs]
When placing warning signs to indicate the asbestos removal area, think about:

[p43 | prose | 8.2 Using warning signs]
– Placement: Place warning signs at all main entry points to the asbestos removal area.

[p43 | prose | 8.2 Using warning signs]
– Visibility: Make sure that signs are weatherproof and securely positioned in prominent locations, so they are easy to see.

[p43 | prose | 8.2 Using warning signs]
– Compliance with standards: Signs should comply with NZS/AS 1319 Safety signs for the occupational environment, covering aspects such as size, illumination, location, and maintenance.

[p43 | prose | 8.2 Using warning signs]
– Regulations: Any signs used must comply with any applicable safe work instrument.

[p44 | prose | 8.3 Creating a buffer zone around the asbestos removal area]
8.3 Creating a buffer zone around the asbestos removal area

[p44 | prose | 8.3 Creating a buffer zone around the asbestos removal area]
Barriers should be used to physically isolate the general area surrounding the asbestos removal area and, if necessary, an enclosure (a buffer zone). They can help control access to the asbestos removal area and prevent unauthorised access.

[p44 | prose | 8.3 Creating a buffer zone around the asbestos removal area]
Types of barriers:

[p44 | prose | 8.3 Creating a buffer zone around the asbestos removal area]
– Fixed barriers: Solid barriers such as fencing should be used to keep unauthorized people away from friable asbestos removal work areas and enclosures.

[p44 | prose | 8.3 Creating a buffer zone around the asbestos removal area]
– Tape barriers: For work involving the removal of non-friable asbestos over a brief period, a tape barrier may be sufficient to keep people at a safe distance.

[p44 | prose | 8.3 Creating a buffer zone around the asbestos removal area]
The placement of barriers should be based on an assessment of the physical environment and the level of risk at the asbestos removal site.

[p44 | prose | 8.3 Creating a buffer zone around the asbestos removal area]
Factors to consider when placing barriers:

[p44 | prose | 8.3 Creating a buffer zone around the asbestos removal area]
– Friability of asbestos material: Friable asbestos will need to be fenced off using a solid barrier.

[p44 | prose | 8.3 Creating a buffer zone around the asbestos removal area]
– Activity levels: Consider the activity around the asbestos removal area, including workers, visitors, and the public, to assess the risk of exposure to asbestos.

[p44 | prose | 8.3 Creating a buffer zone around the asbestos removal area]
– Likelihood of interference from the public: consider the proximity of the work to busy public areas, schools, and other locations where the work may draw the attention of the public. In these scenarios solid barriers rather than tape should be used to prevent access.

[p44 | prose | 8.3 Creating a buffer zone around the asbestos removal area]
– Existing barriers: Use existing barriers such as walls and doors where possible.

[p44 | prose | 8.3 Creating a buffer zone around the asbestos removal area]
FIGURE 1:

[p46 | prose | 8.3 Creating a buffer zone around the asbestos removal area]
Enclosures help contain asbestos during removal to prevent the spread of fibres. It is essential to understand their set up and management.

[p46 | prose | 9.1 The purpose of an enclosure]
9.1 The purpose of an enclosure

[p46 | prose | 9.1 The purpose of an enclosure]
Enclosures are an important control measure during asbestos removal work. They are normally required to:

[p46 | prose | 9.1 The purpose of an enclosure]
– prevent the spread of asbestos

[p46 | prose | 9.1 The purpose of an enclosure]
– prevent other people (employees and others) from being exposed to asbestos.

[p46 | prose | 9.1 The purpose of an enclosure]
This section explains the function and purpose of enclosures and gives practical advice on their design, construction, and use during work with asbestos.

[p46 | prose | 9.2 Definition and use]
9.2 Definition and use

[p46 | prose | 9.2 Definition and use]
An enclosure is a physical barrier around the asbestos work area designed to contain asbestos dust and waste resulting from the work, preventing the spread of asbestos materials to the surrounding environment.

[p46 | prose | 9.2 Definition and use]
Access to the enclosure is controlled through entrance and exit openings called airlocks (Figure 2). Airlocks control the movement of workers, waste, and equipment in and out of the work area and enable the preliminary decontamination of workers, equipment, and waste items on exiting the unit.

[p46 | prose | 9.2 Definition and use]
The enclosure and airlocks must, so far as is reasonably practicable, be:

[p46 | prose | 9.2 Definition and use]
– airtight

[p46 | prose | 9.2 Definition and use]
– under negative pressure, unless glove bags are used for Class A asbestos removal Section 11.5: Dry stripping with control at source

[p46 | prose | 9.2 Definition and use]
– tested for leaks if used for removing friable asbestos.

[p47 | prose | 9.2 Definition and use]
FIGURE 2:

[p47 | prose | 9.2 Definition and use]
Enclosure with three-stage airlock

[p47 | prose | 9.2 Definition and use]
Enclosures, by design and function, will lead to increased amounts of dust and debris inside the enclosure area. Anyone entering or working inside an enclosure must wear suitable protective clothing and RPE and must thoroughly decontaminate themselves on leaving the enclosure.

[p47 | prose | 9.2 Definition and use]
For more information, see Section 15.0: Personal decontamination and Section 17.0: Personal protective equipment (PPE)

[p47 | prose | 9.2 Definition and use]
Enclosures must be constructed before any Class A asbestos removal work starts, including placing injection needles.

[p47 | prose | 9.3 When is an enclosure required?]
9.3 When is an enclosure required?

[p47 | prose | 9.3 When is an enclosure required?]
Class A removal work will almost always require an enclosure including:

[p47 | prose | 9.3 When is an enclosure required?]
– when removing asbestos thermal insulation (such as boiler and pipe insulation, and sprayed asbestos applied for fire protection, anti-condensation, and acoustic control)

[p47 | prose | 9.3 When is an enclosure required?]
– when removing AIB products such as ceiling tiles or panels or boards containing asbestos.

[p47 | prose | 9.3 When is an enclosure required?]
Using an enclosure for Class A removal work should be the default procedure to use and any exception will need to be clearly justified. See When enclosure may not be required below.

[p47 | prose | 9.3 When is an enclosure required?]
There are situations where enclosures should also be considered for Class B or unlicensed work, including:

[p47 | prose | 9.3 When is an enclosure required?]
– where asbestos cement sheets are being removed, and there is a lot of breakage and fine debris being created

[p47 | prose | 9.3 When is an enclosure required?]
– the work is happening close to occupied or sensitive areas.

[p47 | prose | 9.3 When is an enclosure required?]
Enclosures may also be necessary as a secondary containment option in situations where a primary control such as glove bags are in use but there is significant risk to other people near the work area if the control should fail. In these situations, the enclosure provides protection against potential contamination of the area if the primary control fails.

[p48 | prose | When enclosures may not be required]
When enclosures may not be required

[p48 | prose | When enclosures may not be required]
Class A enclosures may not be reasonably practicable in all instances, for example when:

[p48 | prose | When enclosures may not be required]
– the location is extremely remote, and the asbestos or ACM is present in open space

[p48 | prose | When enclosures may not be required]
– the work is at height and an enclosure is not practical due to the height or complexity of the structure (such as pipework in the open air at a high level), or its exposure to severe weather conditions

[p48 | prose | When enclosures may not be required]
– there are practical difficulties in obtaining an effective seal on the structures, such as soffits around building roofs

[p48 | prose | When enclosures may not be required]
– the work involves cleaning up minor contamination or external contamination especially in emergency scenarios

[p48 | prose | When enclosures may not be required]
If an enclosure is not used for Class A asbestos removal, additional control measures need to be put in place to prevent any contamination of the surrounding area or exposure to other people. Examples of other control measures include:

[p48 | prose | When enclosures may not be required]
– enhanced dust suppression methods e.g. misting cannons or low-pressure water spraying

[p48 | prose | When enclosures may not be required]
– enhanced work area zoning including respirator zone and buffer zone with strict access control

[p48 | prose | When enclosures may not be required]
– strict direct supervision of the asbestos removal workers

[p48 | prose | When enclosures may not be required]
The effectiveness of additional control measures must be checked by ongoing air monitoring in close proximity to active removal work

[p48 | prose | When enclosures may not be required]
Although the licenced assessor might decide that clearance air monitoring and surface testing may not be possible or necessary, a Class A clearance inspection must still be undertaken with clear justification for Stage 3 omission recorded by the licenced assessor.

[p48 | prose | When enclosures may not be required]
FIGURE 3:

[p48 | prose | When enclosures may not be required]
Removal work without an enclosure

[p49 | prose | 9.4 Planning for enclosures]
9.4 Planning for enclosures

[p49 | prose | 9.4 Planning for enclosures]
Enclosures are a key feature in the risk management of asbestos removal. Plans for how enclosures will be constructed and used should be considered as early as possible in the planning process.

[p49 | prose | 9.4 Planning for enclosures]
Enclosures can be used in many different situations and conditions. While there are common features to most enclosures, they will need to be designed specifically for the circumstances of the individual job. Factors to consider when planning an enclosure include:

[p49 | prose | 9.4 Planning for enclosures]
– clearance of the work area

[p49 | prose | 9.4 Planning for enclosures]
– removal of mobile or portable items

[p49 | prose | 9.4 Planning for enclosures]
– the need for a pre-clean

[p49 | prose | 9.4 Planning for enclosures]
– the size and shape of the work area

[p49 | prose | 9.4 Planning for enclosures]
– the enclosure being as airtight as possible

[p49 | prose | 9.4 Planning for enclosures]
– plant being switched off (such as boilers) after communication with the client

[p49 | prose | 9.4 Planning for enclosures]
– creating sufficient and uniform negative pressure (without short circuiting) within the enclosure and airlocks

[p49 | prose | 9.4 Planning for enclosures]
– ensuring sufficient air movement through the enclosure

[p49 | prose | 9.4 Planning for enclosures]
– allowing for safe and easy access for workers, equipment and waste

[p49 | prose | 9.4 Planning for enclosures]
– making sure the enclosure will be robust for the conditions

[p49 | prose | 9.4 Planning for enclosures]
– security, and prevention of damage to the enclosure

[p49 | prose | 9.4 Planning for enclosures]
– provision of viewing panels

[p49 | prose | 9.4 Planning for enclosures]
– actions to take in an emergency

[p49 | prose | 9.4 Planning for enclosures]
– the need for fire prevention materials (such as fireproof polythene).

[p49 | prose | 9.4 Planning for enclosures]
The following may also need to be considered:

[p49 | prose | 9.4 Planning for enclosures]
– additional controls if the work is happening in an occupied building

[p49 | prose | 9.4 Planning for enclosures]
– securing external enclosures.

[p49 | prose | 9.5 Enclosure design and main features]
9.5 Enclosure design and main features

[p49 | prose | 9.5 Enclosure design and main features]
Figure 4 shows the main components of a typical asbestos removal enclosure.

[p49 | prose | 9.5 Enclosure design and main features]
FIGURE 4: Schematic diagram of the main components of an enclosure

[p50 | prose | Enclosure size]
Enclosure size

[p50 | prose | Enclosure size]
When designing the enclosure consider the following:

[p50 | prose | Enclosure size]
– the location of the asbestos to be removed and access requirements

[p50 | prose | Enclosure size]
– make sure it is big enough for the work activity, for example:

[p50 | prose | Enclosure size]
the number of workers involved

[p50 | prose | Enclosure size]
the size and shape of the items or materials to be removed

[p50 | prose | Enclosure size]
the space needed for any plant or equipment to be used. Examples include lengths of pipework and plant such as scissor lifts or platforms.

[p50 | prose | Enclosure size]
- avoid oversized enclosures, as these will increase the spread of asbestos and the size of the area to be cleaned and increase ventilation requirements.

[p50 | prose | Using existing structures as part of an enclosure]
Using existing structures as part of an enclosure

[p50 | prose | Using existing structures as part of an enclosure]
Enclosures can make use of parts of the existing building structure or be self- supporting temporary structures purpose-built around the asbestos working area. Existing rooms or walls, ceilings and floors can be used to provide part or all of an enclosure.

[p50 | prose | Using existing structures as part of an enclosure]
The surfaces of the enclosure should be smooth and solid. If any existing surface is unsuitable, such as rough, damaged, or brittle surfaces, it should be pre- cleaned and then lined with material such as polythene sheeting.

[p50 | prose | Using existing structures as part of an enclosure]
The integrity of the enclosure must, so far as is reasonably practicable, be maintained and tested for leaks as work progresses. For example, when AIB ceiling tiles are removed opening the ceiling void, the void will become part of the enclosure and will need to be smoke tested, and the area volume included in the ventilation calculation. If these areas are sealed back again as work progresses, they will need to be cleaned and subject to clearance procedures.

[p51 | prose | Sealing the enclosure]
Sealing the enclosure

[p51 | prose | Sealing the enclosure]
The enclosure should be effectively sealed and be as airtight as possible:

[p51 | prose | Sealing the enclosure]
– seal all joints and corners (particularly in purpose-built structures)

[p51 | prose | Sealing the enclosure]
– seal all windows, doors, ventilation grilles, airbricks, inlets, and exhausts

[p51 | prose | Sealing the enclosure]
– seal any gaps or holes around pipes, ducts, conduits, structural or other items (including inside cupboards or units) that pass through to adjoining rooms or floors

[p51 | prose | Sealing the enclosure]
All leaks should be identified and sealed.

[p51 | prose | Sealing the enclosure]
Openings or gaps, or holes should be sealed using a combination of materials such as tape, sealing compounds such expanding foam, and waterproof sheeting. If complete sealing is not possible, additional controls should be used. These can include mini-enclosures within large enclosures, glove bags, and enhanced negative pressure.

[p51 | prose | Using sealants and foams]
Using sealants and foams

[p51 | prose | Using sealants and foams]
Certain foams and adhesives can produce high concentrations of harmful vapours when used in very confined spaces, or on hot pipework. Follow the product’s safe use instructions. The risks of using foam and sealants should be considered in the overall risk assessment and appropriate controls and protective equipment should be used.

[p51 | prose | Using sealants and foams]
Foam should be trimmed to size after setting.

[p51 | prose | Using sealants and foams]
If any foam or sealant is to remain after the removal work is done, check that it will not interfere with any fire safety regulations.

[p51 | prose | 9.6 Enclosure extraction (negative pressure)]
9.6 Enclosure extraction (negative pressure)

[p51 | prose | 9.6 Enclosure extraction (negative pressure)]
Creating a negative pressure environment inside the enclosure provides an additional control in case there are accidental leaks. However, air extraction should not be treated as an alternative to a well-sealed enclosure. Negative pressure also helps to control air movement when workers are entering or exiting the enclosure.

[p51 | prose | 9.6 Enclosure extraction (negative pressure)]
There should be sufficient negative pressure (mechanical extract ventilation) within the enclosure and the pressure should be as uniform as possible throughout the unit.

[p51 | prose | 9.6 Enclosure extraction (negative pressure)]
