The pressure differential will depend on:

[p51 | prose | 9.6 Enclosure extraction (negative pressure)]
– the extracted air rate from the enclosure

[p51 | prose | 9.6 Enclosure extraction (negative pressure)]
– the physical size and shape of the structure

[p51 | prose | 9.6 Enclosure extraction (negative pressure)]
– the extent of leakage (the effectiveness of the sealing)

[p51 | prose | 9.6 Enclosure extraction (negative pressure)]
– external weather conditions, such as a change in wind direction which can positively pressurise an enclosure.

[p51 | prose | 9.6 Enclosure extraction (negative pressure)]
For more information, see Section 9.13: Air extraction equipment

[p52 | prose | Large work areas]
Large work areas

[p52 | prose | Large work areas]
It may be difficult to achieve good, uniform negative pressure for extremely large enclosures. Consider sub-dividing a large space into a number of smaller enclosures. For example:

[p52 | prose | Large work areas]
– where the work area consists of a series of rooms (such as in office blocks or hotels)

[p52 | prose | Large work areas]
– where large extensive plant or long stretches of pipework are to be stripped of asbestos.

[p52 | prose | Air movement]
Air movement

[p52 | prose | Air movement]
Air movement in the enclosure should be designed to make sure that there is uniform airflow as far as reasonably practicable in all areas. NPUs (negative pressure units) and supplementary air inlets should be located where they will achieve good flow and avoid dead spots. For more information, see Section 9.13: Air extraction equipment

[p52 | prose | Air movement]
FIGURE 6: An NPU

[p52 | prose | Air movement]
Air movement should be checked during the smoke test (checking that smoke is cleared efficiently from all areas of the enclosure) and by using smoke tubes and/ or differential pressure monitors.

[p52 | prose | Air movement]
In situations where negative pressure is difficult to achieve, over-extraction may be used to compensate but the extraction level should not damage the integrity of the enclosure.

[p52 | prose | Air movement]
If the removal process involves opening other parts or sections of the premises, such as when removing ceiling tiles or dividing walls, the ventilation rate should be calculated based on the final enclosure volume, which includes the initial area and the ceiling void.

[p53 | prose | 9.7 Enclosure access]
9.7 Enclosure access

[p53 | prose | 9.7 Enclosure access]
Design all enclosures with access points for workers, plant, equipment, and asbestos waste removal, which prevent dust escaping into the general environment.

[p53 | prose | 9.7 Enclosure access]
Ideally, asbestos workers should enter the enclosure directly from the decontamination unit (DCU) (Figure 7).

[p53 | prose | 9.7 Enclosure access]
Example of a DCU attached directly to an enclosure

[p53 | prose | 9.7 Enclosure access]
Where the DCU cannot be attached directly to the enclosure, a transiting system is required. In transiting systems, workers will enter the enclosure through a three-stage airlock (Figure 8). Any transiting area between an airlock or bag lock and a DCU should be clearly marked and must be included within the scope of the clearance assessment.

[p54 | prose | 9.7 Enclosure access]
9.0 Asbestos removal enclosures

[p54 | prose | Airlocks and baglocks]
Airlocks and baglocks

[p54 | prose | Airlocks and baglocks]
Enclosures should be designed and constructed with separate systems for asbestos waste removal. A system similar to the worker transit facility (airlock) can be used for the removal of waste asbestos from the enclosure (called a baglock). This illustration shows the design of the baglock system.

[p55 | prose | Airlocks and baglocks]
Where it is not possible to have separate airlock and baglock systems, a hybrid arrangement can be used. The design of the hybrid system will depend on whether the DCU is connected directly to the enclosure or whether a three-stage transiting airlock is involved. These illustrations show the various options for setting up airlocks and baglocks.

[p57 | prose | 9.8 Viewing panels]
9.8 Viewing panels

[p57 | prose | 9.8 Viewing panels]
Clear viewing panels should be included in the walls of the enclosure so the site supervisor can see what is going on inside without having to enter (Figure 14). This helps make sure the method detailed in the ARCP is being followed. The number and location of these panels will depend on the location, size, and complexity of the enclosure.

[p57 | prose | 9.8 Viewing panels]
Viewing panels should be used in the inner stage of airlock and baglock. Where viewing panels are impractical, alternative methods to observe and monitor progress within the enclosure should be used such as CCTV, or web-based cameras placed at appropriate locations. Examples of situations where cameras might be needed instead of viewing panels include:

[p57 | prose | 9.8 Viewing panels]
Cameras should be covered in protective sheeting to prevent them becoming contaminated.

[p57 | prose | 9.8 Viewing panels]
For details on constructing viewing panels, see Section 9.12: Asbestos enclosure construction

[p58 | prose | 9.9 Occupied buildings]
9.9 Occupied buildings

[p58 | prose | 9.9 Occupied buildings]
If other parts of the building will be occupied during asbestos removal, additional control measures will be needed to make sure the building’s occupants remain safe and the enclosure is not compromised.

[p58 | prose | 9.9 Occupied buildings]
For example:

[p58 | prose | 9.9 Occupied buildings]
– If possible, plan to do the asbestos removal work during periods when the area will be unoccupied (such as after office hours).

[p58 | prose | 9.9 Occupied buildings]
– Consider greater isolation of the asbestos removal area. For example:

[p58 | prose | 9.9 Occupied buildings]
- consider using a hoarding to form a barrier between the asbestos removal area and the adjoining occupied areas

[p58 | prose | 9.9 Occupied buildings]
- erect a plastic-lined barrier within this hoarding and reserve a buffer area between the hoarding and occupied areas

[p58 | prose | 9.9 Occupied buildings]
- erect platforms and fixed scaffolding during the early stages of the work

[p58 | prose | 9.9 Occupied buildings]
- erect these structures on the outside of the enclosure.

[p58 | prose | 9.9 Occupied buildings]
– Do daily visual inspections and airborne sampling outside the enclosure to confirm the effectiveness of the enclosure (Section 9.14: Testing, monitoring, and maintenance).

[p58 | prose | 9.9 Occupied buildings]
– Make sure enclosures do not obscure or block any fire exits. If this is unavoidable, alternative arrangements must be made and clearly communicated to the building occupants and asbestos removal workers.

[p58 | prose | 9.9 Occupied buildings]
– Check that existing mechanical air movement (such as heating, or air conditioning) within the building will not compromise the negative pressure inside the enclosure, and also that process emissions are not drawn into the enclosure.

[p58 | prose | 9.9 Occupied buildings]
– Check that the movement of any lifts will not cause changes in air pressure that could affect the negative air pressure inside the enclosure.

[p58 | prose | 9.9 Occupied buildings]
– Make sure existing ventilation systems cannot be contaminated through inappropriate siting of NPU discharges near to ventilation intakes.

[p58 | prose | Protect existing fittings and furniture from contamination]
Protect existing fittings and furniture from contamination

[p58 | prose | Protect existing fittings and furniture from contamination]
All moveable items should be removed from the asbestos removal area before asbestos removal work begins. Where removing items from the enclosure is not possible:

[p58 | prose | Protect existing fittings and furniture from contamination]
– items should be moved as far away as possible from the immediate asbestos removal area and covered with two layers of plastic sheeting, with a minimum overlap of 300mm between the layers. Both layers should be double taped

[p58 | prose | Protect existing fittings and furniture from contamination]
– cover all non-moveable items such as fixtures and fittings with plastic sheeting and seal the joints

[p58 | prose | Protect existing fittings and furniture from contamination]
– protect floors with at least one layer of woven plastic to prevent penetration during the asbestos removal work. The joints should be lapped by 300mm and sealed with double-sided tape and duct tape.

[p58 | prose | 9.10 Securing external enclosures and scaffolding]
9.10 Securing external enclosures and scaffolding

[p58 | prose | 9.10 Securing external enclosures and scaffolding]
Secure enclosures properly to prevent them toppling or falling over. This is particularly important for external units which can be subject to changing weather conditions.

[p58 | prose | 9.10 Securing external enclosures and scaffolding]
Attach enclosures securely to the building or scaffolding. Consider the security of the enclosure to make sure unauthorised people/members of the public cannot enter.

[p59 | prose | 9.10 Securing external enclosures and scaffolding]
Seek advice from a competent person (such as a Chartered Professional Engineer) to verify the design and construction of the enclosure where it may be subject to adverse environmental conditions such as earthquake, wind and/ or snow – unless there is sufficient information provided in the manufacturer’s specifications.

[p59 | prose | 9.10 Securing external enclosures and scaffolding]
Scaffolding must meet the relevant standards for construction. For more information about safe scaffolding see Scaffolding in New Zealand

[p59 | prose | 9.11 Site preparation]
9.11 Site preparation

[p59 | prose | 9.11 Site preparation]
Before any preparation and construction work begins:

[p59 | prose | 9.11 Site preparation]
– assess what PPE and RPE will be required for the initial preparation work and construction of the enclosure and make it available to workers

[p59 | prose | 9.11 Site preparation]
– make sure the DCU is set up and operational.

[p59 | prose | 9.11 Site preparation]
Table 15 includes steps that may help with site preparation and pre-cleaning.

[p59 | table | 9.11 Site preparation]
| PREPARATION STEP | DETAILS |
| --- | --- |
| Inspect the proposed work area | The inspection area should include: - the planned enclosure area - surrounding areas such as transit and waste routes - the area next to the enclosure location. Inspect these areas to identify: - areas that will need pre-cleaning - where sheeting may be needed - items that can be removed - any factors that may affect clearance certification. |
| Pre-clean if any asbestos is found | If minor asbestos debris or dust is present, do a pre-clean before constructing the enclosure. Loose material should be cleaned up using appropriate methods such as: - type H vacuuming ' surface wiping ' temporary encapsulation (PVA, tape, or cling film) ' spray wetting ' bagging. Non-asbestos dust and debris should be disposed of as normal waste. Otherwise, it will be treated as hazardous waste once work starts. In cases of significant contamination (such as after a fire), where the enclosure cannot encompass all the material, loose visible asbestos dust, debris, and other items should be removed before constructing the enclosure. For more information about asbestos containing dust and debris see Section 4.0 Removal of asbestos-containing dust (ACD) |
| Remove all movable or portable items | Remove all mobile or portable items from the work area. Remaining items such as plant or electrical equipment should be pre-cleaned, covered with polythene sheeting, and securely taped to prevent contamination. |
| Seal plant with flues | Flues in boilers or similar equipment inside the enclosure should be sealed to prevent asbestos spread. |
| Consider and plan for any live plant | Avoid including live operating plant within an enclosure if possible. If unavoidable, consider factors such as live plant ventilation, and emergency access to live plant. |
| Protect hard-to- decontaminate areas | Pre-clean as much as possible, then protect areas that will be difficult to decontaminate (such as earth floors, or behind radiators/pipes) or areas that are likely to generate dust during cleaning (such as concrete) |

[p59 | prose | 9.11 Site preparation]
TABLE 15: Steps to help with site preparation and pre-cleaning

[p60 | prose | 9.12 Asbestos enclosure construction]
9.12 Asbestos enclosure construction

[p60 | prose | 9.12 Asbestos enclosure construction]
The enclosure should be designed and constructed in a way to prevent disturbance of asbestos materials until construction is finished. This is especially important in areas with extensive contamination on floors or other surfaces.

[p60 | prose | 9.12 Asbestos enclosure construction]
Construction and composition of the enclosure will depend on things such as:

[p60 | prose | 9.12 Asbestos enclosure construction]
– use of the existing building structure

[p60 | prose | 9.12 Asbestos enclosure construction]
– job duration

[p60 | prose | 9.12 Asbestos enclosure construction]
– work location.

[p60 | prose | 9.12 Asbestos enclosure construction]
Existing structures may reduce potential leaks but could present ventilation and access issues. Most enclosures are temporary, self-supporting units made with a frame securely fixed to sheeting material.

[p60 | prose | 9.12 Asbestos enclosure construction]
Table 16 lists factors to consider when constructing an enclosure.

[p60 | table | 9.12 Asbestos enclosure construction]
| FACTORS INFLUENCING ENCLOSURE CONSTRUCTION | DETAILS |
| --- | --- |
| Sealing openings | All openings (vents, doors, windows, holes, etc.) must be sealed to prevent asbestos fibre escape. |
| Polythene sheeting material | Polythene sheeting is commonly used due to its flexibility, imperviousness, and ease of erection. As a minimum, 200-500 microns opaque sheeting should be used. |
| Fire hazard considerations | In fire-prone areas, fire-retardant polythene should be used. |
| Alternative materials for strength | In exposed locations, reinforced PVC sheets or solid barriers (such as panels or wooden boards lined with polythene) may be used for added strength. |
| Timber framework | Timber dimensions should provide adequate fixing and support. For internal work, 50mm x 50mm clean timber is typically sufficient. Secure sheeting to the timber using staples, tape, and spray tack. |
| External sheeting on timber framework | If sheeting is attached to the outside of the timber, protect the timber (for example, tape it up) or thoroughly decontaminate it after use to prevent asbestos fibre retention. |
| Internal sheeting on timber framework | If sheeting is attached inside the timber, it will require reinforcement: additional timber, staples taped over, continuous fabric tape at timber/polythene contact, and increased framework support. |
| Scaffold framework | Scaffold frameworks with internally fixed boards or proprietary metal/plastic frameworks can be used if rigidly connected or properly designed to be free-standing. |
| For scaffold frameworks, sheeting should be fixed inside the framework. For timber frameworks, sheeting can be attached either inside or outside, with considerations for contamination and strength. |  |

[p60 | prose | 9.12 Asbestos enclosure construction]
TABLE 16: Factors to consider when constructing an enclosure

[p61 | prose | Sealing joints]
Sealing joints

[p61 | prose | Sealing joints]
All joints in the sheeting material should be sealed. Where adhesive tape is used to seal joints on polythene sheeting, apply it to both sides of the joint. Vinyl tape is suitable where an enclosure is to be used for a relatively short period. For longer-term use, use fabric-based tape (such as 75mm width polycloth).

[p61 | prose | Viewing panels]
Viewing panels

[p61 | prose | Viewing panels]
The enclosure should contain enough viewing panels (and/or CCTV or webcam systems) to allow all parts of the enclosure to be visible from the outside. The clear plastic panels should be inserted into the sheeting during construction.

[p61 | prose | Viewing panels]
Panels should be taped on both sides. Panel size should be at least 600mm x 300mm unless it is impractical. Panels should be located at a convenient height for viewing (usually about 1.5m from the floor surface).

[p61 | prose | Viewing panels]
For more information, see Section 9.8: Viewing panels

[p61 | prose | Airlocks and baglocks]
Airlocks and baglocks

[p61 | prose | Airlocks and baglocks]
Airlocks and baglocks should be as big as possible to allow the necessary changing, cleaning and transfer activities for workers and waste items and bags. The airlocks and baglocks should be at least 1m x 1m x 2m (height). They will have to be bigger in situations where larger items of waste, such as sections of piping, ducting or board, are being removed.

[p61 | prose | Airlocks and baglocks]
Where space is restricted in one direction, such as along a corridor, the airlocks and baglocks should be extended in the other direction (for example, 0.8m x 2m x 2m). Separate airlocks and baglocks should be used where space allows.

[p61 | prose | Airlocks and baglocks]
Airlocks need to be constructed with access openings between each compartment which prevent asbestos dust passing from one compartment to the next. This can be done by:

[p61 | prose | Airlocks and baglocks]
– cutting vertical slits in the sheeting and fixing polythene sheets across the opening

[p61 | prose | Airlocks and baglocks]
– reinforcing the vertical opening at the top

[p61 | prose | Airlocks and baglocks]
– placing the vertical sheet on the enclosure side of the partition with an adequate overlap so that air can move inwards, but outward air movement is restricted

[p61 | prose | Airlocks and baglocks]
– weighing down the bottom of the inner flaps with timber, a metal bar, or a length of chain. This will allow flexibility in all directions to improve control of airflow

[p61 | prose | Airlocks and baglocks]
– fitting the outer compartment with a further vertical sheet (a security sheet on the outside) which is used to cover over the entrance at the end of the shift. It should be taped back onto the roof area when the airlock is being used.

[p62 | prose | Airlocks and baglocks]
Flap arrangements for airlocks

[p62 | prose | Floors]
Floors

[p62 | prose | Floors]
Cover floors with thick watertight material, such as polythene unless the existing floor has a waterproof surface which can be thoroughly cleaned after asbestos removal work is completed. Polythene can become slippery. If polythene is used, consider covering it with hardboard, or similar material to minimise this risk. Where hardboard or similar materials are used, they will need to be disposed of as asbestos waste afterwards.

[p62 | prose | 9.13 Air extraction equipment]
9.13 Air extraction equipment

[p62 | prose | 9.13 Air extraction equipment]
It is typically not possible to make an enclosure completely airtight, so there is always a risk of contaminated air escaping. To manage this risk, mechanical extract ventilation should be used to maintain the air pressure inside the enclosure slightly below atmospheric pressure (known as negative pressure).

[p62 | prose | 9.13 Air extraction equipment]
This makes sure that the airflow through any leaks in the enclosure will be inwards rather than outwards, keeping asbestos dust contained within the enclosure.

[p62 | prose | 9.13 Air extraction equipment]
This is normally done by allowing clean air to be drawn into the enclosure through the airlock with the help of an air extraction unit (called an NPU) pulling air out somewhere else in the enclosure.

[p62 | prose | 9.13 Air extraction equipment]
Extraction units need to be able to be turned on and off from outside the enclosure. Switches should be clearly identified. When workers are not on site, reasonable steps need to be taken to prevent the enclosure and extraction units from being tampered with.

[p62 | prose | When to use air extraction equipment]
When to use air extraction equipment

[p62 | prose | When to use air extraction equipment]
Air extraction systems should be turned on before work starts, and be left running continuously throughout the removal work, including when workers are not on site.

[p62 | prose | When to use air extraction equipment]
If it is not reasonably practicable to leave the system running continuously during normal workdays, it should be left on during breaks and for at least one hour at the end of each shift to clear the enclosure of airborne dust. During these times, access points to the enclosure should be closed and sealed. Automatic time- delay switches can be used for this purpose.

[p63 | prose | POSITIONING OF NPUS]
POSITIONING OF NPUS

[p63 | prose | POSITIONING OF NPUS]
NPUs should be located opposite the airlocks. Figure 16 shows the ideal position. However, the exact location will depend on the layout and shape of the enclosure and the accessibility, and the suitability of walls for setting up equipment.

[p63 | prose | POSITIONING OF NPUS]
NPUs should be positioned so the airflow passes through most of the enclosure. Avoid setting up NPUs near to the air intake area as this will limit the airflow to that immediate area. Ducting within the enclosure can be used if the NPU cannot be placed directly in the best location.

[p63 | prose | POSITIONING OF NPUS]
Figures 17 and 18 show examples of good and poor airflow management.

[p63 | prose | POSITIONING OF NPUS]
NPUs should normally be located outside the enclosure with only the pre-filter visible from the inside. If flexible ducting is required inside the enclosure, the pre- filter will also be inside the enclosure. Any damage to the ducting could allow the pre-filter to be bypassed. Flexible ducting should be protected and regularly inspected to make sure there is no damage.

[p63 | prose | POSITIONING OF NPUS]
Larger or more complex enclosure set ups may require additional air inlets than just the airlock. PCBUs should consider taking advice from an appropriately qualified ventilation expert when deciding where to position additional intakes and NPUs. Any additional air inlets should have filtration (pre-filter) in them and be sealed if the enclosure is left overnight with the NPUs turned off.

[p64 | prose | POSITIONING OF NPUS]
NPU

[p65 | prose | Required features of NPUs]
Required features of NPUs

[p65 | prose | Required features of NPUs]
Extract ventilation (NPUs) should be provided by a purpose-built unit incorporating a fan and suitable filters.

[p65 | prose | Required features of NPUs]
Table 17 shows the main characteristics of an NPU for asbestos removal.

[p65 | table | Required features of NPUs]
| CHARACTERISTIC | REQUIREMENTS |
| --- | --- |
| Filtration | The system must incorporate a HEPA filter with at least 99.995% efficiency. The HEPA filter must be installed on the negative pressure side of the fan and sealed to prevent leakage. To extend its lifespan, a coarse pre-filter and possibly a second high-efficiency filter must be included. The unit must be tested altogether to make sure at least 99.995% efficiency. |
| Fan performance | The fan must run for extended periods and sustain its rated airflow against resistance from filters, ducting, and wind pressure. Allow for airflow losses due to ducting: 0.05-0.07m?*/s (100-150 CFM) per 6m length. A centrifugal fan is recommended. Typical fan sizes range from 0.084-2.36m/s (200-5000 CFM). |
| Construction | The system must be built for continuous operation and withstand wear and tear, transportation, and rough handling. |
| Reverse flow damper | A damper or similar device must be installed to prevent reverse airflow due to fan failure or wind conditions. Reverse airflow could create positive pressure, leading to unfiltered air leakage. |

[p66 | table | Required features of NPUs]
| CHARACTERISTIC | REQUIREMENTS |
| --- | --- |
| Indication of flow | An airflow indicator (manometer or similar device) must be installed. A visual or audible warning should alert for low airflow (for example, due to filter blockage). Low airflow is defined as falling below the unit’s specified output, linked to the minimum eight air changes per hour (see Calculating ventilation rates, below) The manometer reading should be checked at the start of each shift. |
| Discharge ducting | Extracted air must be safely discharged outside, away from air intakes, windows, and occupied areas. Preferably discharge away from building sides or obstructions to avoid back pressure in high winds. The length and diameter of the ducting depend on the extractor type and exhaust outlet diameter. |

[p66 | prose | TABLE 17: Main characteristics of a good NPU]
TABLE 17: Main characteristics of a good NPU

[p66 | prose | TABLE 17: Main characteristics of a good NPU]
FIGURE 19: The main characteristics of a good extract ventilation system

[p67 | prose | Filter replacement]
Filter replacement

[p67 | prose | Filter replacement]
Pre-filters may need to be replaced during a job. The pre-filters should be accessible from inside the enclosure without disturbing the HEPA filter. When the pre-filter is being replaced the fan should be turned off and removal work should stop.

[p67 | prose | Filter replacement]
Replacement of the final high efficiency filter is more complex and will require special precautions. The whole NPU should be removed from the enclosure and replaced. Filter replacement would subsequently be carried out by a competent trained person wearing adequate PPE under controlled (ventilated) conditions elsewhere.

[p67 | prose | Filter replacement]
After replacement of the HEPA filter, the NPU should be DOP (dispersed oil particulate) tested to make sure that the filter is correctly installed and sealed.

[p67 | prose | Calculating ventilation rates]
Calculating ventilation rates

[p67 | prose | Calculating ventilation rates]
The rate of ventilation required to achieve the right level of negative pressure within the enclosure will depend on several factors:

[p67 | prose | Calculating ventilation rates]
– the extract flow rate

[p67 | prose | Calculating ventilation rates]
– the extent of leakage from the unit

[p67 | prose | Calculating ventilation rates]
– the volume of the enclosure and the volume of the airlock and baglock.

[p67 | prose | Calculating ventilation rates]
– allowances for reduced airflow due to the filters and ducting

[p67 | prose | Calculating ventilation rates]
– external influences (such as wind pressures).

[p67 | prose | Calculating ventilation rates]
The ventilation rate selected on the unit will only be a guide to the negative pressure within the enclosure because the actual pressure will depend on these factors.

[p67 | prose | Calculating ventilation rates]
The exact ventilation requirements will vary between set-ups and should be determined on a case-by-case basis. A minimum flow rate of 0.2m3/s for each 100m3 of enclosed volume should be used. This is equivalent to approximately eight air changes per hour in the enclosure.

[p67 | prose | Calculating ventilation rates]
Where there are difficulties in getting an effective seal, over-extraction can be used to compensate. Extraction rates should not be excessive as this can cause damage to the integrity of the enclosure (such as causing the walls to collapse inwards).

[p67 | prose | Calculating ventilation rates]
In very large enclosures, maintaining internal negative pressure may be difficult. In these cases, the work areas should be divided into multiple smaller enclosures. For more information, see Section 9.5: Enclosure design and main features

[p67 | prose | Example: Method using smoke to check for an adequate ventilation rate]
Example: Method using smoke to check for an adequate ventilation rate

[p67 | prose | Example: Method using smoke to check for an adequate ventilation rate]
Assuming eight air changes an hour is required, measure the time smoke from a smoke machine takes to clear the area after it is turned off.

[p67 | prose | Example: Method using smoke to check for an adequate ventilation rate]
Since 60mins/8 = 7.5 mins, the smoke should take about 7 mins 30 sec to clear.

[p67 | prose | Example: Method using smoke to check for an adequate ventilation rate]
Anything quicker than 7 mins 30 sec will be greater than the best practice threshold. If the smoke takes longer than 7mins 30 sec to clear, the ventilation rate is not high enough.

[p67 | prose | Example: Method using smoke to check for an adequate ventilation rate]
Note: If the removal process involves opening other parts or sections of the premises, such as when removing ceiling tiles or dividing walls, the ventilation rate should be calculated based on the total enclosure volume, which includes the initial area and the ceiling void.

[p68 | prose | 9.14 Testing, monitoring, and maintenance]
9.14 Testing, monitoring, and maintenance

[p68 | prose | 9.14 Testing, monitoring, and maintenance]
For friable asbestos removal, the enclosure should be regularly tested and systematically monitored by the licensed removalist to make sure it is functioning correctly. Checks should include:

[p68 | prose | 9.14 Testing, monitoring, and maintenance]
Before work starts:

[p68 | prose | 9.14 Testing, monitoring, and maintenance]
– a visual inspection

[p68 | prose | 9.14 Testing, monitoring, and maintenance]
– smoke test.

[p68 | prose | Before each shift:]
Before each shift:

[p68 | prose | Before each shift:]
– a visual inspection

[p68 | prose | Before each shift:]
– checking the NPU pressure gauge.

[p68 | prose | If the building is occupied:]
If the building is occupied:

[p68 | prose | If the building is occupied:]
– daily checks with smoke tubes and pressure testing inside the enclosure

[p68 | prose | If the building is occupied:]
– additional daily air sampling:

[p68 | prose | If the building is occupied:]
in the vicinity of the enclosure

[p68 | prose | If the building is occupied:]
- near weak spots (such as sealed joints)

[p68 | prose | If the building is occupied:]
- near airlocks and NPU discharge point(s).

[p68 | prose | If the building is occupied:]
In other situations (such as unoccupied buildings), the same tests should be done soon after work starts and then periodically.

[p68 | prose | Recording testing, monitoring and maintenance activity]
Recording testing, monitoring and maintenance activity

[p68 | prose | Recording testing, monitoring and maintenance activity]
Procedures for testing and monitoring should be clearly documented. It should include the required frequency of inspections.

[p68 | prose | Recording testing, monitoring and maintenance activity]
Nominate a trained and competent person to be responsible for:

[p68 | prose | Recording testing, monitoring and maintenance activity]
– making sure testing and monitoring is done at the agreed intervals

[p68 | prose | Recording testing, monitoring and maintenance activity]
– recording the results of inspections, monitoring and maintenance in a logbook.

[p68 | prose | Recording testing, monitoring and maintenance activity]
All tests, test results, monitoring, and maintenance activity should be recorded, and the records should be readily accessible, for example by:

[p68 | prose | Recording testing, monitoring and maintenance activity]
– recording details in a logbook that is always kept onsite and accessible

[p68 | prose | Recording testing, monitoring and maintenance activity]
– recording details electronically in a way that can be accessed without difficulty.

[p68 | prose | Recording testing, monitoring and maintenance activity]
Managers or supervisors should inspect records frequently to make sure procedures are being followed.

[p68 | prose | Visual inspection]
Visual inspection

[p68 | prose | Visual inspection]
Before removal work starts and before each shift, the enclosure should be visually inspected to make sure that it has been constructed correctly and that it is effectively sealed. For example, the following should be checked:

[p68 | prose | Visual inspection]
– seals

[p68 | prose | Visual inspection]
– airlocks

[p68 | prose | Visual inspection]
– joints

[p68 | prose | Visual inspection]
– the fitting of sheeting around irregular objects such as pipes, pipe trays, and conduits.

[p69 | prose | Using smoke to test for leaks]
Using smoke to test for leaks

[p69 | prose | Using smoke to test for leaks]
Before removal work starts, the enclosure should be tested by releasing smoke from a smoke generator inside the enclosure with the air extraction equipment switched off (Figure 18). Escaping smoke indicates leaks which need to be dealt with, as far as possible.

[p69 | prose | Using smoke to test for leaks]
FIGURE 20:

[p69 | prose | Using smoke to test for leaks]
An enclosure being smoke tested

[p69 | prose | Using smoke to test for leaks]
Once the enclosure is full of smoke, all external areas should be checked for the presence of smoke. This should include any less obvious locations where smoke could emerge such as:

[p69 | prose | Using smoke to test for leaks]
– any floors above and below

[p69 | prose | Using smoke to test for leaks]
– through cavity walls.

[p69 | prose | Using smoke to test for leaks]
Smoke tests should be witnessed by an independent licenced asbestos assessor to ensure impartiality of the testing.

[p69 | prose | Using smoke to test for leaks]
Leaks may not always be easy to spot. Smoke may take some time to emerge such as in complex buildings, or where enclosure walls have multiple folds and pleating. The visual check should be done over a sufficient period to allow any smoke to become detectable.

[p69 | prose | Using smoke to test for leaks]
Even minor leaks can result in significant contamination of adjacent areas.

[p69 | prose | Using smoke to test for leaks]
Minor leaks can be detected by shining a torch beam along the area being checked. Minor releases of smoke will be shown up across the beam of light.

[p69 | prose | Using smoke to test for leaks]
Consider doing smoke tests daily if the risk assessment shows it is needed. For example, when there will be occupants in other parts of the building, or where the work is being done near areas such as schools or childcare centres.

[p70 | prose | Using smoke to test airflow]
Using smoke to test airflow

[p70 | prose | Using smoke to test airflow]
Smoke can also be used to assess airflow inside the enclosure. After leak testing is complete, turn the air extraction equipment back on and look to see how the smoke clears. Areas where smoke remains concentrated or is slower to clear can indicate dead spots where additional extraction may be needed.

[p70 | prose | Testing air extraction equipment]
Testing air extraction equipment

[p70 | prose | Testing air extraction equipment]
Once the smoke has been cleared and any leaks eliminated as much as possible, the enclosure needs to be further tested to make sure there is slight negative pressure being maintained when the extraction equipment is running. If negative pressure is achieved the airlock flaps should be lifted by the airflow through the system and the enclosure walls will bow inwards ensuring that any residual leaks are inwards.

[p70 | prose | Testing air extraction equipment]
Any additional leak testing can be performed externally using smoke tubes, while the air extraction equipment is running, noting any smoke sucked inside. Smoke tube testing should be carried out around the seals and joins to make sure they are effective.

[p70 | prose | Checking differential pressure monitors]
Checking differential pressure monitors

[p70 | prose | Checking differential pressure monitors]
Before each shift, differential pressure monitor gauges should be checked to make sure negative pressure is being maintained inside the enclosure. They help make sure that air is not leaking out into the building.

[p70 | prose | Checking differential pressure monitors]
A pressure difference of 5 Pascals or more (0.5mm water gauge) is usually enough to prevent air from escaping.

[p70 | prose | Checking differential pressure monitors]
The person nominated to check differential pressure monitors should be trained on how to check monitors and interpret readings correctly.

[p70 | prose | Checking differential pressure monitors]
External factors can affect air pressure, such as:

[p70 | prose | Checking differential pressure monitors]
– wind and drafts (from open doors/windows) which can affect readings by changing air pressure inside the enclosure

[p70 | prose | Checking differential pressure monitors]
– varying pressure between different parts of the enclosure.

[p70 | prose | Checking differential pressure monitors]
Note: Using a differential pressure monitor does not remove the need for smoke testing.

[p70 | prose | Daily inspections and maintenance]
Daily inspections and maintenance

[p70 | prose | Daily inspections and maintenance]
At the start of every shift (especially for occupied buildings), a visual check should be done to make sure:

[p70 | prose | Daily inspections and maintenance]
– there are no breaches in the enclosure

[p70 | prose | Daily inspections and maintenance]
– the enclosure sides are pulled inwards (showing negative pressure is maintained)

[p70 | prose | Daily inspections and maintenance]
– the NPU is functioning correctly. Check the NPU pressure gauge to confirm airflow is adequate

[p70 | prose | Daily inspections and maintenance]
– the enclosure walls remain taut

[p70 | prose | Daily inspections and maintenance]
– the airlock flaps are positioned correctly.

[p70 | prose | Daily inspections and maintenance]
Smoke tests should be done daily if the risk assessment requires it.

[p70 | prose | Viewing panels]
Viewing panels

[p70 | prose | Viewing panels]
Viewing panels should be checked frequently and cleaned as necessary to make sure it is easy to see in the enclosure.

[p70 | prose | Viewing panels]
Airborne dust adhering to viewing panels can indicate excessive dust generation within the enclosure. If they need to be cleaned regularly, this may indicate poor or ineffective stripping techniques.

[p71 | prose | Air monitoring]
Air monitoring

[p71 | prose | Air monitoring]
Air monitoring is used to confirm that control measures are working effectively. For example:

[p71 | prose | Air monitoring]
– that the enclosure, airlocks, and air extraction equipment are working effectively

[p71 | prose | Air monitoring]
– that no asbestos has spread outside the enclosure.

[p71 | prose | Air monitoring]
Air monitoring is required for all Class A asbestos removal and may be needed for some Class B removal work.

[p71 | prose | Air monitoring]
For Class A removal work:

[p71 | prose | Air monitoring]
– air monitoring must be done by an independent licensed asbestos assessor

[p71 | prose | Air monitoring]
– air monitoring must be done during the removal work and next to any negative pressure enclosure

[p71 | prose | Air monitoring]
– the licenced assessor may require air monitoring before the work starts if they think the air might have asbestos fibres at levels higher than trace amounts.

[p71 | prose | Air monitoring]
For more information on air monitoring see Section 10.0: Air monitoring

[p71 | prose | Testing and maintaining air extraction equipment]
Testing and maintaining air extraction equipment

[p71 | prose | Testing and maintaining air extraction equipment]
Workers in charge of operating and maintaining air extraction plants must be trained on how to operate the system correctly. A competent person needs to properly install and inspect the system before use to make sure it is in good condition and correctly assembled.

[p71 | prose | Testing and maintaining air extraction equipment]
Set up a maintenance schedule that includes instructions on what to do if the unit fails or does not meet specified performance, for example by stopping work and replacing the equipment.

[p71 | prose | Testing and maintaining air extraction equipment]
Air extraction equipment must be examined and tested at least every six months by a trained and competent person. This process typically involves:

[p71 | prose | Testing and maintaining air extraction equipment]
– thorough decontamination

[p71 | prose | Testing and maintaining air extraction equipment]
– dismantling of the equipment

[p71 | prose | Testing and maintaining air extraction equipment]
– a detailed visual inspection to confirm all components are in good working order

[p71 | prose | Testing and maintaining air extraction equipment]
– replacing all filters unless there is no significant loss of airflow.

[p71 | prose | Testing and maintaining air extraction equipment]
The performance of the unit should be checked after reassembly to make sure the airflow through the unit and the pressure drop across the HEPA filter still meets the manufacturer’s specification.

[p71 | prose | Testing and maintaining air extraction equipment]
Where the airflow has dropped below its design capacity (for example, a 2000 CFM unit is only achieving 1500 CFM), this should be clearly marked on the unit itself and the lower figure used in ventilation calculations.

[p71 | prose | Testing and maintaining air extraction equipment]
A record of all inspections, examination, maintenance, and any defects related to air extraction units must be kept available for inspection.

[p72 | prose | 9.15 Dismantling and disposal of enclosures]
9.15 Dismantling and disposal of enclosures

[p72 | prose | 9.15 Dismantling and disposal of enclosures]
After the asbestos removal work has finished, and clearance inspection stages 1 to 3 have been completed (see Section 13.0: Clearance inspections), the enclosure can be dismantled.

[p72 | prose | Exposure risks during dismantling]
Exposure risks during dismantling

[p72 | prose | Exposure risks during dismantling]
Dismantling the enclosure should be done carefully and methodically. Workers should regularly check for any hidden asbestos that may be uncovered during dismantling. Asbestos fibres can remain trapped in parts of the enclosure such as between polythene sheets or other crevices or corners. Workers should wear PPE, including coveralls and half-masks while dismantling.

[p72 | prose | Polythene sheeting removal]
Polythene sheeting removal

[p72 | prose | Polythene sheeting removal]
To reduce the risk of releasing asbestos fibres from polythene sheeting during dismantling workers should:

[p72 | prose | Polythene sheeting removal]
– spray the polythene sheeting with PVA sealant

[p72 | prose | Polythene sheeting removal]
– carefully remove, roll or fold, and place sheeting in waste bags immediately

[p72 | prose | Polythene sheeting removal]
– dispose of sheeting as asbestos waste – do not reuse sheeting.

[p72 | prose | Dealing with contamination]
Dealing with contamination

[p72 | prose | Dealing with contamination]
If contamination is found during dismantling, further decontamination must be done, and the visual inspection and air sampling (see Section 13.0: Clearance inspections) must be repeated.

[p72 | prose | Negative pressure unit (NPU)]
Negative pressure unit (NPU)

[p72 | prose | Negative pressure unit (NPU)]
Make sure the NPU is sealed before transport. It can either be taken to the next job or sent for decontamination if needed. Do not send equipment for maintenance unless it has been decontaminated first.

[p72 | prose | Cleaning access equipment]
Cleaning access equipment

[p72 | prose | Cleaning access equipment]
To make it easier to clean access equipment afterwards, prepare and protect access equipment from contamination before taking it into the enclosure. For example:

[p72 | prose | Cleaning access equipment]
– cap scaffold tube ends

[p72 | prose | Cleaning access equipment]
– cover boards with polythene (unless it creates a slipping hazard)

[p72 | prose | Cleaning access equipment]
– protect working parts of mobile equipment working platforms (MEWPs) with robust, adjustable sheeting (make sure to allow for exhaust gas dispersion).

[p72 | prose | Cleaning access equipment]
Decontaminate all exposed items before site clearance.

[p72 | prose | Final inspection]
Final inspection

[p72 | prose | Final inspection]
After removing all equipment, partial enclosures, or protective sheeting the independent licenced assessor will conduct a final visual inspection as a stage of their clearance inspection. For more information see Section 6.3 of the Asbestos assessments – good practice guidelines

[p72 | prose | Reassurance sampling]
Reassurance sampling

[p72 | prose | Reassurance sampling]
Reassurance sampling may be done post-certification to confirm no residual asbestos risk. This is not part of the formal clearance but may be needed for inaccessible crevices, complex equipment, or porous materials. See Asbestos assessments – good practice guidelines

[p73 | prose | Reassurance sampling]
Site clearance without an enclosure

[p73 | prose | Reassurance sampling]
The licenced assessor should follow the normal Class A clearance inspection process with adjustments: perform a preliminary check of site condition, thorough visual inspection, and decide on what Stage 3 steps of the clearance inspection (surface testing air monitoring) are required based on the circumstances. See Asbestos assessments – good practice guidelines

[p73 | prose | 9.16 Emergency procedures]
9.16 Emergency procedures

[p73 | prose | 9.16 Emergency procedures]
Responding to incidents or injuries

[p73 | prose | 9.16 Emergency procedures]
Removal work often involves working at height or in confined spaces within enclosures. These types of work activities carry their own risks that must be controlled. Emergency procedures for evacuating ill or injured workers from enclosures must be planned and these details should be included in the removal plan.

[p73 | prose | 9.16 Emergency procedures]
An adequate number of workers should be trained on how to provide appropriate first aid without unnecessarily exposing themselves or others to asbestos. For example:

[p73 | prose | 9.16 Emergency procedures]
– if the victim can be moved safely, they should be taken outside the enclosure

[p73 | prose | 9.16 Emergency procedures]
– follow safe procedures for removal of a victim’s respirator.

[p73 | prose | 9.16 Emergency procedures]
Decontamination should be performed as much as possible, by vacuuming themselves, the victim, and sponging down RPE and boots. However, evacuation of a seriously ill or injured person should not be delayed by overcomplicated decontamination efforts.

[p73 | prose | Safety of emergency responders]
Safety of emergency responders

[p73 | prose | Safety of emergency responders]
Removalists must include procedures in emergency plans about the potential presence of asbestos to warn emergency responders in advance, so they can prepare for related hazards. Spare disposable protective clothing and RPE should be available for emergency responders who lack their own equipment.

[p73 | prose | Safety of emergency responders]
In cases where emergency responders have had to administer treatment inside the enclosure, they should decontaminate themselves in the DCU afterward.

[p73 | prose | Evacuation procedures]
Evacuation procedures

[p73 | prose | Evacuation procedures]
Workers should be familiar with emergency evacuation procedures for the premises where they are working. This should be covered in the site induction and include:

[p73 | prose | Evacuation procedures]
– location of fire alarms and fire extinguishers

[p73 | prose | Evacuation procedures]
– making sure alarms can be heard from inside the enclosure

[p73 | prose | Evacuation procedures]
– location of emergency exits and escape routes

[p73 | prose | Evacuation procedures]
– emergency access to and from the enclosure.

[p73 | prose | Evacuation procedures]
In an emergency requiring evacuation, prioritise leaving the premises, but do not let decontamination delay escape. Leaving the premises first must come first.

[p73 | prose | Evacuation procedures]
If RPE inhibits escape (for example limits the field of vision), it should be removed inside the enclosure. After reaching safety, PPE and RPE should be decontaminated as much as possible.

[p75 | prose | Use air monitoring to measure airborne asbestos levels to help check if control measures a working effectively.]
Use air monitoring to measure airborne asbestos levels to help check if control measures a working effectively.

[p75 | prose | 10.1 What is air monitoring?]
10.1 What is air monitoring?

[p75 | prose | 10.1 What is air monitoring?]
Air monitoring is a process of measuring the concentration of airborne asbestos fibres to evaluate the effectiveness of asbestos removal control measures.

[p75 | prose | 10.1 What is air monitoring?]
The purpose of air monitoring is to show:

[p75 | prose | 10.1 What is air monitoring?]
– that control measures (such as wet stripping) are effective and being properly used

[p75 | prose | 10.1 What is air monitoring?]
– that the RPE worn is sufficient to provide adequate protection (in addition to primary control measures).

[p75 | prose | 10.1 What is air monitoring?]
Other reasons for carrying out air monitoring include:

[p75 | prose | 10.1 What is air monitoring?]
– benchmark monitoring before asbestos removal starts

[p75 | prose | 10.1 What is air monitoring?]
– during Stage 3 of the four-stage clearance procedure (see Section 13.0: Clearance inspections)

[p75 | prose | 10.1 What is air monitoring?]
– leak sampling to check the ongoing integrity of the enclosure (see Section 13.0: Clearance inspections)

[p75 | prose | 10.1 What is air monitoring?]
– reassurance air sampling after removal work has been completed.

[p75 | prose | 10.1 What is air monitoring?]
There are two main ways to do air monitoring:

[p75 | prose | 10.1 What is air monitoring?]
– static monitoring where monitoring stations are in specific areas inside and outside of an enclosure (including near transiting routes)

[p75 | prose | 10.1 What is air monitoring?]
– personal air monitoring where workers wear portable air monitoring devices.

[p75 | prose | 10.1 What is air monitoring?]
The data from personal air monitoring devices may be used to inform worker exposure data as well (Section 19.0: Exposure monitoring).

[p75 | prose | 10.1 What is air monitoring?]
Static air monitoring results cannot be used for worker exposure monitoring.

[p76 | prose | 10.2 When is air monitoring required?]
10.2 When is air monitoring required?

[p76 | prose | 10.2 When is air monitoring required?]
Air monitoring is required in certain situations, as shown in Table 18.

[p76 | prose | FOR CLASS A LICENSED ASBESTOS REMOVAL WORK]
FOR CLASS A LICENSED ASBESTOS REMOVAL WORK

[p76 | prose | FOR CLASS A LICENSED ASBESTOS REMOVAL WORK]
Air monitoring must be done:

[p76 | prose | FOR CLASS A LICENSED ASBESTOS REMOVAL WORK]
– immediately before Class A asbestos removal work starts (if the licensed asbestos assessor determines it is likely the air contains respirable asbestos fibres in concentrations greater than trace level) – during Class A asbestos removal work

[p76 | prose | FOR CLASS A LICENSED ASBESTOS REMOVAL WORK]
– during Stage 3 of the clearance inspection unless no enclosure is in place and Stage 3 is not feasible .

[p76 | prose | FOR CLASS A LICENSED ASBESTOS REMOVAL WORK]
TABLE 18: When to perform air monitoring

[p76 | prose | FOR CLASS B ASBESTOS REMOVAL AND UNLICENSED ASBESTOS REMOVAL]
FOR CLASS B ASBESTOS REMOVAL AND UNLICENSED ASBESTOS REMOVAL

[p76 | prose | FOR CLASS B ASBESTOS REMOVAL AND UNLICENSED ASBESTOS REMOVAL]
Air monitoring is not required but may be carried out to check if the asbestos removalist is:

[p76 | prose | FOR CLASS B ASBESTOS REMOVAL AND UNLICENSED ASBESTOS REMOVAL]
– complying with their duty to eliminate or minimise exposure to airborne asbestos

[p76 | prose | FOR CLASS B ASBESTOS REMOVAL AND UNLICENSED ASBESTOS REMOVAL]
– not exceeding the airborne contamination standard for asbestos.

[p76 | prose | FOR CLASS B ASBESTOS REMOVAL AND UNLICENSED ASBESTOS REMOVAL]
Air monitoring should also be considered if the asbestos removal work is being done in, or next to a public location.

[p76 | prose | 10.3 Who can do air monitoring?]
10.3 Who can do air monitoring?

[p76 | prose | 10.3 Who can do air monitoring?]
Air monitoring can be carried out by specific people, as shown in Table 19.

[p76 | prose | 10.3 Who can do air monitoring?]
FOR CLASS A ASBESTOS REMOVAL WORK

[p76 | prose | 10.3 Who can do air monitoring?]
FOR CLASS B OR UNLICENSED ASBESTOS REMOVAL WORK

[p76 | prose | 10.3 Who can do air monitoring?]
– An independent licensed asbestos assessor.

[p76 | prose | 10.3 Who can do air monitoring?]
– a licensed asbestos assessor, or

[p76 | prose | 10.3 Who can do air monitoring?]
– a competent person.

[p76 | prose | 10.3 Who can do air monitoring?]
TABLE 19: Who can carry out air monitoring

[p76 | prose | 10.3 Who can do air monitoring?]
For detailed information on what makes an assessor independent, see Asbestos assessments – good practice guidelines

[p76 | prose | 10.3 Who can do air monitoring?]
A competent person is a person who has relevant industry experience, skills, and knowledge. They must also hold either of the following qualifications:

[p76 | prose | 10.3 Who can do air monitoring?]
– a certificate from a training course, specified by WorkSafe for asbestos assessor work

[p76 | prose | 10.3 Who can do air monitoring?]
– a tertiary qualification in occupational health and safety, occupational hygiene, science, or environmental health.

[p76 | prose | 10.3 Who can do air monitoring?]
For detailed information, see Section 2.0: The role of an assessor in the Asbestos assessments – good practice guidelines

[p77 | prose | 10.4 How often should air monitoring be done?]
10.4 How often should air monitoring be done?

[p77 | prose | 10.4 How often should air monitoring be done?]
Class A air monitoring should be done once work starts and continue throughout the duration of the work until the clearance inspection is started. It should be used to confirm that control measures such as dust suppression, enclosure, airlocks, and air extraction equipment are working effectively, and no asbestos has spread outside the enclosure/work area.

[p77 | prose | 10.4 How often should air monitoring be done?]
The requirement for monitoring and its extent will depend on the nature of the work and location. The licensed asbestos assessor will decide the best air monitoring strategy for the work.

[p77 | prose | 10.4 How often should air monitoring be done?]
Class A air monitoring in the vicinity of the NPU discharge outlet(s) is necessary where it vents inside the building. This is particularly important if it has been necessary to replace the NPU during the operation. NPUs should always vent to outside the building wherever possible. Venting inside a building creates additional risk for any building occupants especially if a fault occurs. Venting inside a building should be done in exceptional circumstances only

[p77 | prose | 10.4 How often should air monitoring be done?]
The frequency of air monitoring during Class B removal is to be decided by the assessor based on site specific risk assessment and/or requirements of the commissioning PCBU.

[p77 | prose | 10.4 How often should air monitoring be done?]
For detailed information, see Section 4.0: Asbestos assessments – good practice guidelines

[p77 | prose | Developing an air monitoring plan]
Developing an air monitoring plan

[p77 | prose | Developing an air monitoring plan]
The asbestos assessor will develop a plan to make sure that the correct monitoring is done at the correct times, and that monitoring will test a representative range of jobs and work methods (see Section 4.0: Asbestos assessments – good practice guidelines).

[p77 | prose | Developing an air monitoring plan]
Occupational exposures can vary from day to day and even throughout a shift. The plan should take account of the range of circumstances and conditions that can occur. For example, different:

[p77 | prose | Developing an air monitoring plan]
– asbestos types

[p77 | prose | Developing an air monitoring plan]
– work methods

[p77 | prose | Developing an air monitoring plan]
– work areas, or transit routes

[p77 | prose | Developing an air monitoring plan]
– work durations.

[p77 | prose | Developing an air monitoring plan]
Air sampling results should be used to inform and modify control measures as necessary.

[p77 | prose | 10.5 Assessing air monitoring results]
10.5 Assessing air monitoring results

[p77 | prose | 10.5 Assessing air monitoring results]
The asbestos assessor should interpret the results and provide advice if any action needs to be taken, particularly if results show that respirable asbestos fibre levels are exceeding prescribed action levels.

[p77 | prose | 10.5 Assessing air monitoring results]
For more information on assessing monitoring results, see see Section 4.4: Asbestos assessments – good practice guidelines

[p77 | prose | 10.5 Assessing air monitoring results]
Data from personal air monitoring can be included in exposure data for the worker who was wearing that monitoring device. For more information, see Section 19.2: When and how exposure monitoring should be done for removal work

[p78 | prose | 10.6 Action to take if asbestos fibres are exceeded]
10.6 Action to take if asbestos fibres are exceeded

[p78 | prose | 10.6 Action to take if asbestos fibres are exceeded]
During Class A removal work, if the results of static air monitoring outside the enclosure show that respirable asbestos fibre levels exceed the prescribed action levels, the licensed asbestos removalist must take immediate action.

[p78 | prose | 10.6 Action to take if asbestos fibres are exceeded]
Table 20 below summarises the prescribed action level and actions for Class A asbestos removal static air monitoring (outside of enclosures).

[p78 | table | 10.6 Action to take if asbestos fibres are exceeded]
| ACTION LEVEL | CONTROL | IMMEDIATE ACTION |
| --- | --- | --- |
| < 0.01 fibres/ml (trace level) | No new control measures are necessary | Continue with existing measures. |
| = 0.01 fibres/ml but | . Investigate | Investigate the cause. |
| <0.02 fibres/ml | 2 Implement | Put controls in place to prevent exposure. |
| 3 Prevent | Prevent further fibre release. |  |
| =0.02 fibres/ml | Stop | Order all asbestos removal work to stop straight away. |
| Notify | Notify WorkSafe as a notifiable incident as soon as possible. Include the results of the air monitoring. |  |
| Investigate | As soon as possible: 1. Determine the cause of the high fibre levels. 2. Conduct a thorough visual inspection of the enclosure (if used) and associated equipment in consultation with all asbestos workers. 3. Review controls. |  |
| Put controls in place to prevent exposure and further asbestos fibre release | As soon as possible: 1. Extend the isolated/barricaded area around the work area/enclosure so far as is reasonably practicable (until fibre levels are at or below 0.01 fibres/ml). |  |
| 2. Wet-wipe and vacuum the surrounding area, seal any identified leaks (for example, with expandable foam or tape). |  |  |
| 3. Smoke test the enclosure until it is satisfactorily sealed. |  |  |
| Conduct further air monitoring | Do not re-start until fibre levels are at or below 0.01 fibres/ml. |  |
| Retain records for five years |  |  |

[p78 | prose | 10.6 Action to take if asbestos fibres are exceeded]
TABLE 20: Class A asbestos removal static air monitoring actions (outside enclosure)

[p78 | prose | 10.6 Action to take if asbestos fibres are exceeded]
If asbestos fibre levels exceed 0.1 f/ml for any other type of work (Class B, unlicensed, asbestos-related) the results must also be shared with the parties listed in Sections 10.7 or 10.8

[p79 | prose | 10.7 Communicating air monitoring results]
10.7 Communicating air monitoring results

[p79 | prose | 10.7 Communicating air monitoring results]
For Class A asbestos removal, the PCBU who commissions the licensed asbestos removal work must make sure air monitoring results are given to:

[p79 | prose | 10.7 Communicating air monitoring results]
– workers at the workplace

[p79 | prose | 10.7 Communicating air monitoring results]
– worker representatives

[p79 | prose | 10.7 Communicating air monitoring results]
– PCBUs at the workplace (including the licensed asbestos removalist)

[p79 | prose | 10.7 Communicating air monitoring results]
– other people at the workplace

[p79 | prose | 10.7 Communicating air monitoring results]
– other people living or working in the vicinity of the workplace if it is likely they may be affected by the contamination (so far as is reasonably practicable).

[p79 | prose | 10.8 When the workplace is a home]
10.8 When the workplace is a home

[p79 | prose | 10.8 When the workplace is a home]
If the workplace is a home, the licensed asbestos removalist must make sure the results of the air monitoring are given to:

[p79 | prose | 10.8 When the workplace is a home]
– the PCBU who commissioned the work, such as the landlord or property management company

[p79 | prose | 10.8 When the workplace is a home]
– workers at the workplace

[p79 | prose | 10.8 When the workplace is a home]
– worker representatives

[p79 | prose | 10.8 When the workplace is a home]
– PCBUs in relation to the workplace

[p79 | prose | 10.8 When the workplace is a home]
– the occupier of the home

[p79 | prose | 10.8 When the workplace is a home]
– the owner of the home (if not the occupier)

[p79 | prose | 10.8 When the workplace is a home]
– other people at the workplace.

[p80 | prose | 10.8 When the workplace is a home]
7878

[p81 | prose | 10.8 When the workplace is a home]
Controlled techniques help minimise risk during removal and repair of asbestos and asbestos-containing materials (ACMs).

[p81 | prose | 10.8 When the workplace is a home]
This section focuses mainly on stripping methods for friable asbestos. Information sheets outlining approved methods for the removal of various non-friable asbestos removal safe work practices

[p81 | prose | 10.8 When the workplace is a home]
This guide does not cover asbestos in soil. For detailed information about remediation or removal of asbestos in soil see New Zealand Guidelines for Assessing and Managing Asbestos in Soil

[p81 | prose | 11.1 Choosing an asbestos stripping technique]
11.1 Choosing an asbestos stripping technique

[p81 | prose | 11.1 Choosing an asbestos stripping technique]
There are two main types of controlled stripping techniques:

[p81 | prose | 11.1 Choosing an asbestos stripping technique]
– controlled wet stripping

[p81 | prose | 11.1 Choosing an asbestos stripping technique]
– dry stripping with control at source.

[p81 | prose | 11.1 Choosing an asbestos stripping technique]
For more information on using these methods safely, see Sections 11.3: Controlled wet stripping through to Section 11.5: Dry stripping with control at source

[p81 | prose | 11.1 Choosing an asbestos stripping technique]
There are several factors to consider before deciding what stripping technique, or combination of techniques, to use. These should be detailed in the risk assessment and ARCP).

[p81 | prose | 11.1 Choosing an asbestos stripping technique]
Factors to consider include:

[p81 | prose | 11.1 Choosing an asbestos stripping technique]
– the requirement to minimise the amount of fibres generated at the point where asbestos is being stripped:

[p81 | prose | 11.1 Choosing an asbestos stripping technique]
- choose the stripping method (or combination of methods) to cause the least amount of disturbance

[p81 | prose | 11.1 Choosing an asbestos stripping technique]
- minimise disturbance in the enclosure to keep air concentrations lower, help keep workers safer and make later decontamination easier

[p81 | prose | 11.1 Choosing an asbestos stripping technique]
– the type of asbestos being removed

[p81 | prose | 11.1 Choosing an asbestos stripping technique]
– any coatings applied to the asbestos (this can affect how easy it is to wet the asbestos)

[p81 | prose | 11.1 Choosing an asbestos stripping technique]
– how the asbestos is attached (for example, screws, nails, clips, or glue)

[p81 | prose | 11.1 Choosing an asbestos stripping technique]
– what the asbestos is attached to (for example, asbestos applied onto a bituminous layer). This can affect the ease of removal

[p81 | prose | 11.1 Choosing an asbestos stripping technique]
– what the most appropriate wetting method is for the type of product (spray or injection, or both)

[p81 | prose | 11.1 Choosing an asbestos stripping technique]
– the location of the asbestos and ACM and the nature of the work area, such as accessibility and environmental conditions.

[p81 | prose | 11.1 Choosing an asbestos stripping technique]
– the need to prevent the escape of asbestos fibres from the enclosure to the general environment.

[p82 | prose | 11.1 Choosing an asbestos stripping technique]
In addition to the requirement to keep exposure to asbestos as low as reasonably practicable, removalists need to make sure their chosen stripping method does not create other risks. For example, the:

[p82 | prose | 11.1 Choosing an asbestos stripping technique]
– presence of live electrical equipment may prevent, or restrict, the use of controlled wet stripping

[p82 | prose | 11.1 Choosing an asbestos stripping technique]
– presence of chemicals may present a direct risk to the workers or prevent the use of controlled wet stripping techniques

[p82 | prose | 11.1 Choosing an asbestos stripping technique]
– use of wetting agents may create a risk of slips. This is particularly important when working at height.

[p82 | prose | 11.1 Choosing an asbestos stripping technique]
See Section 11.7: Managing other risks during asbestos removal work for more information.

[p82 | prose | 11.2 General good practice for stripping]
11.2 General good practice for stripping

[p82 | prose | 11.2 General good practice for stripping]
Table 21 below shows general good practice for stripping asbestos.

[p82 | table | 11.2 General good practice for stripping]
| GOOD PRACTICE | DETAILS |
| --- | --- |
| Worker training | Provide adequate training, including refresher training, on controlled stripping techniques. Site supervisors require more detailed training to oversee work effectively. |
| Site supervision | Maintain close supervision to make sure control measures and safe work methods are followed. Use viewing panels in enclosures to monitor workers. |
| Maintenance of equipment | Regularly maintain all equipment to make sure it operates effectively throughout the work. |
| Waste control | Use good waste management practices, such as clearing waste as work progresses to maintain a safe and controlled environment. |
| Avoid breakage wherever possible | Always attempt to remove asbestos and ACM (such as AIB) without breaking it. Where breakage is the only option, use wetting on exposed surfaces to prevent dust being created. Use shadow vacuuming as well. |
| Removing nailed boards | Spray any unpainted surfaces first then place heavy-duty tape over the areas where there are nails and carefully ease the board away from the wall using a crowbar (or similar) without breaking the board. Once a nail has been exposed between the board and other surface, if possible, cut the nail |
| Locating hidden nails Or screws | Use magnets to locate screws or nails which have been hidden by paint or other coatings. If the nails or screws cannot be easily found use the method for removing glued boards. |
| Removing glued boards | If the boards cannot be easily lifted without breaking, then scribing and breaking can be used. This should only be used if other safer methods will not work as there can be significant fibre release. Use wetting and shadow vacuuming to minimise fibre release. |

[p82 | prose | 11.2 General good practice for stripping]
TABLE 21: Good practice for asbestos stripping

[p82 | prose | 11.2 General good practice for stripping]
This section focuses mainly on stripping methods for friable asbestos. Information sheets outlining approved methods for the removal of various non-friable asbestos removal safe work practices

[p82 | prose | Using wetting agents]
Using wetting agents

[p82 | prose | Using wetting agents]
The amount of asbestos fibre released during removal can be reduced by soaking or wetting the asbestos with water or a wetting agent. Wetting agents contain additional chemicals to help penetrate ACM (such as an added surfactant). Even though some types of asbestos can be wetted just with water, wetting

[p83 | prose | Using wetting agents]
agents should always be used, as a mixture of asbestos types may be present. Some wetting agents can also ease removal by loosening the binding agents within the ACM.

[p83 | prose | Using wetting agents]
Wetting agents should always be used according to the manufacturer’s instructions.

[p83 | prose | Using wetting agents]
PPE, including RPE, should always be used when injecting or applying wetting agents.

[p83 | prose | Using wetting agents]
Asbestos stripping workers should receive specific and detailed training in the use of controlled wet stripping techniques, and in the problems which may be encountered.

[p83 | prose | Using wetting agents]
Wetting agents can be applied by injection or spraying. For more information on these techniques, see Section 11.3: Controlled wetting by injection and Section 11.4: Controlled wetting by spraying

[p83 | prose | Using wetting agents]
The asbestos should be wetted all the way through to reduce fibre levels as much as possible. Table 22 lists important factors to make sure wetting is effective.

[p83 | table | Using wetting agents]
| FACTORS FOR WETTING | DETAILS |
| --- | --- |
| Allow enough time for the wetting agent to thoroughly penetrate the ACM | The time taken will depend on the type of ACM being injected: = More porous and less dense materials, such as sprayed asbestos coatings, become saturated more quickly. Denser and less porous materials such as hand applied pipe lagging and high calcium silicate pipe lagging may take longer. Some materials may be removed after only 3-4 hours soaking, while others may require up to 24 hours. With some ACMs, there is a visible colour change when adequate wetting has been achieved. The degree of penetration and wetting should be checked by visual examination before attempting removal. If the wetting agent is being used to suppress fibre release during preparation for injection, only a few minutes may be necessary. |
| Avoid over-wetting | Over wetting may cause: - wetting agent to seep out of cracks in the asbestos - asbestos coating or lagging to become sodden, causing the asbestos to fall off under its own weight. |
| Check for signs that it is safe to start removal | With some ACMs, there is a visible colour change when adequate wetting has been achieved. Lagging should be of a dough-like consistency when adequately wetted. Take small samples to check the degree of penetration and wetting. When wetting by injection: - take samples from areas further away from the pump, near the last group of needles - during testing use shadow vacuuming in case any dry patches are found - any dry patches should be sprayed immediately and then re-injected. |

[p83 | prose | Using wetting agents]
TABLE 22: Key factors for effective wetting of asbestos

[p83 | prose | Using wetting agents]
Workers carrying out the wetting should have sufficient training and experience to judge when it is safe to begin removal. Supervisors should be available to provide advice or confirmation if needed.

[p83 | prose | 11.3 Controlled wetting by injection]
11.3 Controlled wetting by injection

[p83 | prose | 11.3 Controlled wetting by injection]
Injection techniques can be used when the outer surface of the asbestos is sealed, covered, or coated and the skin will prevent loss of fluid. It is particularly useful for friable insulation and coatings, such as boiler lagging or limpet.

[p83 | prose | 11.3 Controlled wetting by injection]
Injection and preparation for injection should be done after the enclosure has been built and smoke tested. PPE and RPE should be worn.

[p84 | prose | 11.3 Controlled wetting by injection]
When using wet injection, consider the following:

[p84 | prose | 11.3 Controlled wetting by injection]
– what injection system to use

[p84 | prose | 11.3 Controlled wetting by injection]
– needle type and size

[p84 | prose | 11.3 Controlled wetting by injection]
– needle placement

[p84 | prose | 11.3 Controlled wetting by injection]
– flow rate of wetting agent

[p84 | prose | 11.3 Controlled wetting by injection]
– surface thickness to be injected.

[p84 | prose | Choose the right injection system]
Choose the right injection system

[p84 | prose | Choose the right injection system]
Injection systems come in two basic forms: multi and single point. Table 23 explains the differences between them.

[p84 | prose | MULTI-POINT SYSTEM]
MULTI-POINT SYSTEM

[p84 | prose | SINGLE-POINT INJECTION]
SINGLE-POINT INJECTION

[p84 | prose | SINGLE-POINT INJECTION]
Multi-point systems have a number of needles connected together and are connected to a common injection pump:

[p84 | prose | SINGLE-POINT INJECTION]
– hedgehogs (where the needles are grouped together on a flat board) can be used for flat surfaces (Figure 21)

[p84 | prose | SINGLE-POINT INJECTION]
– string of needles, usually set 10–15cm apart and linked by tubing from the injection pump can be wrapped around the item containing asbestos (Figure 22).

[p84 | prose | SINGLE-POINT INJECTION]
Single-point injection systems (such as needle guns) deliver wetting agent to single small areas each time:

[p84 | prose | SINGLE-POINT INJECTION]
– they can be useful for inaccessible areas, where it is difficult to set up a multi-point unit

[p84 | prose | SINGLE-POINT INJECTION]
– single-point injection systems should only be used when multi-point systems cannot be used.

[p84 | prose | SINGLE-POINT INJECTION]
Needles in this system can be fitted with individual flow control valves which allows adjustment to make sure the appropriate volume of fluid is delivered to each needle.

[p84 | prose | TABLE 23: Comparison of multi-point and single-point injection systems]
TABLE 23: Comparison of multi-point and single-point injection systems

[p84 | prose | Needle type and size]
Needle type and size

[p84 | prose | Needle type and size]
Injection needles are available in many sizes and designs. The specification of the needle will depend on the characteristics of the asbestos and ACM, particularly thickness, shape, and condition.

[p84 | prose | Needle type and size]
Thin coatings or insulation (1 cm or less), such as a sprayed coating, require needles with holes at the tip, or long, angled needles. This allows the needle to be pushed into and flat with the thin coating (Figure 21). These angled needles help the lateral movement of the wetting agent.

[p84 | prose | Needle type and size]
Thick coatings or insulation require long needles with holes along their length, sufficient to penetrate the full depth of the insulation. The holes should face the substrate.

[p84 | prose | Needle type and size]
Structure (concrete-steel-wood)

[p84 | prose | Needle type and size]
FIGURE 21: Needle board multi-point injection system for thin asbestos layers

[p85 | prose | Needle type and size]
FIGURE 22: Controlled multi-point injection delivery through a network of fine needles

[p85 | prose | Needle placement]
Needle placement

[p85 | prose | Needle placement]
Once the needles have been selected, consider carefully where to place them:

[p85 | prose | Needle placement]
– If needles are placed too far apart, dry patches may occur.

[p85 | prose | Needle placement]
– The greater the number of needles, the more likely it is that uniform penetration will be achieved.

[p85 | prose | Needle placement]
– Where reasonably practicable, place needles on the top side so gravity helps move the wetting agent through the asbestos.

[p85 | prose | Needle placement]
– Horizontal pipes should have needles running along the top of the pipe, spaced 10–15cm apart (Figures 21 and 22), allowing the wetting solution to diffuse down from the top of the lagging.

[p85 | prose | Needle placement]
– Large diameter pipes may need additional runs of needles, again towards the top of the horizontal pipe.

[p85 | prose | Needle placement]
– Vertical pipes should have needles placed horizontally around the top of the pipe, allowing wetting solution to diffuse as a wet band down the lagging. They should not spiral down the pipe.

[p85 | prose | Needle placement]
– Tall vertical pipes may need additional horizontal rings of needles every 1–2m.

[p85 | prose | Flow rate of wetting agent]
Flow rate of wetting agent

[p85 | prose | Flow rate of wetting agent]
Wet injection techniques should be operated at a low pressure (under 50psi) to make sure controlled, uniform wetting of asbestos and ACM. Capillary action aids penetration, with gravity assisting downward flow. Higher pressure may cause the wetting agent to follow paths of least resistance, leading to uneven wetting.

[p85 | prose | Penetrating dense or thick surfaces]
Penetrating dense or thick surfaces

[p85 | prose | Penetrating dense or thick surfaces]
For lagging covered with a cement-like layer (typically 6mm thick), holes may need to be drilled to allow needle access by either:

[p85 | prose | Penetrating dense or thick surfaces]
– using hand drills and shadow vacuuming

[p85 | prose | Penetrating dense or thick surfaces]
– drilling using a low-speed drill with a cowl around the bit and fitted with LEV.

[p85 | prose | Penetrating dense or thick surfaces]
If pipe or vessel lagging is covered in metal cladding, carefully remove it to expose the lagging. If the lagging is damaged, use airless sprays and shadow vacuuming while removing the cladding. Make sure casings that can be drilled are fully wet before continuing.

[p86 | prose | Penetrating dense or thick surfaces]
Some surfaces (such as lagging on some industrial plant) may be so thick that thorough wetting cannot be achieved in one attempt. In these cases, wetting should be done progressively:

[p86 | prose | Penetrating dense or thick surfaces]
– remove any outer layers (such as metal cladding)

[p86 | prose | Penetrating dense or thick surfaces]
– inject the material using the longest needles available, or liberally spray with a wetting agent

[p86 | prose | Penetrating dense or thick surfaces]
– allow the agent to soak into the outer layer of the lagging.

[p86 | prose | Penetrating dense or thick surfaces]
– once the outer material is wetted, carefully remove it until the material is starting to look dry again.

[p86 | prose | Penetrating dense or thick surfaces]
– re-inject or re-spray and repeat the process.

[p86 | prose | Injecting damaged ACM]
Injecting damaged ACM

[p86 | prose | Injecting damaged ACM]
Injection may disturb or break damaged asbestos. If damage is minor, use a generous amount of wetting agent. If there is a risk asbestos may fall off, or there is obvious cracking, wrap the material in impervious sheeting, tape it, and inject carefully.

[p86 | prose | Injecting damaged ACM]
