Insulation that has internal cracks or varying porosity can make even wetting difficult. Position needles carefully for cracked areas and adjust flow rates for materials with variable porosity.

[p86 | prose | 11.4 Controlled wetting by spraying]
11.4 Controlled wetting by spraying

[p86 | prose | 11.4 Controlled wetting by spraying]
Spray wetting can be used in the following applications:

[p86 | prose | 11.4 Controlled wetting by spraying]
– where the ACM is unsealed and porous (such as thin, sprayed coatings)

[p86 | prose | 11.4 Controlled wetting by spraying]
– where the ACM is thin (less than 1cm thick)

[p86 | prose | 11.4 Controlled wetting by spraying]
– in preparation for injecting (such as before the injection of damaged pipe lagging)

[p86 | prose | 11.4 Controlled wetting by spraying]
– for the removal of AIB

[p86 | prose | 11.4 Controlled wetting by spraying]
– for asbestos textiles, including blankets and rope seals

[p86 | prose | 11.4 Controlled wetting by spraying]
– when used with glove bags

[p86 | prose | 11.4 Controlled wetting by spraying]
– when removing asbestos debris

[p86 | prose | 11.4 Controlled wetting by spraying]
– when working on asbestos cement.

[p86 | prose | 11.4 Controlled wetting by spraying]
The method of application will vary depending on the type of material. Table 24 shows examples of how different asbestos materials are applied.

[p86 | table | 11.4 Controlled wetting by spraying]
| PRODUCT OR SURFACE TO BE WETTED | APPLICATION METHOD |
| --- | --- |
| Unsealed sprayed coatings | Use a wide-angled and fine spray. The spray should be moved continuously back and forth across the surface, avoiding disturbance. The number of spraying passes required will depend on the material. |
| Unpainted AIB boards and tiles | Vacuum unpainted surfaces to remove all dust and debris, then spray as described for unsealed sprayed coatings. Once they are thoroughly wet, carefully remove by unscrewing while shadow vacuuming. |
| Painted IB boards and tiles | Where the unpainted surface is not accessible, remove a single tile or board by unscrewing while using shadow vacuuming. A magnet can be used to locate screws hidden by paint. Vacuum the unpainted top surface and spray it. Once access is available to the upper surfaces of the surrounding boards or tiles, vacuum and spray them Wetting the upper surfaces of ceiling tiles can be done using long metal tubes with holes along their length. The metal tubes can be laid on the surface of the AIB and the wetting agent fed through in a controlled manner. |

[p87 | table | 11.4 Controlled wetting by spraying]
| PRODUCT OR SURFACE TO BE WETTED | APPLICATION METHOD |
| --- | --- |
| Insulating blankets, ropes, and quilts | Spray wetting agents over all accessible surfaces |
| Hard plastered or coated surfaces | These surfaces are typically impermeable to sprayed wetting agents or direct injection. Pre-drilling holes under controlled conditions followed by injection can be used (see Section 11.3: Controlled wetting by injection). Alternatively, remove this material by carefully cracking it while applying a fine spray: - Once cracks appear, the spray can be directed along the cracks to aid wetting. - The material should then be carefully removed without further breakage. Shadow vacuuming can also be used in some circumstances. Because this material does not readily soak up wetting agent, there may be some spilling of the agent. Polythene sheeting or a suitable container should be placed under the spraying point to collect any spillage. |
| Small areas | For relatively small applications, such as the preparation for injection or removal of AIB tiles, use hand-pressurised and operated spraying equipment, such as those used in gardening (Figure 23). |
| Large areas | For larger areas use a low-pressure spraying machine (less than 50 psi). Multi-point tubes can be used to cover a wider area (Figure 24). |

[p87 | prose | 11.4 Controlled wetting by spraying]
TABLE 24: Application methods for different types of asbestos materials

[p87 | prose | 11.4 Controlled wetting by spraying]
FIGURE 23: Spraying AIB ceiling tiles

[p88 | prose | 11.4 Controlled wetting by spraying]
FIGURE 24: Multi-point spray system

[p88 | prose | Troubleshooting wet stripping techniques]
Troubleshooting wet stripping techniques

[p88 | prose | Troubleshooting wet stripping techniques]
There are situations where the use of wetting agents can cause other risks or problems. Control measures must be put in place to address these.

[p88 | prose | Troubleshooting wet stripping techniques]
Table 25 shows control measures to address the risks of using wetting agents.

[p88 | table | Troubleshooting wet stripping techniques]
| RISK | CONTROL MEASURE |
| --- | --- |
| Wetting agent may cause skin irritation | Workers should already be wearing full PPE when applying wetting agent. If workers are accidentally exposed to wetting agent directly on their skin check the manufacturer’s safety data sheet and follow instructions on what precautions to take. |
| Wetting agents causing | Place drip trays or troughs under the area being treated. |
| Extreme temperature conditions affecting performance of wetting agent | In freezing weather conditions wetting agent may not spread properly. Wetting agents can be treated to allow for this. Liquids are also available that work at up to 240°C, although hot work should be avoided as much as see near hot in Section 11.7 |
|  | reasonably practicable, Working plant |
| Wetting agents staining building materials | Added dyes in wetting agents can lead to the discolouration of the fabric of the building. Use of polythene sheeting on susceptible surfaces to prevent this. |

[p88 | prose | Troubleshooting wet stripping techniques]
TABLE 25: Control measures for risks from wetting agents

[p89 | prose | 11.5 Dry stripping with control at source]
11.5 Dry stripping with control at source

[p89 | prose | 11.5 Dry stripping with control at source]
There may be some situations where it is not possible to use wet stripping techniques for safety reasons, for example, working directly by live electrics that cannot be isolated. In these circumstances, strict direct supervision and other methods should be used to control asbestos at source.

[p89 | prose | 11.5 Dry stripping with control at source]
At source controls include:

[p89 | prose | 11.5 Dry stripping with control at source]
– wrap and cut

[p89 | prose | 11.5 Dry stripping with control at source]
– glove bags

[p89 | prose | 11.5 Dry stripping with control at source]
– direct vacuuming

[p89 | prose | 11.5 Dry stripping with control at source]
– enhanced air management.

[p89 | prose | Ventilated enclosures are not considered a control at source]
Ventilated enclosures are not considered a control at source

[p89 | prose | Ventilated enclosures are not considered a control at source]
Ventilated enclosures do not provide control at source. They do not regulate fibre release at the point of removal. Their primary purpose is to reduce the spread of asbestos.

[p89 | prose | Wrap-and-cut]
Wrap-and-cut

[p89 | prose | Wrap-and-cut]
Wrap-and-cut is where the asbestos is left in-situ on or in the object (such as a piece of plant or lagged pipework) and the entire object is wrapped up/sealed and removed.

[p89 | prose | Wrap-and-cut]
Enclosures are usually still required when wrap-and-cut methods are being used. Always wear full PPE and RPE while wrap-and-cut methods are used.

[p89 | prose | Wrap-and-cut]
Wrap-and-cut is only suitable in certain circumstances:

[p89 | prose | Wrap-and-cut]
– The object is no longer needed or will be replaced.

[p89 | prose | Wrap-and-cut]
– Any contents of the object have been removed.

[p89 | prose | Wrap-and-cut]
– The object has been cleaned, where necessary, to remove any residual hazardous materials.

[p89 | prose | Wrap-and-cut]
The object needs to be manageable in size:

[p89 | prose | Wrap-and-cut]
– Ideally it should be small enough to be removed through the baglock and fit inside waste containers for transporting.

[p89 | prose | Wrap-and-cut]
– It needs to be small enough that workers can handle it without causing injuries.

[p89 | prose | Wrap-and-cut]
– If it is too big there is a greater chance of the polyethene sheeting ripping during handling.

[p89 | prose | Wrap-and-cut]
– Some disposal facilities may have size limits for wrapped waste.

[p89 | prose | Wrap-and-cut]
Wrapped items too large to pass through the baglock should remain in the work area until the four-stage clearance has been completed and the enclosure removed. These items will be subject to the four-stage clearance process. For more information, see Section 13.0: Clearance inspections

[p89 | prose | Wrap-and-cut]
Wrap-and-cut does not eliminate asbestos disturbance or release. For example, when lagging needs to be removed to allow cutting of the exposed pipework, or from disturbing damaged areas of lagging.

[p90 | prose | Wrap-and-cut]
The following steps will help minimise disturbing asbestos when wrapping and cutting lagging:

[p90 | prose | Wrap-and-cut]
– Treat lagging with a penetrating encapsulant which will bind the asbestos fibres together.

[p90 | prose | Wrap-and-cut]
– Wet the lagging using injection or spraying with a wetting agent. – For small areas of damage near the area to be wrapped and cut, seal with polythene sheeting and tape.

[p90 | prose | Wrap-and-cut]
– Where there are several areas of damage along a length of pipework, the entire run can be dampened using sprays and wrapped in polythene sheeting.

[p90 | prose | Wrap-and-cut]
– Glove bag systems can also be useful for stripping short sections of pipework (see Glove bags in Section 11.5: Dry stripping with control at source

[p90 | prose | Dividing pipework or plant into manageable sections]
Dividing pipework or plant into manageable sections

[p90 | prose | Dividing pipework or plant into manageable sections]
Long stretches of pipe work will have to be reduced before they can be removed:

[p90 | prose | Dividing pipework or plant into manageable sections]
– Look for breaks in the lagging where flanges could be unbolted or pipe hangers removed, or the bare pipe cut without disturbing the asbestos.

[p90 | prose | Dividing pipework or plant into manageable sections]
– Where such breaks do not occur naturally, or if they are not at convenient positions, short sections of lagging will have to be removed.

[p90 | prose | Dividing pipework or plant into manageable sections]
- Make sure enough lagging is removed to allow cutting/burning to take place, without disturbing the remaining asbestos. At least 20–30cm of pipework should be exposed.

[p90 | prose | Dividing pipework or plant into manageable sections]
- Wrap the lagging with heavy-duty polythene and seal the end sections securely with tape.

[p90 | prose | Dividing pipework or plant into manageable sections]
– Pipework can be cut using:

[p90 | prose | Dividing pipework or plant into manageable sections]
- hacksaws for very small diameter pipes.

[p90 | prose | Dividing pipework or plant into manageable sections]
- abrasive cut-off wheels and flame-cutting techniques for larger diameter pipework. Be careful to avoid damaging the wrapped ends of the lagging and use flame retardant sheeting.

[p90 | prose | Dividing pipework or plant into manageable sections]
– Support pipework while it is being worked on.

[p90 | prose | Dividing pipework or plant into manageable sections]
– Wrapped sections of pipework should be carefully supported during cutting and/or unbolting, such as using sheet metal bands at sling attachment points.

[p90 | prose | Dividing pipework or plant into manageable sections]
– Pipework sections should be transferred to the skip/disposal container as soon as reasonably practicable.

[p90 | prose | Glove bags]
Glove bags

[p90 | prose | Glove bags]
Glove bags are made of strong clear plastic materials that are designed to allow stripping activities inside the bag by an external worker using integrated plastic gloves. The top part of a glove bag fits around the item to be stripped while the bottom part acts as storage for tools and asbestos waste. A vacuum should be attached to create slight negative pressure.

[p90 | prose | Glove bags]
Enclosures are usually still required even when glove bags are being used. This is because if the bag fails (for example, a failed or punctured seal) there would be no way to stop asbestos spreading.

[p90 | prose | Glove bags]
Only use glove bags without an enclosure where the risk assessment shows minimal risks to others if the glove bag leaks or fails (where the site is remote and far away from people).

[p91 | prose | Glove bags]
FIGURE 25:

[p91 | prose | Glove bags]
Example of glove bag setup with HEPA vacuum

[p91 | prose | Glove bags should:]
Glove bags should:

[p91 | prose | Glove bags should:]
– be made from a strong material that does not tear

[p91 | prose | Glove bags should:]
– have shoulders wide enough to seal the bag properly to the pipe or vessel

[p91 | prose | Glove bags should:]
– have internal zips to seal and isolate waste at the bottom, so the top can be purged once the work is done. This helps minimise fibre release when removing the bag

[p91 | prose | Glove bags should:]
– have an entry port to insert a spray nozzle to wet the asbestos or a vacuum nozzle to create negative pressure and purge the top of the bag once work is done.

[p91 | prose | When using glove bags, make sure:]
When using glove bags, make sure:

[p91 | prose | When using glove bags, make sure:]
– workers have had specific training on the use of glove bags

[p91 | prose | When using glove bags, make sure:]
– the operators wear suitable PPE and RPE

[p91 | prose | When using glove bags, make sure:]
– the work area is well-defined, movable items such as furniture are removed, and ledges and other surfaces are covered in polythene sheeting

[p91 | prose | When using glove bags, make sure:]
– the glove bag completely covers the section of pipe or other structure being worked on

[p91 | prose | When using glove bags, make sure:]
– the glove bag fully seals in the work area - smoke-test using a smoke tube to confirm this before starting work

[p91 | prose | When using glove bags, make sure:]
– where reasonably practicable, that the work is carried out with the glove bag under slight negative pressure

[p91 | prose | When using glove bags, make sure:]
– wet stripping techniques are used where reasonably practicable (small spray bottles may be useful)

[p91 | prose | When using glove bags, make sure:]
– the glove bag is only used once and not reused

[p91 | prose | When using glove bags, make sure:]
– the glove bag is not moved - do not slide it along pipes

[p91 | prose | When using glove bags, make sure:]
– the glove bag is not used for jobs requiring more than two glove bags, unless the assessment shows that this is safe for the job in hand

[p91 | prose | When using glove bags, make sure:]
– the glove bag needs two operators to use it safely

[p91 | prose | When using glove bags, make sure:]
– removed asbestos waste is fully sealed while still inside the glove bag

[p91 | prose | When using glove bags, make sure:]
– contingency plans, and equipment for spillages and clean-up, are in place.

[p92 | prose | When using glove bags, make sure:]
Have a safe system for using and removing the tools from the glove bag, without compromising the glove bag’s integrity. For example:

[p92 | prose | When using glove bags, make sure:]
1. Hold the tool in one hand within the glove of the glove bag.

[p92 | prose | When using glove bags, make sure:]
2. Pull the tool out (keeping it within the glove sleeve).

[p92 | prose | When using glove bags, make sure:]
3. Twist the lower end of the sleeve to enclose the tool in a pouch at the end.

[p92 | prose | When using glove bags, make sure:]
4. Seal the twisted end at two points several centimetres apart.

[p92 | prose | When using glove bags, make sure:]
5. Cut between the two points, this will leave the tool sealed in the glove and the elbow or shoulder of the glove sealed to prevent any escape of fibres.

[p92 | prose | When using glove bags, make sure:]
6. The glove pouch can be placed in the next glove bag for use or in a container of water for cleaning later.

[p92 | prose | Direct vacuum removal]
Direct vacuum removal

[p92 | prose | Direct vacuum removal]
Direct vacuuming removal is removing and vacuuming asbestos at source using purpose-designed vacuum equipment. It can be used for removing loose asbestos (such as thermal or noise insulation), where wetting could cause it to bond to other surfaces.

[p92 | prose | Direct vacuum removal]
Enclosures are still required when direct vacuum removal is being used. Always wear full PPE and RPE.

[p92 | prose | Direct vacuum removal]
When using direct vacuum removal consider the following:

[p92 | prose | Direct vacuum removal]
– Vacuuming will not work properly on wet asbestos. Any wet asbestos will have to be removed using other techniques, with appropriate controls in place.

[p92 | prose | Direct vacuum removal]
– The asbestos being removed will need to be transported, isolated, and bagged under controlled conditions. The bagging unit should be in a separate ventilated enclosure at negative pressure to prevent leaks.

[p92 | prose | Direct vacuum removal]
– Exhaust air must be filtered to a high standard (HEPA filtration).

[p92 | prose | Direct vacuum removal]
– Have plans and procedures in place for if something goes wrong. For example, if the vacuum ducting gets blocked or there is a leak in the system:

[p92 | prose | Direct vacuum removal]
- securely cap both ends, draw it into the enclosure, open and clear the ducting

[p92 | prose | Direct vacuum removal]
- seal any openings immediately

[p92 | prose | Direct vacuum removal]
include these details in the ACMP.

[p92 | prose | Enhanced air management]
Enhanced air management

[p92 | prose | Enhanced air management]
Enhanced air movement is when air is introduced by a blower near the work area and extracted at a higher rate to maintain negative pressure. The blower, equipped with a manometer, automatically shuts off if the pressure drops too low.

[p92 | prose | Enhanced air management]
Enhanced air management can be used for any type of stripping work when wetting or other controlled techniques cannot be used. It can also be used as an additional control alongside other controlled stripping methods.

[p92 | prose | Enhanced air management]
Due to the high exposure risks when stripping dry, careful planning and close monitoring are essential. To effectively reduce exposure:

[p92 | prose | Enhanced air management]
– correctly position the push-pull air system to avoid dispersing asbestos fibres

[p92 | prose | Enhanced air management]
– the blower and extraction points should be positioned close to and opposite the work area to draw contaminated air away from workers

[p92 | prose | Enhanced air management]
– reposition the blower and extraction points as work progresses to make sure they stay close to the work area

[p92 | prose | Enhanced air management]
– workers should stay to the side of the blower unit.

[p92 | prose | Enhanced air management]
This complex system requires constant monitoring and is mainly used when other techniques are not suitable.

[p93 | prose | 11.6 Uncontrolled dry stripping]
11.6 Uncontrolled dry stripping

[p93 | prose | 11.6 Uncontrolled dry stripping]
Uncontrolled dry stripping is prohibited.

[p93 | prose | 11.6 Uncontrolled dry stripping]
Uncontrolled dry stripping refers to stripping ACM in its dry state inside an enclosure, without any direct controls in place to reduce exposure. Uncontrolled dry stripping can produce excessively high airborne asbestos fibre concentrations (up to 100–1000f/ml for sprayed coatings). Levels this high will result in significant asbestos exposure even when workers are wearing RPE. No RPE can provide adequate protection for these fibre levels. Air extraction within the enclosure will also not be able to control levels this high.

[p93 | prose | 11.6 Uncontrolled dry stripping]
Some form of control must always be used, such as direct control at source (for example, wet stripping or shadow vacuuming) or other specialised techniques, such as glove bags or enhanced air management.

[p93 | prose | 11.6 Uncontrolled dry stripping]
For more information, see Sections 11.3: Controlled wet stripping through to Section 11.5: Dry stripping with control at source

[p93 | prose | 11.7 Managing other risks during asbestos removal work]
11.7 Managing other risks during asbestos removal work

[p93 | prose | 11.7 Managing other risks during asbestos removal work]
In addition to the requirement to manage the risk of asbestos exposure, removalists must manage any other risks that may be present during asbestos removal work. For example:

[p93 | prose | 11.7 Managing other risks during asbestos removal work]
– working in extreme temperatures. See Working safely in extreme temperatures and Working on or near hot plant below

[p93 | prose | 11.7 Managing other risks during asbestos removal work]
– working at height. See Working at height and Working at height below

[p93 | prose | 11.7 Managing other risks during asbestos removal work]
– working in confined spaces. See Confined spaces: planning entry and working safely in a confined space and Working in confined spaces below

[p93 | prose | 11.7 Managing other risks during asbestos removal work]
– working near live electrical equipment or overhead lines. See Working near low voltage overhead electric lines

[p93 | prose | 11.7 Managing other risks during asbestos removal work]
– presence of chemicals or other substances hazardous to health. See Hazardous Substances

[p93 | prose | Working on or near hot plant]
Working on or near hot plant

[p93 | prose | Working on or near hot plant]
Wherever possible, all plant that cannot be removed from the enclosure should not be operational while removal is taking place on or near it. Working on or near operational plant can create the following risks:

[p93 | prose | Working on or near hot plant]
– Produce excessive heat within the enclosure, creating thermal risks for workers. For more information about the risks of working in extreme temperatures, see WorkSafe’s webpage Working safely in extreme temperatures

[p93 | prose | Working on or near hot plant]
– Produce combustion gases within the enclosure that will need to be vented without compromising the integrity of the enclosure.

[p93 | prose | Working on or near hot plant]
– May be incompatible with wet stripping techniques. For example, wetting of ACM insulation on hot pipes may not stay wetted long enough.

[p93 | prose | Working on or near hot plant]
– Temperatures may increase further as asbestos-containing insulation is removed from plant or pipework.

[p93 | prose | Working on or near hot plant]
– Excessive heat may soften polythene sheeting and weaken joints and seams of the enclosure. If the enclosure fails, strong warm air currents can escape causing significant contamination of the surrounding area.

[p93 | prose | Working on or near hot plant]
Plan with the commissioning PCBU to arrange for plant to be removed or turned off wherever possible. This may mean doing the asbestos removal work during hours when the plant is not required. This information should be included in the ACRP.

[p94 | prose | Working on or near hot plant]
Where work arises at short notice through incidents or emergencies and plant cannot be turned off at short notice, consider temporary repairs or encapsulation until the plant can be shut down and work carried out with the plant cold.

[p94 | prose | Working on or near hot plant]
If plant cannot be removed or turned off during removal work, and work cannot be delayed, put control measures in place to manage the risks.

[p94 | prose | Working on or near hot plant]
Table 26 shows control measures to use when plant must stay operating during asbestos removal.

[p94 | table | Working on or near hot plant]
| CONTROL MEASURE | DETAILS |
| --- | --- |
| Minimise heat sources | Operate equipment at minimum temperatures. For example: switch one boiler off, run the other at the lowest setting, and then reverse the arrangement. |
| Minimise heat spread | Use temporary insulation to cover exposed hot surfaces. Shield radiant surfaces with radiant heat barriers. |
| Make sure air is effectively managed inside the enclosure | Increase ventilation rates: = remove air at higher levels = make sure supply and extract vents provide a positive rate of air change at the working position draw make-up air from outside the heat-affected area where the air is cooler use air conditioning units to maintain cooler conditions. |
| Minimise the effects of excessive heat on workers | Rotate workers frequently Provide cooler rest areas Supply cool water Provide personal cooling vests For more information, see WorkSafe’s webpage Working safely in extreme temperatures |

[p94 | prose | Working on or near hot plant]
In addition, consider other sources or causes of excessive heat that may present

[p94 | prose | Working on or near hot plant]
a risk to workers while removing asbestos. For example:

[p94 | prose | Working on or near hot plant]
– working in direct sunlight

[p94 | prose | Working on or near hot plant]
– working in warmer/summer weather conditions

[p94 | prose | Working on or near hot plant]
– working in confined places

[p94 | prose | Working on or near hot plant]
– working while wearing heavy PPE/RPE

[p94 | prose | Working on or near hot plant]
– physically demanding removal work that can cause internal body temperatures to rise.

[p94 | prose | Working on or near hot plant]
For information on the effects of extreme heat on the body and suggested control measures, see Working safely in extreme temperatures

[p94 | prose | Working at height]
Working at height

[p94 | prose | Working at height]
Working at height means working in a place where a person could be injured if they fell from one level to another. This includes situations where there is a risk of falling from an elevated place, through an opening, or over an edge.

[p94 | prose | Working at height]
Preventing falls should be actively managed so that people working at height are kept safe.

[p94 | prose | Working at height]
For more information about managing risks of while working at height see Working at height

[p95 | prose | Working in confined spaces]
Working in confined spaces

[p95 | prose | Working in confined spaces]
A confined space is an enclosed or partially enclosed space that not intended or designed primarily for human occupancy.

[p95 | prose | Working in confined spaces]
Confined spaces may present a risk from one or more of the following at any time:

[p95 | prose | Working in confined spaces]
– unsafe concentration of harmful airborne contaminants (other substances as well as asbestos)

[p95 | prose | Working in confined spaces]
– unsafe concentration of flammable substances

[p95 | prose | Working in confined spaces]
– poor levels of oxygen

[p95 | prose | Working in confined spaces]
– substances that can cause engulfment.

[p95 | prose | Working in confined spaces]
Examples include: storage tanks, tank cars, process vessels, boilers, silos, pits, pipes, sewers, shafts, ducts and shipboard spaces.

[p95 | prose | Working in confined spaces]
Any removal work in a confined space must have the associated risks managed. For more information about managing risks of working in confined spaces see Confined spaces: planning entry and working safely in a confined space

[p95 | prose | 11.8 Removing asbestos waste from the enclosure]
11.8 Removing asbestos waste from the enclosure

[p95 | prose | 11.8 Removing asbestos waste from the enclosure]
Sealed waste bags and wrapped items must be decontaminated before they are removed from the enclosure. Decontamination should be done in the baglock as follows:

[p95 | prose | 11.8 Removing asbestos waste from the enclosure]
– Inner stage of the baglock: wipe down the bagged or wrapped waste. The bag/wrapped item should be clearly marked as containing asbestos waste. Once wiped down it can be moved to the middle stage of the baglock.

[p95 | prose | 11.8 Removing asbestos waste from the enclosure]
– Middle stage of the baglock: the bag should be placed in a second bag (usually transparent), sealed, and wiped down. The waste can then be taken to the outer stage of the baglock

[p95 | prose | 11.8 Removing asbestos waste from the enclosure]
– Outer stage of the baglock: the waste should be collected from the outer stage by an outside worker and transferred to the waste skip.

[p95 | prose | 11.8 Removing asbestos waste from the enclosure]
If there is no worker available for this, then the person putting the waste through the baglock will have to carry out preliminary decontamination before taking the waste to the skip or vehicle. For more information on personal decontamination, see Section 15.0: Personal decontamination

[p95 | prose | 11.8 Removing asbestos waste from the enclosure]
Where there is not enough space for a dedicated three-stage baglock, the decontamination should be carried out in an alternative baglock arrangement. For more information, see Airlocks and baglocks in Section 9.7 Enclosure access

[p95 | prose | 11.8 Removing asbestos waste from the enclosure]
Once the decontaminated waste package is outside the baglock it should not be left unattended:

[p95 | prose | 11.8 Removing asbestos waste from the enclosure]
– Have a worker based on the outside of the enclosure take the waste to the skip or vehicle being used for disposal. Make sure this worker is wearing appropriate PPE.

[p95 | prose | 11.8 Removing asbestos waste from the enclosure]
– This worker should always remain on the outside of the enclosure to avoid having to decontaminate.

[p95 | prose | 11.8 Removing asbestos waste from the enclosure]
For information about the next steps in disposing of asbestos waste, see Section 14.0: Containment and disposal of asbestos waste

[p96 | prose | 11.9 Site cleaning and preparation for four-stage clearance]
11.9 Site cleaning and preparation for four-stage clearance

[p96 | prose | 11.9 Site cleaning and preparation for four-stage clearance]
Use work methods and equipment which will prevent or minimise the build-up of asbestos debris and waste in the work area. This will make cleaning up easier.

[p96 | prose | 11.9 Site cleaning and preparation for four-stage clearance]
– Remove all asbestos waste from the work area without creating additional risks of spreading it.

[p96 | prose | 11.9 Site cleaning and preparation for four-stage clearance]
– ACM should be placed directly into waste bags as it is removed.

[p96 | prose | 11.9 Site cleaning and preparation for four-stage clearance]
– Where wet removal techniques have been used – place waste into bags before it starts to dry out (or keep residue damp until it can be bagged).

[p96 | prose | 11.9 Site cleaning and preparation for four-stage clearance]
– Clean throughout the work period and make sure the area is clean at the end of each shift.

[p96 | prose | Cleaning methods]
Cleaning methods

[p96 | prose | Cleaning methods]
Cleaning methods should not create dust.

[p96 | prose | Cleaning methods]
– Do not use brushes or brooms.

[p96 | prose | Cleaning methods]
– Pieces of debris can be cleaned up using rakes and shovels. Damp cloths and wipes should be used for additional cleaning of surfaces.

[p96 | prose | Cleaning methods]
– Dust and debris should be dampened down as necessary before cleaning.

[p96 | prose | Cleaning methods]
– Use Type H vacuum cleaners which are designed for asbestos (Figure 24).

[p96 | prose | Cleaning methods]
- Type H vacuum cleaners are fitted with HEPA filters and are designed for dust-free disposal.

[p96 | prose | Cleaning methods]
- Do not use domestic or general-purpose vacuum cleaners (even those with HEPA filters)

[p96 | prose | Cleaning methods]
- Do not use type H vacuum cleaners on very wet material. Excessive liquid will damage the HEPA filter. Instead use vacuum cleaners suitable for wet surfaces. The vacuum cleaner will need immediate cleaning and decontamination after use, including removing and disposing of the filter. For more information see Section 12.5 Industrial vacuum cleaners

[p96 | prose | Cleaning methods]
FIGURE 26:

[p96 | prose | Cleaning methods]
Type H vacuum cleaner

[p97 | prose | Cleaning stages]
Cleaning stages

[p97 | prose | Cleaning stages]
Clear the enclosure (including airlocks and baglocks) of all asbestos waste and other non-essential items (such as tools and equipment). The only remaining items should be:

[p97 | prose | Cleaning stages]
– any wrapped waste that could not be removed through the baglock system

[p97 | prose | Cleaning stages]
– a type H vacuum cleaner, wipes, and waste bags which may be needed for any additional cleaning as directed by the assessor

[p97 | prose | Cleaning stages]
– any equipment the assessor may need to perform the four-stage clearance procedure (such as a ladder)

[p97 | prose | Cleaning stages]
– a type H vacuum cleaner and buckets for decontamination, which should be left in or near the airlock.

[p97 | prose | Cleaning stages]
Once the area is cleared, it can be cleaned one last time to prepare for the four- stage clearance procedure. Final cleaning should include the following steps:

[p97 | prose | Cleaning stages]
– Replace pre-filters on the air extraction equipment with new filters.

[p97 | prose | Cleaning stages]
– Thoroughly clean the whole work area including all surfaces and items using dustless methods, including:

[p97 | prose | Cleaning stages]
- floors

[p97 | prose | Cleaning stages]
- walls

[p97 | prose | Cleaning stages]
- all sides of sheeted items

[p97 | prose | Cleaning stages]
- high-level surfaces

[p97 | prose | Cleaning stages]
- pipes

[p97 | prose | Cleaning stages]
- ductwork

[p97 | prose | Cleaning stages]
- cables

[p97 | prose | Cleaning stages]
- undersides of items (pipes, ledges)

[p97 | prose | Cleaning stages]
- behind and below plant

[p97 | prose | Cleaning stages]
- equipment and other furnishings and fittings.

[p97 | prose | Cleaning stages]
– Use a type H vacuum cleaner for the initial clean.

[p97 | prose | Cleaning stages]
– Surfaces should then be wiped as necessary with damp cloths or wipes to make sure all fine settled dust has been removed.

[p97 | prose | Cleaning stages]
– Remove sheeting or boarding which has been used to protect any equipment, plant, items, or other surfaces (including sacrificial flooring)

[p97 | prose | Cleaning stages]
– Consider spraying sheeting with an adhesive sealant, such as PVA, before removal to reduce the potential for release of residual fine settled dust. But make sure sealant does not spread onto other surfaces which would inhibit the issuing of the clearance certificate. PVA should have be a 1:5 solution mixed with water.

[p97 | prose | Cleaning stages]
– After protective sheeting has been removed, check the underlying surfaces for any dust or debris which may have penetrated or settled on to them. Clean these as appropriate.

[p97 | prose | Cleaning stages]
– Allow enough time for the work area to completely dry out before the four-stage clearance procedure is carried out.

[p97 | prose | Cleaning stages]
– Finally, carry out a thorough visual and fingertip inspection to make sure that there has been complete removal of the ACM as planned and that the work area has been properly cleaned of visible debris and fine settled dust.

[p97 | prose | Cleaning stages]
The removalist should complete a handover document to confirm the area has been checked and is clean. See Appendix 5: Asbestos removal area handover form

[p97 | prose | Cleaning stages]
If the handover document is missing, incomplete, or there is any doubt about the site cleanliness, the assessor can refuse to start the clearance inspection.

[p98 | prose | Cleaning stages]
Final cleaning before Class A clearance

[p98 | prose | 11.10 Approval of methods for managing risks associated with asbestos]
11.10 Approval of methods for managing risks associated with asbestos

[p98 | prose | 11.10 Approval of methods for managing risks associated with asbestos]
WorkSafe-approved methods can allow for work involving asbestos to be carried out that would otherwise be prohibited by the asbestos regulations.

[p98 | prose | 11.10 Approval of methods for managing risks associated with asbestos]
This can apply to situations where existing approved methods for managing asbestos (including its removal) cannot be followed. PCBUs cannot use an alternative method until it has been approved by WorkSafe.

[p98 | prose | 11.10 Approval of methods for managing risks associated with asbestos]
For more information on the criteria and how to apply for an approved method, see WorkSafe’s webpages:

[p98 | prose | 11.10 Approval of methods for managing risks associated with asbestos]
– Asbestos approved methods

[p98 | prose | 11.10 Approval of methods for managing risks associated with asbestos]
– How we approve methods for working with asbestos

[p100 | prose | Select the appropriate tools and equipment to minimise airborne asbestos fibres.]
Select the appropriate tools and equipment to minimise airborne asbestos fibres.

[p100 | prose | 12.1 Selecting the right tools and equipment]
12.1 Selecting the right tools and equipment

[p100 | prose | 12.1 Selecting the right tools and equipment]
It is important to select the correct tools and equipment for any work on asbestos material.

[p100 | prose | 12.1 Selecting the right tools and equipment]
Some tools and equipment are prohibited for use on asbestos material because they increase the chances of asbestos fibres becoming airborne.

[p100 | prose | 12.1 Selecting the right tools and equipment]
Certain tools and equipment that generate dust can be used under strict controls. These are called controlled tools and equipment.

[p100 | prose | 12.2 Prohibited tools and equipment]
12.2 Prohibited tools and equipment

[p100 | prose | 12.2 Prohibited tools and equipment]
The following equipment must not be used on asbestos or ACM, and a PCBU must not direct or allow a worker to use it.

[p100 | prose | 12.2 Prohibited tools and equipment]
High-pressure water spray

[p100 | prose | 12.2 Prohibited tools and equipment]
This means water pressurised by positive displacement pumps that have an output capability of more than 350kPa.

[p100 | prose | 12.2 Prohibited tools and equipment]
Using high-pressure water spray on asbestos, ACM, or asbestos-contaminated dust is prohibited because it increases the chances of asbestos fibres becoming airborne.

[p100 | prose | 12.2 Prohibited tools and equipment]
High-pressure water spray and compressed air must not be used on asbestos material, except in the following situations:

[p100 | prose | 12.2 Prohibited tools and equipment]
– for fire-fighting or fire prevention purposes

[p100 | prose | 12.2 Prohibited tools and equipment]
– water jetting to clear or prevent blockages in wastewater or water pipe networks

[p100 | prose | 12.2 Prohibited tools and equipment]
– specific instances of the use of a relevant method for managing risk associated with asbestos that is approved.

[p100 | prose | Compressed air]
Compressed air

[p100 | prose | Compressed air]
Using compressed air on asbestos material is prohibited because it significantly increases the risk of asbestos fibres becoming airborne.

[p100 | prose | 12.3 Controlled tools and equipment]
12.3 Controlled tools and equipment

[p100 | prose | 12.3 Controlled tools and equipment]
Power tools and other equipment (including angle grinders, sanders, saws, drills, brushes, and brooms) may be used only under controlled conditions. For example, if the equipment is:

[p100 | prose | 12.3 Controlled tools and equipment]
– enclosed while being used (such as within an enclosure with mechanical ventilation operating)

[p100 | prose | 12.3 Controlled tools and equipment]
– designed to capture or suppress airborne asbestos and is used according to its design

[p101 | prose | 12.3 Controlled tools and equipment]
– used in a way designed to capture or suppress airborne asbestos safely, such as through engineering control measures like dust suppression or extraction ventilation

[p101 | prose | 12.3 Controlled tools and equipment]
– a combination of the above.

[p101 | prose | 12.3 Controlled tools and equipment]
Use manually-operated (non-powered) hand tools wherever possible. If this is not possible, use low-speed, battery-powered tools that can be used with wet methods for dust control.

[p101 | prose | 12.3 Controlled tools and equipment]
Fit battery-powered tools with local exhaust ventilation (LEV) dust control hoods wherever possible. If LEV cannot be attached and other dust control methods are unsuitable, the asbestos worker should use shadow vacuuming techniques (Section 11.3: Controlled wetting by injection).

[p101 | prose | 12.3 Controlled tools and equipment]
When using power tools with dust suppression or extraction, air monitoring should be carried out to ensure the controls in place are effective in reducing the release of fibres.

[p101 | prose | 12.3 Controlled tools and equipment]
For more information, see Section 10.0: Air monitoring

[p101 | prose | 12.4 Equipment inspection and maintenance]
12.4 Equipment inspection and maintenance

[p101 | prose | 12.4 Equipment inspection and maintenance]
Inspect all equipment used for removing asbestos:

[p101 | prose | 12.4 Equipment inspection and maintenance]
– before work involving asbestos starts

[p101 | prose | 12.4 Equipment inspection and maintenance]
– after any repairs

[p101 | prose | 12.4 Equipment inspection and maintenance]
– at least once every seven days when it is continually used.

[p101 | prose | 12.4 Equipment inspection and maintenance]
PCBUs should keep records with details of these inspections, the state of the equipment and any repairs made.

[p101 | prose | 12.4 Equipment inspection and maintenance]
Never take anything that could have asbestos on it out of the work area – not even during the job. Tools must be either:

[p101 | prose | 12.4 Equipment inspection and maintenance]
– decontaminated after finishing work involving asbestos. The PCBU must not allow anything to leave the enclosure if it is likely to be contaminated.

[p101 | prose | 12.4 Equipment inspection and maintenance]
– sealed in a container. Before removing the container, decontaminate the outside and label it to show it contains asbestos.

[p101 | prose | 12.4 Equipment inspection and maintenance]
See Section 16: Decontamination of tools, vehicles, machinery or mobile plant for more information.

[p101 | prose | 12.5 Industrial vacuum cleaners]
12.5 Industrial vacuum cleaners

[p101 | prose | 12.5 Industrial vacuum cleaners]
Vacuum cleaners used for hazardous dusts, such as asbestos, must be fit for purpose to make sure the dust is safely captured and contained.

[p101 | prose | 12.5 Industrial vacuum cleaners]
Domestic or standard commercial vacuums are not suitable for this purpose, regardless of the filter used.

[p101 | prose | 12.5 Industrial vacuum cleaners]
Industrial vacuums rated for use with hazardous dusts are classified as L, M, or H, which refer to the hazard rating: light, medium, or high hazard.

[p101 | prose | 12.5 Industrial vacuum cleaners]
Asbestos is classified as a high-risk material, so only H-class industrial vacuums should be used with for work involving asbestos material (including asbestos-contaminated dust).

[p101 | prose | 12.5 Industrial vacuum cleaners]
High-efficiency particulate air (HEPA) does not mean H-class.

[p102 | prose | 12.6 Vacuum cleaner filters]
12.6 Vacuum cleaner filters

[p102 | prose | 12.6 Vacuum cleaner filters]
Filters used in vacuum cleaners for hazardous dusts must:

[p102 | prose | 12.6 Vacuum cleaner filters]
– be designed to fit the specific model of the vacuum cleaner being used

[p102 | prose | 12.6 Vacuum cleaner filters]
– achieve the same or higher filtration efficiency that the vacuum cleaner is rated for.

[p102 | prose | 12.6 Vacuum cleaner filters]
Class H vacuum cleaners used for asbestos work should not be used on wet materials or surfaces unless designed for that type of work.

[p102 | prose | 12.6 Vacuum cleaner filters]
For more information on selecting the right industrial vacuum or filter or maintaining a vacuum, see WorkSafe’s webpage Industrial vacuums and portable extractors for hazardous dust

[p102 | prose | 12.7 Maintaining vacuum cleaners]
12.7 Maintaining vacuum cleaners

[p102 | prose | 12.7 Maintaining vacuum cleaners]
When vacuum cleaners have been used with asbestos, removal and disposal of the contents must be performed by a competent person. Vacuum cleaners should be maintained according to the manufacturer’s guidance.

[p102 | prose | Competent person]
Competent person

[p102 | prose | Competent person]
A competent person is someone who has the appropriate skills, training, knowledge, and experience to perform the task or role.

[p102 | prose | The competent person should:]
The competent person should:

[p102 | prose | The competent person should:]
– wear personal protective equipment (PPE), including appropriate respiratory protective equipment (RPE) - which has been fit-tested

[p102 | prose | The competent person should:]
– seal or cordon off an appropriate area to prevent unnecessary dust exposure to others or carry out the operation in an enclosure

[p102 | prose | The competent person should:]
– make sure the dust bag has been removed and disposed of first

[p102 | prose | The competent person should:]
– use a damp cloth to clean the dust off the outside of the vacuum, and any inside parts that are accessible

[p102 | prose | The competent person should:]
– dispose of dust and containment bags and contaminated damp cloths as asbestos waste in tightly sealed and labelled bags or containers

[p102 | prose | The competent person should:]
– only dispose of asbestos waste at authorised asbestos disposal sites.

[p102 | prose | The competent person should:]
For a more thorough clean, use another industrial vacuum cleaner. Only use this method if the other vacuum cleaner is rated at the same class, or higher.

[p102 | prose | The competent person should:]
Dry brushing or using compressed air should never be used to clean vacuums. These methods cause the hazardous dust to spread and become airborne. Compressed air can also damage the filters, making them ineffective.

[p102 | prose | 12.8 Transporting vacuum cleaners]
12.8 Transporting vacuum cleaners

[p102 | prose | 12.8 Transporting vacuum cleaners]
Vacuum cleaners used for asbestos work must be treated as asbestos waste during transportation. This means double bagging the vacuum cleaners and any hoses and attachments in suitable asbestos waste bags, clearly identified as containing asbestos.

[p102 | prose | 12.8 Transporting vacuum cleaners]
For more information, see Section 14.0: Containment and disposal of asbestos waste

[p104 | prose | 12.8 Transporting vacuum cleaners]
Clearance inspection is carried out after licensed asbestos removal work to make sure the premises are safe to reoccupy.

[p104 | prose | 12.8 Transporting vacuum cleaners]
This section provides information on clearance inspections relevant to asbestos removalists. More detailed information about the clearance process is covered in the Asbestos assessments – good practice guidelines

[p104 | prose | 13.1 What are clearance inspections and who can do them?]
13.1 What are clearance inspections and who can do them?

[p104 | prose | 13.1 What are clearance inspections and who can do them?]
A clearance certificate is required before the area can be reoccupied for demolition, work, or normal use.

[p104 | prose | 13.1 What are clearance inspections and who can do them?]
The person who commissioned the asbestos removal work at a workplace must make sure a clearance inspection of the asbestos removal area is completed, and a clearance certificate is issued. For removal work in residential homes the removalist must make sure the clearance inspection is completed.

[p104 | prose | 13.1 What are clearance inspections and who can do them?]
Unauthorised people must not enter the asbestos area before the clearance certificate is issued. Signage and barriers must remain in place until then.

[p104 | prose | 13.1 What are clearance inspections and who can do them?]
Clearance inspections can be carried out by certain people, as shown in Table 27.

[p104 | prose | 13.1 What are clearance inspections and who can do them?]
CLASS A ASBESTOS REMOVAL WORK

[p104 | prose | 13.1 What are clearance inspections and who can do them?]
CLASS B AND UNLICENSED ASBESTOS REMOVAL WORK

[p104 | prose | 13.1 What are clearance inspections and who can do them?]
– an independent licensed asbestos assessor

[p104 | prose | 13.1 What are clearance inspections and who can do them?]
– an independent licensed asbestos assessor

[p104 | prose | 13.1 What are clearance inspections and who can do them?]
– an independent competent person

[p104 | prose | 13.1 What are clearance inspections and who can do them?]
TABLE 27: Who can carry out clearance inspections

[p104 | prose | 13.1 What are clearance inspections and who can do them?]
For detailed information on what makes an assessor or a competent person independent, see Section 2.4: Independence and conflicts of interest in the Asbestos assessments – good practice guidelines

[p104 | prose | 13.1 What are clearance inspections and who can do them?]
A competent person is a person who has relevant industry experience, skills, and knowledge. They must also hold either of the following qualifications:

[p104 | prose | 13.1 What are clearance inspections and who can do them?]
– a certificate from a training course, specified by WorkSafe for asbestos assessor work

[p104 | prose | 13.1 What are clearance inspections and who can do them?]
– a tertiary qualification in occupational health and safety, occupational hygiene, science, or environmental health.

[p104 | prose | 13.1 What are clearance inspections and who can do them?]
For more detailed information see Section 6.0: Asbestos assessments – good practice guidelines

[p105 | prose | 13.2 How to find a licensed asbestos assessor]
13.2 How to find a licensed asbestos assessor

[p105 | prose | 13.2 How to find a licensed asbestos assessor]
The register of licensed asbestos assessors is on the WorkSafe website Licence holder register

[p105 | prose | 13.3 What does the inspection process involve?]
13.3 What does the inspection process involve?

[p105 | prose | 13.3 What does the inspection process involve?]
There are four stages to the clearance inspection process. The assessor and removalist have actions to take at each step.

[p105 | prose | 13.3 What does the inspection process involve?]
Table 28 summarises these steps.

[p105 | table | 13.3 What does the inspection process involve?]
| STAGE | THE ASSESSOR WILL | THE REMOVALIST WILL |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Stage 1 Preliminary check of site condition and job completeness | Inspect the removal control plan Check the decontamination facilities are still intact, operational and clean Check for signs of contamination, leaks, burst waste bags or debris Check the enclosure’s integrity Check transit and waste routes | Fix any breaches Clean and remove any debris found by the assessor |
| Stage 2: Visual inspection inside the enclosure or work area | Check asbestos/ACM removal including underlying surfaces Check for any visible ACD left inside the enclosure, airlocks, or work area Check for any fine settled dust | Make sure the removal area has been cleaned and dried before the inspection Accompany the assessor to correct minor problems Clear any debris or dust if they are found during the inspection |
| Stage 3: Air sampling | Class A removal: Conduct surface testing by surface disturbance, then conduct air sampling immediately after. Class B removal: conduct air sampling and/ or surface testing by surface disturbance, if the results of the visual inspection determine it is necessary | Make sure NPUs are turned off and capped while clearance air sampling is being undertaken |
| Stage 4: Final assessment of work area and any transiting area after enclosure dismantling | - Check for any residual contamination following dismantling of the enclosure - Check transit and waste routes | - Dismantle the enclosure when the nclosure or work area has passed the visual inspection and/or air monitoring |

[p105 | prose | 13.3 What does the inspection process involve?]
TABLE 28: Actions for each step of the four-stage inspection clearance process

[p105 | prose | 13.4 Surface testing]
13.4 Surface testing

[p105 | prose | 13.4 Surface testing]
Surface testing is a planned and controlled disturbance of enclosure surfaces immediately prior to clearance air monitoring.

[p105 | prose | 13.4 Surface testing]
Surface testing is mandatory for Class A asbestos clearance inspections and optional for Class B asbestos clearance inspections undertaken following completion of the asbestos removal work.

[p105 | prose | 13.4 Surface testing]
Surface testing by surface disturbance followed by air monitoring is carried out during Stage 3 of the clearance inspection by the assessor.

[p105 | prose | 13.4 Surface testing]
Data from surface testing by surface disturbance and air monitoring helps assessors decide whether the area is safe to reoccupy.

[p105 | prose | 13.4 Surface testing]
For more information on surface testing, see the Asbestos assessments – good practice guidelines

[p106 | prose | 13.5 Air sampling]
13.5 Air sampling

[p106 | prose | 13.5 Air sampling]
Air sampling during clearance inspections is mandatory for Class A asbestos removal work (unless there was no enclosure used and the licensed assessor determines it is not feasible), and optional for Class B asbestos removal work. For more information, see the Asbestos assessments – good practice guidelines

[p106 | prose | 13.5 Air sampling]
Air sampling during clearance (viewed through a vision panel)

[p106 | prose | 13.6 Wet enclosures or work areas]
13.6 Wet enclosures or work areas

[p106 | prose | 13.6 Wet enclosures or work areas]
Where practicable, the enclosure or work area should be clean and dry before the inspection begins. It may be hazardous for the assessor to conduct their inspections in a wet environment. It is also harder to inspect for the presence of fine dust.

[p106 | prose | 13.7 Using sealant]
13.7 Using sealant

[p106 | prose | 13.7 Using sealant]
Sealants, including PVA, may be used in limited circumstances. For example, where the enclosure also contains significant amounts of non-asbestos (for example, brick or wood) dust that may interfere with the air monitoring readings. Where PVA is used, it should be mixed 1:5 with water.

[p106 | prose | 13.7 Using sealant]
It should not be used to replace the need for thoroughly cleaning the enclosure.

[p106 | prose | 13.7 Using sealant]
Sealants should not be applied inside the enclosure before the visual inspection begins.

[p106 | prose | 13.7 Using sealant]
If an assessor finds a sealant-sprayed enclosure, the asbestos removalist may be instructed to clean and dry the enclosure before the visual inspection starts.

[p107 | prose | 13.7 Using sealant]
If the removalist is considering applying sealant before the visual inspection stage begins, they should seek advice from the assessor first.

[p107 | prose | 13.7 Using sealant]
If the removalist has used a sealant in the asbestos removal area, it should notify the client and advise them that the sealant may break down over time.

[p107 | prose | 13.8 Clearance certificates]
13.8 Clearance certificates

[p107 | prose | 13.8 Clearance certificates]
A clearance certificate will be issued if the asbestos assessor’s testing confirms that the asbestos removal area no longer poses a risk to health and safety from asbestos exposure.

[p107 | prose | 13.8 Clearance certificates]
The area must not be reoccupied until the PCBU who commissioned the work, or the removalist (when the work is at a residential home) has obtained a clearance certificate for the asbestos removal area. This includes reoccupation for demolition, other work activities or normal use.

[p107 | prose | 13.8 Clearance certificates]
The clearance certificate must be issued by the assessor electronically or in a hard copy. It must contain the following:

[p107 | prose | 13.8 Clearance certificates]
– the assessor’s name, qualifications, and contact details

[p107 | prose | 13.8 Clearance certificates]
– the address and location of the asbestos removal area – the date and time of the inspection

[p107 | prose | 13.8 Clearance certificates]
– confirmation that no visible asbestos residue remains

[p107 | prose | 13.8 Clearance certificates]
– if applicable, confirmation that air monitoring results are below trace level.

[p107 | prose | 13.8 Clearance certificates]
The commissioning PCBU, or the removalist (when the work is at a residential home) must not accept an assessor’s verbal confirmation, text message or email confirming clearance inspection completion instead of the clearance certificate.

[p107 | prose | 13.8 Clearance certificates]
A PCBU must make sure, so far as is reasonably practicable, that only people involved in the asbestos removal work or authorised people, can enter the asbestos removal area before the clearance certificate is issued. Signs and barriers must remain in place until the clearance certificate is issued.

[p107 | prose | 13.8 Clearance certificates]
For more detailed information about the clearance process, see Section 7.0: Decontamination of the Asbestos assessments – good practice guidelines

[p109 | prose | Correctly containing and disposing of asbestos waste reduces the risk of asbestos fibre exposure.]
Correctly containing and disposing of asbestos waste reduces the risk of asbestos fibre exposure.

[p109 | prose | 14.1 Proper handling, containment, transport, and disposal of asbestos waste]
14.1 Proper handling, containment, transport, and disposal of asbestos waste

[p109 | prose | 14.1 Proper handling, containment, transport, and disposal of asbestos waste]
Proper handling, containment, transport, and disposal of asbestos waste can help to minimise the risk of workers and others being exposed to asbestos fibres.

[p109 | prose | 14.1 Proper handling, containment, transport, and disposal of asbestos waste]
As well as all the asbestos material removed during the removal process, the following items should also be treated as asbestos waste:

[p109 | prose | 14.1 Proper handling, containment, transport, and disposal of asbestos waste]
– all enclosure building materials (such as timber and sheeting)

[p109 | prose | 14.1 Proper handling, containment, transport, and disposal of asbestos waste]
– any items that have been present (and unprotected) inside contaminated areas that cannot or will not be cleaned (including tools and equipment)

[p109 | prose | 14.1 Proper handling, containment, transport, and disposal of asbestos waste]
– all disposable PPE used in the enclosure, transit and waste routes and in the DCU.

[p109 | prose | 14.1 Proper handling, containment, transport, and disposal of asbestos waste]
– any disposable or discarded items used in cleaning and decontamination such as cloths, wipes and towels.

[p109 | prose | 14.1 Proper handling, containment, transport, and disposal of asbestos waste]
Wastewater from buckets in airlocks should be disposed of through the filtered drainage system in the shower of the DCU.

[p109 | prose | 14.1 Proper handling, containment, transport, and disposal of asbestos waste]
The asbestos removalist must:

[p109 | prose | 14.1 Proper handling, containment, transport, and disposal of asbestos waste]
– make sure asbestos waste is properly contained and marked clearly to indicate the presence of asbestos, before removing it from the work area

[p109 | prose | 14.1 Proper handling, containment, transport, and disposal of asbestos waste]
– make sure asbestos waste awaiting disposal is stored in sealed containers such as drums with removable lids, or a sealed skip (lockable to prevent any tampering)

[p109 | prose | 14.1 Proper handling, containment, transport, and disposal of asbestos waste]
– dispose of waste as soon as is reasonably practicable.

[p109 | prose | 14.1 Proper handling, containment, transport, and disposal of asbestos waste]
Asbestos removed as unlicensed removal of non-friable asbestos under 10m2 should still be wrapped and disposed of as explained in this section.

[p109 | prose | 14.2 Double bagging asbestos waste]
14.2 Double bagging asbestos waste

[p109 | prose | 14.2 Double bagging asbestos waste]
Double bag friable asbestos and small pieces of non-friable asbestos in new, clearly labelled heavy-duty, robust and tear resilient/high tensile strength plastic bags to minimise the risk of release of asbestos fibres.

[p109 | prose | 14.2 Double bagging asbestos waste]
Consider using a coloured internal bag (such as red) and a clear/see-through bag for the outer bag. This makes it easy to see that the waste has been double bagged.

[p110 | prose | 14.2 Double bagging asbestos waste]
To double bag asbestos waste:

[p110 | prose | 14.2 Double bagging asbestos waste]
– only fill the bag halfway with waste to minimise the risk of tearing or splitting and to leave a long neck on the bag

[p110 | prose | 14.2 Double bagging asbestos waste]
– lightly spray the contents of the bag with a water and detergent mix

[p110 | prose | 14.2 Double bagging asbestos waste]
– gently squeeze any excess air out

[p110 | prose | 14.2 Double bagging asbestos waste]
– twist the neck of the bag tightly, and seal with duct tape close to the base of the neck to fully enclose the contents

[p110 | prose | 14.2 Double bagging asbestos waste]
– clean the external surface of the bag with a wet wipe to remove any potential contamination

[p110 | prose | 14.2 Double bagging asbestos waste]
– place the used wet wipe in the upper neck of the bag (creating a pouch) then seal the neck of the bag above the wet wipe pouch

[p110 | prose | 14.2 Double bagging asbestos waste]
– fold the neck over and seal it again near the base of the neck – trapping the pouch in the middle (creating a ‘goose-neck twist’). See Figure 29

[p110 | prose | 14.2 Double bagging asbestos waste]
– place the bag inside a second (outer) bag and seal it with a ‘goose-neck’ twist

[p110 | prose | 14.2 Double bagging asbestos waste]
– clearly mark the outer bag with Caution: Asbestos – Do not open or damage bag.

[p110 | prose | 14.2 Double bagging asbestos waste]
See Disposing of asbestos waste and PPE safely for a video that demonstrates sealing a waste bag with a goose-neck twist.

[p110 | prose | FIGURE 29:]
FIGURE 29:

[p110 | prose | FIGURE 29:]
Asbestos waste double bagged and sealed with a goose-neck twist

[p110 | prose | 14.3 Polythene sheeting]
14.3 Polythene sheeting

[p110 | prose | 14.3 Polythene sheeting]
Large items that do not fit into asbestos waste bags, such as asbestos sheeting, asbestos-lagged pipes and similar long or large items should be double-wrapped in heavy-duty (minimum thickness of 200–500 m) polythene sheeting.

[p110 | prose | 14.3 Polythene sheeting]
– Avoid using recycled polythene sheeting, as it may have rips or flaws.

[p110 | prose | 14.3 Polythene sheeting]
– Apply adhesive tape to the entire length of every overlap to minimise the risk of tearing or splitting.

[p110 | prose | 14.3 Polythene sheeting]
– Clean the external surface of the sheeting to remove any dust before removing it from the asbestos work area.

[p110 | prose | 14.3 Polythene sheeting]
– Clearly mark the outer wrapping with Caution: Asbestos – Do not open or damage bag. Do not inhale dust.

[p110 | prose | 14.4 Asbestos waste drums or bins]
14.4 Asbestos waste drums or bins

[p110 | prose | 14.4 Asbestos waste drums or bins]
Drums or bins used for storing and disposing of wrapped asbestos waste should be well-maintained, with lids and rims in good working order.

[p110 | prose | 14.4 Asbestos waste drums or bins]
Drums or bins used to store or dispose of asbestos waste should:

[p110 | prose | 14.4 Asbestos waste drums or bins]
– be placed in or near the asbestos work area before starting work

[p110 | prose | 14.4 Asbestos waste drums or bins]
– be lined with heavy-duty polythene

[p110 | prose | 14.4 Asbestos waste drums or bins]
– be clearly marked to indicate the presence of asbestos

[p110 | prose | 14.4 Asbestos waste drums or bins]
– be wetted down while being filled to minimise the release of asbestos fibres into the air

[p110 | prose | 14.4 Asbestos waste drums or bins]
– have rims sealed and outer surfaces cleaned before removal from the work area.

[p110 | prose | 14.4 Asbestos waste drums or bins]
Store asbestos waste drums or bins in a secure location when not in use.

[p110 | prose | 14.4 Asbestos waste drums or bins]
Use trolleys or drum lifters to move them manually once filled.

[p110 | prose | 14.4 Asbestos waste drums or bins]
If a drum or bin is to be reused, pack and seal the existing asbestos waste to minimise the risk of residual contamination. Inspect each drum or bin after use to make sure it is free of asbestos residue.

[p111 | prose | 14.4 Asbestos waste drums or bins]
Purpose-made heavy-duty asbestos waste bags can also be used. They usually contain a zippered top, ensuring a tighter seal and are available in various sizes. Make sure they meet relevant New Zealand standards, follow the manufacturer’s instructions before using and make sure the waste is double bagged or wrapped in polythene before loading it into heavy duty asbestos waste bags.

[p111 | prose | FIGURE 30:]
FIGURE 30:

[p111 | prose | FIGURE 30:]
Examples of asbestos waste being removed

[p111 | prose | 14.5 Asbestos skips, vehicle trays, and similar containers]
14.5 Asbestos skips, vehicle trays, and similar containers

[p111 | prose | 14.5 Asbestos skips, vehicle trays, and similar containers]
If asbestos waste is too large for bags, drums or bins, use skips, vehicle trays or similar containers. Make sure they are in good condition.

[p111 | prose | 14.5 Asbestos skips, vehicle trays, and similar containers]
If using a skip, vehicle tray, or another similar container to dispose of asbestos waste, make sure to:

[p111 | prose | 14.5 Asbestos skips, vehicle trays, and similar containers]
– line the container with polythene before loading with waste

[p111 | prose | 14.5 Asbestos skips, vehicle trays, and similar containers]
– locate the container as close as possible to the enclosure to reduce the risk of the spread of contamination and the potential for musculoskeletal injuries while carrying heavy bags or wrapped items

[p111 | prose | 14.5 Asbestos skips, vehicle trays, and similar containers]
– keep the container well away from sensitive areas, such as in the middle of a school playground or near the entrance to a public building

[p111 | prose | 14.5 Asbestos skips, vehicle trays, and similar containers]
– mark out the transit route to be taken from the enclosure to the container and make sure waste is only taken along this route. This route will need to be checked during the clearance process

[p112 | prose | 14.5 Asbestos skips, vehicle trays, and similar containers]
– double wrap asbestos waste in heavy-duty plastic sheeting (or double bag the waste) before placing it in the container (see Section 14.2: Double bagging asbestos waste and Section 14.3: Polythene sheeting)

[p112 | prose | 14.5 Asbestos skips, vehicle trays, and similar containers]
– carefully place bagged or wrapped items into the container. Do not throw items into the container

[p112 | prose | 14.5 Asbestos skips, vehicle trays, and similar containers]
– seal the contents of the container completely with plastic sheeting when it is full and have a procedure to prevent the plastic sheeting from tearing when the container is emptied

[p112 | prose | 14.5 Asbestos skips, vehicle trays, and similar containers]
– secure the contents of the container to prevent unauthorised access until the waste is disposed of (for example, using a lockable lid or placing the container in a secure area)

[p112 | prose | 14.5 Asbestos skips, vehicle trays, and similar containers]
All drums and bins containing asbestos must be sealed and clearly marked to indicate the presence of asbestos before removal from the asbestos work area.

[p112 | prose | 14.6 Removing asbestos waste from the asbestos removal area]
14.6 Removing asbestos waste from the asbestos removal area

[p112 | prose | 14.6 Removing asbestos waste from the asbestos removal area]
Once waste has been removed from the asbestos work area, it needs to either be:

[p112 | prose | 14.6 Removing asbestos waste from the asbestos removal area]
– placed in an appropriate container for secure storage and eventual disposal

[p112 | prose | 14.6 Removing asbestos waste from the asbestos removal area]
– immediately removed from the site for transport to the disposal site.

[p112 | prose | 14.7 Storing asbestos waste]
14.7 Storing asbestos waste

[p112 | prose | 14.7 Storing asbestos waste]
Asbestos waste can only be stored under the following circumstances:

[p112 | prose | 14.7 Storing asbestos waste]
– on site in a secured skip or locked vehicle

[p112 | prose | 14.7 Storing asbestos waste]
– at a waste management facility.

[p112 | prose | 14.7 Storing asbestos waste]
For small amounts of waste (within unlicensed removal limits) it is acceptable to place correctly bagged waste in a well labelled and lockable bin. The waste can then be disposed of it once enough has accumulated for economical disposal.

[p112 | prose | 14.7 Storing asbestos waste]
Waste should not remain stored for extended periods unnecessarily.

[p112 | prose | 14.8 Asbestos waste removal plan]
14.8 Asbestos waste removal plan

[p112 | prose | 14.8 Asbestos waste removal plan]
The ARCP should include an asbestos waste removal plan. It should describe how to transport and dispose of asbestos waste, and detail:

[p112 | prose | 14.8 Asbestos waste removal plan]
– the name of the asbestos waste transporter

[p112 | prose | 14.8 Asbestos waste removal plan]
– how asbestos waste will be contained

[p112 | prose | 14.8 Asbestos waste removal plan]
– the quantity (amount and dimensions) of asbestos waste

[p112 | prose | 14.8 Asbestos waste removal plan]
– where the asbestos waste will be stored onsite before disposal

[p112 | prose | 14.8 Asbestos waste removal plan]
– how the asbestos waste will be transported

[p112 | prose | 14.8 Asbestos waste removal plan]
– approval requirements from the local council, such as permits and paperwork

[p112 | prose | 14.8 Asbestos waste removal plan]
– local council requirements, such as the quantity of asbestos and container dimensions

[p112 | prose | 14.8 Asbestos waste removal plan]
– the destination of the asbestos waste

[p112 | prose | 14.8 Asbestos waste removal plan]
– how correct disposal will be verified, such as through tip dockets.

[p112 | prose | 14.9 Transporting asbestos waste]
14.9 Transporting asbestos waste

[p112 | prose | 14.9 Transporting asbestos waste]
While asbestos is not considered hazardous waste under the New Zealand road rules, it is recommended that PCBUs transporting asbestos waste should, where relevant, follow the rules for transporting hazardous waste.

[p112 | prose | 14.9 Transporting asbestos waste]
All loads must be made secure.

[p113 | prose | It is recommended that:]
It is recommended that:

[p113 | prose | It is recommended that:]
– drivers hold a dangerous goods licence

[p113 | prose | It is recommended that:]
– skips are well sealed prior to transport to prevent fibre release during transport

[p113 | prose | It is recommended that:]
– sideboards are used to help keep loads secure

[p113 | prose | It is recommended that:]
– waste vehicles have GPS tracking

[p113 | prose | It is recommended that:]
– drivers have completed an asbestos awareness course

[p113 | prose | It is recommended that:]
– there is an emergency response plan in place in case of a crash or incident including:

[p113 | prose | It is recommended that:]
letting first responders know as soon as possible if there is a risk of spilled asbestos waste

[p113 | prose | It is recommended that:]
- having an emergency plan for containing spilled asbestos waste.

[p113 | prose | It is recommended that:]
If a package or bag bursts during transit, the vehicle, container or skip it was being carried in will need to be cleaned out and visibly inspected before it can be reused. A vehicle may also require a disturbed air test.

[p113 | prose | It is recommended that:]
For further information on transporting and disposal of low-level asbestos contaminated soil see WasteMINZ guidance Waste Industry Guidelines for the Disposal of low-level asbestos-contaminated soil at a landfill (2024)

[p113 | prose | Waste: Key points]
Waste: Key points

[p113 | prose | Waste: Key points]
Make sure that waste is properly bagged and/or wrapped with the appropriate approved markings.

[p113 | prose | Waste: Key points]
Waste bags or packages should be removed through the baglock.

[p113 | prose | Waste: Key points]
Position the skip, container or vehicle as close to the enclosure as possible.

[p113 | prose | Waste: Key points]
Ensure that no sharp objects can burst bags or packages during transit.

[p113 | prose | Waste: Key points]
Ensure that the skip, container or vehicle is kept locked or secured.

[p113 | prose | Waste: Key points]
All waste should be taken to a licensed or permitted waste management facility.

[p113 | prose | Waste: Key points]
Asbestos waste can only be stored on site in a secured skip, container or vehicle, or at a waste transfer station.

[p113 | prose | Waste: Key points]
Make sure there are emergency procedures for clearing up any burst bags or packages.

[p115 | prose | Personal decontamination after leaving an enclosure or removal area helps control the spread of asbestos fibres.]
Personal decontamination after leaving an enclosure or removal area helps control the spread of asbestos fibres.

[p115 | prose | 15.1 Personal decontamination helps prevent the spread of asbestos]
15.1 Personal decontamination helps prevent the spread of asbestos

[p115 | prose | 15.1 Personal decontamination helps prevent the spread of asbestos]
Personal decontamination is necessary to make sure workers, their PPE, and RPE are free of asbestos fibres before leaving the enclosure or work area. It helps prevent the spread of asbestos outside the enclosure.

[p115 | prose | 15.1 Personal decontamination helps prevent the spread of asbestos]
Personal decontamination is also important for preventing others who come into contact with asbestos workers, equipment, or waste from getting secondary contamination. Family members can be exposed to asbestos if contaminated clothing is taken home.

[p115 | prose | 15.1 Personal decontamination helps prevent the spread of asbestos]
Personal decontamination is required for all licensed asbestos removal work, whether or not an enclosure is used.

[p115 | prose | 15.1 Personal decontamination helps prevent the spread of asbestos]
There are three stages of personal decontamination (see Section 15.3: Three stages of personal decontamination). The following section provides information on the second and third steps of personal decontamination:

[p115 | prose | 15.1 Personal decontamination helps prevent the spread of asbestos]
– preliminary decontamination and

[p115 | prose | 15.1 Personal decontamination helps prevent the spread of asbestos]
– final decontamination through a decontamination unit (DCU).

[p115 | prose | 15.1 Personal decontamination helps prevent the spread of asbestos]
This section also covers set-up, cleaning, and maintenance requirements for DCUs.

[p115 | prose | 15.1 Personal decontamination helps prevent the spread of asbestos]
For information on Stage 1 personal decontamination procedures, see Section 11: Controlled removal and repair techniques

[p115 | prose | 15.1 Personal decontamination helps prevent the spread of asbestos]
Note: For more information on layout configurations for airlocks, baglocks, enclosures and DCUs see Section 9: Asbestos removal enclosures

[p115 | prose | 15.2 Entering an enclosure or work area]
15.2 Entering an enclosure or work area

[p115 | prose | 15.2 Entering an enclosure or work area]
Workers enter enclosures and work areas through the airlock or designated entry/exit area. They should wear appropriate layers of PPE to allow for correct decontamination when leaving.

[p115 | prose | 15.2 Entering an enclosure or work area]
Place domestic items such as clothes and towels, at the clean end of the DCU for use after decontamination. If the DCU is not connected directly to the airlock, make sure blue transiting overalls are available in the second stage of the airlock for transiting procedures.

[p116 | prose | 15.2 Entering an enclosure or work area]
Removal work often involves crawling, kneeling, stretching, and climbing. These actions can tear or rip coveralls and contaminate underclothes. Workers should not wear any domestic clothes inside the enclosure.

[p116 | prose | 15.2 Entering an enclosure or work area]
Before entering the enclosure or work area, while in the clean end of the 3-stage airlock or DCU, workers should:

[p116 | prose | 15.2 Entering an enclosure or work area]
– remove all domestic clothes

[p116 | prose | 15.2 Entering an enclosure or work area]
– put on any necessary disposable underclothes and overshoes

[p116 | prose | 15.2 Entering an enclosure or work area]
– put on red or orange coveralls

[p116 | prose | 15.2 Entering an enclosure or work area]
– make sure any equipment taken into the enclosure is clean

[p116 | prose | 15.2 Entering an enclosure or work area]
– Put on required RPE and use a mirror to perform a seal check.

[p116 | prose | 15.2 Entering an enclosure or work area]
See Appendix 4 for more detailed instructions for entering enclosures based on different scenarios.

[p116 | prose | 15.3 Three stages of personal decontamination for licenced removal work]
15.3 Three stages of personal decontamination for licenced removal work

[p116 | prose | 15.3 Three stages of personal decontamination for licenced removal work]
Personal decontamination consists of three stages, as shown here.

[p116 | prose | 15.3 Three stages of personal decontamination for licenced removal work]
FIGURE 31:

[p116 | prose | 15.3 Three stages of personal decontamination for licenced removal work]
Three stages of personal decontamination

[p116 | prose | 15.3 Three stages of personal decontamination for licenced removal work]
This three-stage structured approach to personal decontamination allows the process to be conducted systematically and consistently. Workers should follow the same series of steps every time they leave an enclosure or work area.

[p116 | prose | 15.3 Three stages of personal decontamination for licenced removal work]
It is not acceptable to use airlocks as DCU facilities. They should be treated as two separate areas with two specific and different functions.

[p116 | prose | 15.3 Three stages of personal decontamination for licenced removal work]
For information on Stage 1 procedures, see Section 11: Controlled removal and repair techniques

[p116 | prose | 15.4 Preliminary decontamination (Stage 2)]
