15.4 Preliminary decontamination (Stage 2)

[p116 | prose | 15.4 Preliminary decontamination (Stage 2)]
Preliminary decontamination procedures will be different depending on if:

[p116 | prose | 15.4 Preliminary decontamination (Stage 2)]
– the DCU is directly attached to an enclosure (non-transiting)

[p116 | prose | 15.4 Preliminary decontamination (Stage 2)]
– the DCU is separate from the enclosure (transiting)

[p116 | prose | 15.4 Preliminary decontamination (Stage 2)]
– there is no enclosure present.

[p116 | prose | 15.4 Preliminary decontamination (Stage 2)]
Where possible, when enclosures are in use, non-transiting set ups should be used unless it is not reasonably practicable to do so, such as when there is limited space, restricted access, or multi-storey work.

[p116 | prose | 15.4 Preliminary decontamination (Stage 2)]
Transiting set-ups increase the risk of spreading contamination along the transit route. The transit route will form part of the four-stage clearance test at the end of the job.

[p117 | prose | 15.4 Preliminary decontamination (Stage 2)]
Workers must keep their RPE on during preliminary decontamination.

[p117 | prose | Procedures for accessing a DCU directly connected to the enclosure (non-transiting)]
Procedures for accessing a DCU directly connected to the enclosure (non-transiting)

[p117 | prose | Procedures for accessing a DCU directly connected to the enclosure (non-transiting)]
In this set-up the DCU is connected to the enclosure through a short intervening space or tunnel and a one-stage airlock constructed of polythene sheeting (Figure 32).

[p117 | prose | Procedures for accessing a DCU directly connected to the enclosure (non-transiting)]
The procedure relevant for this type of setup is also described in Appendix 4.

[p117 | prose | Procedures for accessing a DCU directly connected to the enclosure (non-transiting)]
Before entering this space, while still in the enclosure, but near the exit to the airlock, the worker should use a vacuum to make sure the bulk of contaminants are removed from their PPE, RPE, and footwear.

[p117 | prose | Procedures for accessing a DCU directly connected to the enclosure (non-transiting)]
They can then proceed to the one stage airlock to clean their RPE (while still wearing it) and footwear then go directly to the DCU for full decontamination (see Section 15.6).

[p118 | prose | Procedures for accessing a DCU separate from the enclosure (transiting)]
Procedures for accessing a DCU separate from the enclosure (transiting)

[p118 | prose | Procedures for accessing a DCU separate from the enclosure (transiting)]
Where it is not possible to attach the DCU directly to the enclosure, take additional steps to make sure that asbestos fibres are not spread while the worker is walking to the DCU.

[p118 | prose | Procedures for accessing a DCU separate from the enclosure (transiting)]
These steps involve the use of a three-stage airlock block (Figure 33) where the worker carries out preliminary decontamination before walking to the DCU for full decontamination.

[p118 | prose | Procedures for accessing a DCU separate from the enclosure (transiting)]
Note: The transit route will form part of the four-stage clearance test at the end of the job.

[p118 | prose | Procedures for accessing a DCU separate from the enclosure (transiting)]
Procedures for using the three-stage airlock

[p118 | prose | Procedures for accessing a DCU separate from the enclosure (transiting)]
The decontamination procedure relevant to this type of setup is also described in

[p119 | prose | Enclosure edge]
Enclosure edge

[p119 | prose | Enclosure edge]
1. Before entering the airlock (while still in the enclosure near the exit to the airlock) vacuum PPE, RPE, and footwear to remove the bulk of contaminants.

[p119 | prose | First (inner) stage of airlock]
First (inner) stage of airlock

[p119 | prose | First (inner) stage of airlock]
2. Keep RPE on.

[p119 | prose | First (inner) stage of airlock]
3. Wipe or dampen RPE with a wet sponge or wipe.

[p119 | prose | First (inner) stage of airlock]
4. Use a brush to clean footwear in the bucket.

[p119 | prose | Middle stage for airlock]
Middle stage for airlock

[p119 | prose | Middle stage for airlock]
5. Keep RPE on.

[p119 | prose | Middle stage for airlock]
6. Remove red/orange coverall. Keep it for re-entry or place it in a hazardous waste bag if damaged or if re-entry is not needed.

[p119 | prose | Middle stage for airlock]
7. Remove shoes or overshoes worn inside the enclosure.

[p119 | prose | Middle stage for airlock]
8. Put on blue transiting coverall.

[p119 | prose | Middle stage for airlock]
9. Use wet wipes to clean the outside of smooth surfaces or equipment such as phones, to remove any attached fibres. Dispose used wipes as hazardous waste.

[p119 | prose | Middle stage for airlock]
10. Place decontaminated items in a labelled bag or container.

[p119 | prose | Third (outer) stage of airlock]
Third (outer) stage of airlock

[p119 | prose | Third (outer) stage of airlock]
11. Keep RPE on.

[p119 | prose | Third (outer) stage of airlock]
12. Put on transiting footwear.

[p119 | prose | Third (outer) stage of airlock]
13. Exit the airlock and walk to the DCU.

[p119 | prose | Third (outer) stage of airlock]
FIGURE 34: Preliminary decontamination - exiting the enclosure (right to left)

[p119 | prose | Third (outer) stage of airlock]
All transit routes should be marked to make sure other workers or members of the public keep away from this route. The transit route will form part of the four-stage clearance test at the end of the job.

[p120 | prose | Procedure for personal decontamination when no enclosure present]
Procedure for personal decontamination when no enclosure present

[p120 | prose | Procedure for personal decontamination when no enclosure present]
Where no enclosure has been used a designated area at the edge of the work area should be set up with all the same materials that are provided for preliminary decontamination within a three-stage or airlock. See Table 29. The same steps should be taken inside this area as would be taken if using a three-stage airlock. Workers may wish to wear two layers of coveralls so they can remove the outer layer more freely in the designated area. After preliminary decontamination, the worker should walk to the DCU. This area between the work area and the DCU (the transiting route) will form part of the area requiring clearance. It should be clearly marked, the same as for transit routes from enclosures to DCUs.

[p121 | prose | 15.5 Set-up requirements for three-stage airlocks]
15.5 Set-up requirements for three-stage airlocks

[p121 | prose | 15.5 Set-up requirements for three-stage airlocks]
Three-stage airlocks should be specially constructed and made of polythene sheeting. They should be attached to the enclosure and should comprise three compartments separated by weighted sheets to minimise the spread of dust between the compartments.

[p121 | prose | 15.5 Set-up requirements for three-stage airlocks]
The three-stage airlock should be as big as possible to enable the workers to change and decontaminate. The absolute minimum dimensions for each compartment should be 1m x 1m x 2m.

[p121 | prose | 15.5 Set-up requirements for three-stage airlocks]
The three stages should have certain facilities within them, as shown in Table 29.

[p121 | table | 15.5 Set-up requirements for three-stage airlocks]
| STAGE | FACILITIES |
| --- | --- |
| Outer stage | Facilities to store transit coveralls and footwear, such as hooks and/or shoe-holders. |
| Middle stage | Facilities to store coveralls and footwear worn in the enclosure, such as hooks and/or shoe-holders. |
| Inner stage | Footbath and brush, water bucket and sponge or wipes for RPE. (Type H vacuum cleaner is usually located at the edge of the enclosure). |

[p121 | prose | 15.5 Set-up requirements for three-stage airlocks]
TABLE 29:

[p121 | prose | 15.5 Set-up requirements for three-stage airlocks]
Three stages of a

[p121 | prose | 15.5 Set-up requirements for three-stage airlocks]
transit facility, or

[p121 | prose | 15.5 Set-up requirements for three-stage airlocks]
three-stage airlock

[p121 | prose | 15.5 Set-up requirements for three-stage airlocks]
Remember: Do not use airlocks as DCU facilities. They should be treated as two separate areas with two specific and different functions.

[p121 | prose | 15.6 Final decontamination in a DCU (Stage 3)]
15.6 Final decontamination in a DCU (Stage 3)

[p121 | prose | 15.6 Final decontamination in a DCU (Stage 3)]
Stage 3 is where the remaining contamination is removed, workers shower and change back into domestic clothing. Purpose-built DCUs that are divided into three zones should be used for final decontamination.

[p121 | prose | 15.6 Final decontamination in a DCU (Stage 3)]
For an example of a DCU layout, see Figure 37.

[p121 | prose | 15.6 Final decontamination in a DCU (Stage 3)]
DCUs also allow workers to prepare for work before entering an enclosure or work area (changing from their personal clothing into their PPE and RPE).

[p121 | prose | 15.6 Final decontamination in a DCU (Stage 3)]
Provide enough DCUs for the number of workers expected to use them. If many workers are finishing work at the same time, multiple DCUs may be required.

[p122 | prose | Zone one – the dirty end]
Zone one – the dirty end

[p122 | prose | Zone one – the dirty end]
1. In the first stage (the dirty end), take off all footwear, coveralls and underwear and place in storage or disposal bags. Do not remove RPE.

[p122 | prose | Zone two – the shower]
Zone two – the shower

[p122 | prose | Zone two – the shower]
2. Move to the shower area (with RPE still on) and use a sponge to clean RPE without allowing water onto filter ports. Remove or cap used filters and place in waste bag for disposal if appropriate.

[p122 | prose | Zone two – the shower]
3. Once RPE has been cleaned, remove it and start showering:

[p122 | prose | Zone two – the shower]
– take thorough showers (a minimum of five minutes)

[p122 | prose | Zone two – the shower]
– thoroughly wash hair

[p122 | prose | Zone two – the shower]
– thoroughly scrub fingernails

[p122 | prose | Zone two – the shower]
4. Use disposable towels to dry.

[p122 | prose | Zone two – the shower]
– If choosing to dry in the shower cubicle, treat the towel used in the shower cubicle as contaminated and dispose of it as asbestos waste.

[p122 | prose | Zone three – the clean end]
Zone three – the clean end

[p122 | prose | Zone three – the clean end]
5. Step into the clean end of the DCU and if not already dry, use a fresh disposable towel to dry completely. If the towel has never progressed beyond the clean end of the DCU, it can be treated as uncontaminated non-asbestos waste.

[p122 | prose | Zone three – the clean end]
6. Get dressed.

[p123 | prose | Zone three – the clean end]
FIGURE 38: Full decontamination in the DCU – exiting the enclosure (right to left)

[p123 | prose | 15.7 Set-up requirements for DCUs]
15.7 Set-up requirements for DCUs

[p123 | prose | 15.7 Set-up requirements for DCUs]
DCUs should be purpose-built and should only be used for the decontamination of asbestos workers. They need to meet the minimum design criteria and specifications set out in Appendix 3.

[p123 | prose | 15.7 Set-up requirements for DCUs]
There are two main types of DCUs:

[p123 | prose | 15.7 Set-up requirements for DCUs]
– mobile DCUs (includes vehicle-based units)

[p123 | prose | 15.7 Set-up requirements for DCUs]
– modular DCUs.

[p123 | prose | 15.7 Set-up requirements for DCUs]
Using pop-up shower pods or makeshift set-ups is not recommended.

[p123 | prose | 15.7 Set-up requirements for DCUs]
DCUs should be on site and functional from the very start of the job. They should be available during early activities such as:

[p123 | prose | 15.7 Set-up requirements for DCUs]
– during pre-clean work

[p123 | prose | 15.7 Set-up requirements for DCUs]
– while the enclosure is being built

[p123 | prose | 15.7 Set-up requirements for DCUs]
– when scaffolding is being erected – especially if there is a risk of the scaffolding disturbing asbestos.

[p123 | prose | 15.7 Set-up requirements for DCUs]
Waste should never be taken through the DCU as it could lead to cross contamination within the unit. Waste (and equipment) should be removed from the enclosure through a separate baglock (see Section 9.7: Enclosure access).

[p123 | prose | Mobile units]
Mobile units

[p123 | prose | Mobile units]
A mobile unit is a caravan-style or relocatable self-contained unit that can be towed to the site. This type of unit should be used in preference to modular units, as generally they are larger and provide more space and comfort for thorough decontamination.

[p123 | prose | Mobile units]
Mobile units are usually available in several sizes to accommodate 2–8 people.

[p124 | prose | Mobile units]
Mobile DCUs should arrive on site clean. The unit should contain a copy of the clearance certificate from the most recent asbestos removal job. Attach the certificate in a visible place in the clean end.

[p124 | prose | Mobile units]
Mobile units must remain on site for the duration of the work activity, including during clearance. Mobile units should not be taken away until after clearance has been issued.

[p124 | prose | FIGURE 39:]
FIGURE 39:

[p124 | prose | FIGURE 39:]
Example of mobile DCU facilities

[p124 | prose | Modular decontamination units]
Modular decontamination units

[p124 | prose | Modular decontamination units]
Modular DCUs are panel-based systems which enable construction of the facilities on site. Some modular units can be assembled in different configurations (for example, straight line or L-shape) or be extended to more than three compartments.

[p124 | prose | Modular decontamination units]
The air extraction unit, the water management system and the electrical/sockets system are usually positioned outside the DCU.

[p124 | prose | Modular decontamination units]
Modular units can often be directly attached to enclosures, avoiding the need for transiting.

[p124 | prose | Modular decontamination units]
There are potential disadvantages with modular units, particularly size, integrity, and the effectiveness of the water management systems. Modular units need to meet the design criteria described in Appendix 3

[p125 | prose | 15.8 Connecting DCUs to services]
15.8 Connecting DCUs to services

[p125 | prose | 15.8 Connecting DCUs to services]
All service connections must meet all relevant regulatory requirements and standards. Table 30 shows what is required.

[p125 | prose | 15.8 Connecting DCUs to services]
ELECTRICAL CONNECTIONS

[p125 | prose | 15.8 Connecting DCUs to services]
GAS APPLIANCES

[p125 | prose | 15.8 Connecting DCUs to services]
DCUs must comply with the Electricity (Safety) Regulations 2010 and the relevant standards depending on the type of connection:

[p125 | prose | 15.8 Connecting DCUs to services]
– DCUs connected by a plug and lead need an electrical warrant of fitness which must be renewed every four years – see AS/NZS 3001

[p125 | prose | 15.8 Connecting DCUs to services]
The gas system needs to be checked regularly to make sure it is operating safely:

[p125 | prose | 15.8 Connecting DCUs to services]
– Gas pipework connections and boilers can be damaged during transportation so should be inspected daily and before the next use of the DCU.

[p125 | prose | 15.8 Connecting DCUs to services]
– DCUs that use a detachable connection complying with IEC 60309 (round pin) with a minimum rating of 16 A (such as ‘caravan type plugs), must comply with AS/NZS 3001

[p125 | prose | 15.8 Connecting DCUs to services]
– Gas bottles (LPG bottles) must be regularly serviced and checked for any leaks each time they are refilled.

[p125 | prose | 15.8 Connecting DCUs to services]
– DCUs connecting to a generator must comply with AS/NZS 3010

[p125 | prose | 15.8 Connecting DCUs to services]
– DCUs with fixed connections must follow the wiring rules for electrical installations – AS/NZS 3000

[p125 | prose | 15.8 Connecting DCUs to services]
– DCUs connected at construction sites must follow specific requirements related to construction site connections – AS/NZS 3012.

[p125 | prose | 15.8 Connecting DCUs to services]
TABLE 30: Requirements for service connections

[p125 | prose | 15.8 Connecting DCUs to services]
For more information, see Section 15.12: Equipment testing

[p125 | prose | 15.8 Connecting DCUs to services]
– Wastewater needs to be filtered through a high efficiency filter (specification less than 5 microns).

[p125 | prose | 15.8 Connecting DCUs to services]
– Check there is sufficient water pressure and hot water for the duration of the job.

[p125 | prose | 15.8 Connecting DCUs to services]
– Filtered wastewater can be disposed of with normal wastewater.

[p125 | prose | 15.9 DCU signage]
15.9 DCU signage

[p125 | prose | 15.9 DCU signage]
Notices should be posted at each entrance door, clearly indicating which is the clean end and which is the dirty end and prohibiting unauthorised entry (Figure 40). Drainage pipes and electrical connections should also be labelled.

[p125 | prose | CLEAN]
CLEAN

[p125 | prose | CLEAN]
FIGURE 40:

[p125 | prose | CLEAN]
Example of notice on DCU

[p125 | prose | CLEAN]
DCUs should have self-closing doors and be lockable between sections so workers can prevent unauthorised entry by others to ensure privacy especially while they are changing clothes and washing.

[p126 | prose | 15.10 Inspection and cleaning of DCUs]
15.10 Inspection and cleaning of DCUs

[p126 | prose | 15.10 Inspection and cleaning of DCUs]
DCUs should be kept clean and deep cleaned at the end of each working day.

[p126 | prose | 15.10 Inspection and cleaning of DCUs]
When cleaning DCUs:

[p126 | prose | 15.10 Inspection and cleaning of DCUs]
– cleaners should wear protective clothing and RPE

[p126 | prose | 15.10 Inspection and cleaning of DCUs]
– work from the clean end towards the dirty end

[p126 | prose | 15.10 Inspection and cleaning of DCUs]
– waste bags from the shower and dirty end (properly cleaned, labelled and double bagged) should be removed through the dirty end

[p126 | prose | 15.10 Inspection and cleaning of DCUs]
– water systems should be drained, traps in the shower and washbasin should be emptied and residues placed in a plastic bag for disposal as asbestos waste.

[p126 | prose | 15.10 Inspection and cleaning of DCUs]
Any transit route and the area where the facility has been parked should be inspected and cleaned to make sure that there is no residual asbestos waste.

[p126 | prose | 15.10 Inspection and cleaning of DCUs]
At the end of the asbestos job, the DCU should be thoroughly cleaned and subject to a clearance test. A clearance certificate should be posted in the clean end of the unit before it is used for the next job.

[p126 | prose | 15.10 Inspection and cleaning of DCUs]
Where the DCU is removed from site at the end of the working day for security reasons, it should be locked and treated as being contaminated. It should be returned as close to the previous location as possible.

[p126 | prose | 15.11 Maintenance of DCUs]
15.11 Maintenance of DCUs

[p126 | prose | 15.11 Maintenance of DCUs]
The DCU should be maintained in good working order and according to any manufacturer’s instructions.

[p126 | prose | 15.11 Maintenance of DCUs]
If there are any problems with the shower, heating, or extraction facilities, the unit should not be used until the problem is fixed.

[p126 | prose | 15.11 Maintenance of DCUs]
Table 31 shows a suggested daily and weekly checklist for supervisors.

[p126 | table | 15.11 Maintenance of DCUs]
| CHECKLIST FOR DCUs |  |
| --- | --- |
| Before DCU use | Do a visual check of all electrical equipment and confirm: ' it is compatible with the electrical power sources ' the earth fault loop impedance test is complete ' the voltage and plug pins are compatible ' the earth bonding works correctly (for modular DCUs installed on site). |
| Daily (before each shift) | Daily operational checks should be carried out by a suitably trained supervisor to confirm: - water, gas, and electricity supplies are working and sufficient = the shower has good pressure and the right temperature = the heating system works the NPU works (check the pressure gauge or warning device to make sure the HEPA filter is not saturated battery chargers work there are enough waste bags, towels (one in the shower and one in the clean end), filters, and PPE the unit, transit routes, and facilities are clean the vacuum cleaner works, and there are enough sponges and water baths in any transit airlock airlocks are intact there are no environmental issues affecting the DCU, such as frozen pipes RCDs work. |
| Weekly | Visual check of electrical equipment for damage, wear, overheating etc. |

[p126 | prose | 15.11 Maintenance of DCUs]
TABLE 31: Daily and weekly checklist for DCUs

[p127 | prose | 15.12 Equipment testing]
15.12 Equipment testing

[p127 | prose | 15.12 Equipment testing]
Equipment should be examined and tested by an appropriate competent person and records kept.

[p127 | prose | 15.12 Equipment testing]
Table 32 shows what should be checked, by who, and how often.

[p127 | table | 15.12 Equipment testing]
| EQUIPMENT | CHECKING REQUIREMENTS | WHO SHOULD CHECK AND HOW OFTEN |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Air extraction system | DOP or sodium chloride tests on the HEPA filter Volumetric flow rates | - Qualified technician/competent person - Every six months |
| Gas appliances | Service and maintain in accordance with the manufacturer’s recommendations | Qualified gas technician As required for the appliance warrant of fitness |
| Electrical systems | Look for evidence of breakages, wear, or deterioration Look for signs of overheating Check for missing parts (such as screws or covers) Make sure switchgear is accessible (not obstructed) Check for loose fittings Make sure there is no access to live conductive parts Check the equipment is working properly - lighting, heating, shower unit, RDCs (using test button*), sockets, and switchgear (where reasonable). | A competent person should do these routine checks every week and a formal documented check every month. The competent person doing the checks does not need to be an electrician, but they should be trained in what to look for and be trusted to do the job properly. *Test the RCDs that provide automatic disconnection on the distribution circuits every day. |

[p127 | prose | 15.12 Equipment testing]
TABLE 32: DCU equipment that should be checked, by who, and how often.

[p127 | prose | 15.12 Equipment testing]
Record-keeping of equipment tests and checks

[p127 | prose | 15.12 Equipment testing]
Records should be kept of site inspections as well as equipment tests and checks. Records should be kept for five years and copies of the most recent checks should be available for inspection on site.

[p127 | prose | 15.13 Training in decontamination procedures]
15.13 Training in decontamination procedures

[p127 | prose | 15.13 Training in decontamination procedures]
Workers should receive practical training on decontamination procedures so they can decontaminate properly. Training should involve practicing how to decontaminate in a simulated environment.

[p127 | prose | 15.13 Training in decontamination procedures]
It is particularly important for workers to recognise the need for primary decontamination during transit procedures.

[p127 | prose | 15.13 Training in decontamination procedures]
For more information, see Section 5.0 Training and instruction

[p127 | prose | 15.14 Monitoring of the decontamination process]
15.14 Monitoring of the decontamination process

[p127 | prose | 15.14 Monitoring of the decontamination process]
Supervisors should monitor and regularly check that workers are consistently following decontamination procedures. For example:

[p127 | prose | 15.14 Monitoring of the decontamination process]
– make sure that workers do not develop poor practices or become complacent – make sure that no inappropriate items are taken into the DCU (such as filled waste bags, drink bottles)

[p127 | prose | 15.14 Monitoring of the decontamination process]
– check that decontamination is carried out regardless of the time spent inside the enclosure.

[p127 | prose | 15.14 Monitoring of the decontamination process]
Where workers are found not following the decontamination procedures, consider retraining for those workers.

[p128 | prose | 15.15 Emergency procedures for DCU failure]
15.15 Emergency procedures for DCU failure

[p128 | prose | 15.15 Emergency procedures for DCU failure]
Have a plan for what to do if there is a loss of services to the DCU. Plans should be developed for alternative basic decontamination procedures, to use other facilities on site, or by organising the services of another DCU.

[p128 | prose | 15.15 Emergency procedures for DCU failure]
These alternative arrangements should be clearly documented, and workers and supervisors should be aware of what actions to take.

[p130 | prose | All equipment used in asbestos removal must be decontaminated or appropriately sealed before leaving the work area]
All equipment used in asbestos removal must be decontaminated or appropriately sealed before leaving the work area

[p130 | prose | 16.1 Decontamination of equipment helps prevent the spread of asbestos]
16.1 Decontamination of equipment helps prevent the spread of asbestos

[p130 | prose | 16.1 Decontamination of equipment helps prevent the spread of asbestos]
Nothing that is likely to be contaminated with asbestos should be removed from the asbestos removal area unless it:

[p130 | prose | 16.1 Decontamination of equipment helps prevent the spread of asbestos]
– is decontaminated before being removed

[p130 | prose | 16.1 Decontamination of equipment helps prevent the spread of asbestos]
– is sealed in a container that has been decontaminated on the exterior and clearly labelled to show it may contain asbestos.

[p130 | prose | 16.2 Decontaminating tools]
16.2 Decontaminating tools

[p130 | prose | 16.2 Decontaminating tools]
All tools used during asbestos work need to be fully dismantled (where possible), cleaned under controlled conditions and decontaminated using within the baglock (or airlock if there was no space for baglock erection), or designated entry and exit area.

[p130 | prose | 16.2 Decontaminating tools]
If tools cannot be decontaminated, they must be placed in a sealed container and marked clearly to indicate the presence of asbestos. The outside surfaces of the container must be wiped clean

[p130 | prose | 16.2 Decontaminating tools]
The containers of tools must remain sealed until decontamination or the start of the next asbestos task, when the equipment can be taken into the asbestos enclosure or work area and re-used under controlled conditions.

[p130 | prose | 16.2 Decontaminating tools]
Workers should wear PPE and RPE when opening containers to clean or re-use the equipment or tools stored inside.

[p130 | prose | 16.2 Decontaminating tools]
In some circumstances it may be better to dispose of contaminated tools and equipment, depending on the level of contamination and the ease of replacement. If tools and equipment are disposable, they should be disposed of and not re-used.

[p131 | prose | 16.3 Decontaminating vehicles, machinery or mobile plant]
16.3 Decontaminating vehicles, machinery or mobile plant

[p131 | prose | 16.3 Decontaminating vehicles, machinery or mobile plant]
Avoid exposing vehicles, machinery or mobile plant to asbestos contamination. Where this is unavoidable, a decontamination unit large enough to accommodate the vehicle, machinery or mobile plant will have to be built:

[p131 | prose | 16.3 Decontaminating vehicles, machinery or mobile plant]
– Locate the unit away from the personal decontamination unit but next to the asbestos enclosure or work area. It can be purpose-built with suitable heavy- duty timbers and plywood. It should be lined internally and externally with heavy-duty plastic sheeting (200 m thickness).

[p131 | prose | 16.3 Decontaminating vehicles, machinery or mobile plant]
– The unit should be watertight to prevent excess water run-off during wash- down procedures. Make sure all wastewater is collected for filtration or disposal.

[p131 | prose | 16.3 Decontaminating vehicles, machinery or mobile plant]
– Use spring-loaded doors on either side of the unit to provide an airlock when the vehicle, machine, or mobile plant passes through the unit. Only open one set of doors at a time during entry and exit to prevent airborne fibres from escaping the enclosure.

[p131 | prose | 16.3 Decontaminating vehicles, machinery or mobile plant]
– Once the decontamination unit is dismantled, dispose of the materials used in building the unit as asbestos waste.

[p131 | prose | 16.3 Decontaminating vehicles, machinery or mobile plant]
To decontaminate vehicles, machinery or mobile plant inside the decontamination unit:

[p131 | prose | 16.3 Decontaminating vehicles, machinery or mobile plant]
1. thoroughly wash down the vehicle, machinery or mobile plant using low-pressure water spray

[p131 | prose | 16.3 Decontaminating vehicles, machinery or mobile plant]
2. make sure the cab, tracks or tyres, undercarriage, boom and body are thoroughly doused with water to remove any asbestos dust

[p131 | prose | 16.3 Decontaminating vehicles, machinery or mobile plant]
3. leave the vehicle, machinery or mobile plant in the unit and wash down the inside walls and floor of the unit

[p131 | prose | 16.3 Decontaminating vehicles, machinery or mobile plant]
4. have another worker in clean PPE and RPE enter the unit from the clean side to take the machine from the unit to the clean area.

[p131 | prose | 16.3 Decontaminating vehicles, machinery or mobile plant]
Note: Workers should not exit the asbestos working area through the vehicle decontamination unit.

[p131 | prose | Managing wastewater]
Managing wastewater

[p131 | prose | Managing wastewater]
If not using filtration, collect the water from the decontamination process in a sump and dispose of it as contaminated waste.

[p131 | prose | Managing wastewater]
Do not dump unfiltered water in council catchments. Talk to the local council or territorial authority about their wastewater requirements.

[p133 | prose | Managing wastewater]
PPE is essential to help minimise the risk of exposure to asbestos fibres. PPE should also be considered when minimising risks from other workplace hazards.

[p133 | prose | 17.1 PPE and RPE is essential for asbestos removal work]
17.1 PPE and RPE is essential for asbestos removal work

[p133 | prose | 17.1 PPE and RPE is essential for asbestos removal work]
PCBUs must eliminate the risk of airborne asbestos fibres during removal work so far as is reasonably practicable. In addition, PCBUs must provide suitable PPE (including RPE) to workers to manage any remaining risk from exposure to airborne asbestos during removal work.

[p133 | prose | 17.1 PPE and RPE is essential for asbestos removal work]
The PCBU must make sure that PPE is:

[p133 | prose | 17.1 PPE and RPE is essential for asbestos removal work]
– suitable for work with asbestos

[p133 | prose | 17.1 PPE and RPE is essential for asbestos removal work]
– a suitable size and fit, and reasonably comfortable

[p133 | prose | 17.1 PPE and RPE is essential for asbestos removal work]
– is compatible with other PPE used

[p133 | prose | 17.1 PPE and RPE is essential for asbestos removal work]
– supported by training so workers know how to wear, use, and store it properly

[p133 | prose | 17.1 PPE and RPE is essential for asbestos removal work]
– maintained, repaired, or replaced so it continues to minimise the risk

[p133 | prose | 17.1 PPE and RPE is essential for asbestos removal work]
– kept clean, hygienic and in good working order.

[p133 | prose | 17.2 General PPE information]
17.2 General PPE information

[p133 | prose | 17.2 General PPE information]
Who provides PPE

[p133 | prose | 17.2 General PPE information]
The PCBU directing the work must provide PPE unless it is provided by another PCBU.

[p133 | prose | 17.2 General PPE information]
Potentially there may be more than one PCBU with a duty to provide the PPE. If this is the case, the PCBUs need to consult with each other to make sure suitable arrangements are made.

[p133 | prose | 17.2 General PPE information]
The PCBU must pay for the cost of PPE (unless a worker chooses to provide their own). If a worker does choose to provide their own, the PCBU must still make sure it meets the requirements for working with asbestos and decontamination.

[p133 | prose | 17.2 General PPE information]
Workers can change their mind at any time and choose that the PCBU provide it instead. Workers must give the PCBU reasonable notice of this change.

[p134 | prose | PPE for asbestos removal work]
PPE for asbestos removal work

[p134 | prose | PPE for asbestos removal work]
Suitable PPE is required for any work where there is a risk of exposure to asbestos fibres. The types of PPE that are commonly used when working on or near asbestos are shown in Table 33.

[p134 | table | PPE for asbestos removal work]
| TYPE OF PPE | EXAMPLE | PURPOSE |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Protective clothing | Coveralls | s Protective clothing helps to prevent contamination of workers’ clothing with asbestos fibres. This helps to minimise the risk of spreading asbestos fibres (for example, into workers’ homes). |
| Respiratory protective equipment (RPE) | Respirator with filter | Respiratory protective equipment helps to minimise the risk of workers breathing in asbestos fibres. |
| Foot protection | Gumboots and shoe coverings | Foot protection that is easy to clean helps to prevent the spread of asbestos fibres away from the work site. |
| Hand protection | Single-use gloves | Minimises the risk of asbestos fibres getting stuck under workers’ fingernails or getting into cuts and grazes. |
| Eye protection | Safety goggles | Protects the eyes from dust and other particles. |

[p134 | prose | PPE for asbestos removal work]
TABLE 33: Types of PPE commonly used when working on or near asbestos

[p134 | prose | PPE for asbestos removal work]
More detailed information on the above types of PPE is provided later in this section.

[p134 | prose | PPE for managing non-asbestos-related risks]
PPE for managing non-asbestos-related risks

[p134 | prose | PPE for managing non-asbestos-related risks]
Workers may need other types of PPE in addition to PPE for working with asbestos.

[p134 | prose | PPE for managing non-asbestos-related risks]
If the removal work involves other hazardous substances, the PCBU should take these risks into consideration when conducting the risk assessment.

[p134 | prose | PPE for managing non-asbestos-related risks]
When other hazardous substances are involved, the PCBU should refer to safety data sheets (SDS) for information on the appropriate PPE to use and any other precautions to take.

[p134 | prose | PPE for managing non-asbestos-related risks]
Note: The manufacturer, importer or seller of a hazardous substance must supply a New Zealand-specific SDS on request. For more information see Safety data sheets

[p134 | prose | PPE for other people at the removal site]
PPE for other people at the removal site

[p134 | prose | PPE for other people at the removal site]
PCBUs must make sure other people at the site, such as visitors, wear PPE if their health and safety may be affected.

[p134 | prose | PPE for other people at the removal site]
Other people, such as visitors, are likely to require instruction or training on how to wear PPE correctly. This should be provided by either:

[p134 | prose | PPE for other people at the removal site]
– the PCBU responsible for providing PPE and making sure visitors use it, or – the licensed asbestos removalist.

[p134 | prose | PPE for other people at the removal site]
Other people visiting the work area must follow the reasonable instructions of the PCBU who has control of the work area regarding wearing or using PPE.

[p134 | prose | Training in correct PPE use]
Training in correct PPE use

[p134 | prose | Training in correct PPE use]
Workers must receive training from the PCBU on the correct use of PPE and RPE.

[p134 | prose | Training in correct PPE use]
Workers must use and wear PPE in accordance with any information, training or reasonable instruction given.

[p134 | prose | Training in correct PPE use]
This training should be completed before work with asbestos commences and on regular occasions afterwards.

[p135 | prose | Training in correct PPE use]
Training in the use of PPE should include:

[p135 | prose | Training in correct PPE use]
– correct use, including safe removal

[p135 | prose | Training in correct PPE use]
– inspection, care, and maintenance

[p135 | prose | Training in correct PPE use]
– repair and replacement

[p135 | prose | Training in correct PPE use]
– emergency procedures

[p135 | prose | Training in correct PPE use]
– correct storage.

[p135 | prose | Training in correct PPE use]
Safe removal of PPE

[p135 | prose | Training in correct PPE use]
Removal of contaminated PPE should be carried out using strict control measures.

[p135 | prose | Training in correct PPE use]
For information on proper decontamination procedures, see Section 15.0: Personal decontamination

[p135 | prose | Storing reusable PPE]
Storing reusable PPE

[p135 | prose | Storing reusable PPE]
After each use, reusable PPE must be stored correctly:

[p135 | prose | Storing reusable PPE]
– reusable PPE must be stored in a sealed container until it is reused

[p135 | prose | Storing reusable PPE]
– RPE must be stored in airtight, sealed containers

[p135 | prose | Storing reusable PPE]
– reusable footwear such as gumboots should be stored upside down after wiping down the outside surface with disposable wipes

[p135 | prose | Storing reusable PPE]
– when using sealed containers, the outside of the containers must be wiped down and the container labelled as containing asbestos (for example, there may be asbestos fibres still trapped in the capped filter of a respirator).

[p135 | prose | Storing reusable PPE]
Note: Never store used, disposable PPE. This must be disposed of as asbestos waste.

[p135 | prose | Storing reusable PPE]
Workers should never take asbestos-contaminated PPE home.

[p135 | prose | Providing replacement PPE]
Providing replacement PPE

[p135 | prose | Providing replacement PPE]
PPE must be clean, hygienic, and in good working order. Make sure that PPE is maintained, repaired, or replaced so that it continues to minimise risk to the worker that uses it.

[p135 | prose | Providing replacement PPE]
Replace PPE whenever it becomes worn out, no longer provides adequate protection, or is past its use-by date. Replacing PPE should be assessed based on need.

[p135 | prose | Disposing of used PPE]
Disposing of used PPE

[p135 | prose | Disposing of used PPE]
Place contaminated PPE, as well as wipes used for decontamination, into a heavy-duty 200 m polythene plastic bags or double wrap them in polythene sheets for disposal. The bag should be tied securely with a goose-neck tie and be clearly labelled as containing asbestos.

[p135 | prose | Disposing of used PPE]
If a vacuum cleaner has been used to vacuum down clothing, the vacuum cleaner bag is also considered asbestos waste, and will need to be decontaminated and quarantined.

[p135 | prose | Disposing of used PPE]
Asbestos waste can only be disposed of at authorised disposal sites. Check with the relevant local authority where these are, and the requirements they have.

[p135 | prose | Disposing of used PPE]
For more information on asbestos waste disposal, see Section 14.0: Containment and disposal of asbestos waste

[p136 | prose | Training workers to use PPE]
Training workers to use PPE

[p136 | prose | Training workers to use PPE]
Workers must be trained on how to properly wear or use PPE, and how to store and maintain it. Training should be delivered by a competent person.

[p136 | prose | Training workers to use PPE]
Train workers before they start work. Training should include:

[p136 | prose | Training workers to use PPE]
– what risks workers might be exposed to

[p136 | prose | Training workers to use PPE]
– how each item of PPE works

[p136 | prose | Training workers to use PPE]
– why workers should wear PPE

[p136 | prose | Training workers to use PPE]
– when workers should wear PPE

[p136 | prose | Training workers to use PPE]
– how to correctly put on and remove PPE

[p136 | prose | Training workers to use PPE]
– how to make sure PPE fits properly

[p136 | prose | Training workers to use PPE]
– how to clean, store, and maintain PPE

[p136 | prose | Training workers to use PPE]
– how to dispose of PPE

[p136 | prose | Training workers to use PPE]
– what to do if there is a problem with a worker’s PPE.

[p136 | prose | Training workers to use PPE]
New or inexperienced workers should be supervised in putting on and using PPE until they have shown they are competent to do so unsupervised.

[p136 | prose | Training workers to use PPE]
Experienced workers may also need to be supervised in putting on and using PPE if changes are made to the PPE being used, or if they have not used the PPE recently.

[p136 | prose | Training workers to use PPE]
Review worker training regularly.

[p136 | prose | 17.3 Coveralls]
17.3 Coveralls

[p136 | prose | 17.3 Coveralls]
Coveralls are a type of protective clothing used to reduce the risk of asbestos fibre exposure when working with or near asbestos. They provide a barrier between a worker’s body and potential hazards (for example, dust, fibres, and other contaminants).

[p136 | prose | 17.3 Coveralls]
Disposable (or single use) coveralls should be used for removal work.

[p136 | prose | 17.3 Coveralls]
Colour coding coveralls can help workers and managers/supervisors stay aware of what level of contamination or exposure risk they are working at. For example:

[p136 | prose | 17.3 Coveralls]
– red/orange overalls can be used for removing/stripping

[p136 | prose | 17.3 Coveralls]
– blue can be used for transit and waste

[p136 | prose | 17.3 Coveralls]
– white for assessors and preliminary work.

[p136 | prose | 17.3 Coveralls]
Table 34 outlines examples of what to consider when choosing suitable coveralls for asbestos work.

[p136 | table | 17.3 Coveralls]
| WHAT TO CONSIDER | GOOD PRACTICE |
| --- | --- |
| Type | Disposable coveralls should meet the minimum requirements for chemical protective clothing resistant to penetration by airborne solid particles (as described in Standard BS EN ISO 13982-1). Get disposable coveralls (for example, Type 5 disposable coveralls) to meet this standard. |
| Quality | Disposable coveralls should be of a suitable quality to prevent tearing. Coveralls that are too thin are more likely to break at the seams. |
| Comfort | Disposable coveralls must be reasonably comfortable for workers to wear. |

[p137 | table | 17.3 Coveralls]
| WHAT TO CONSIDER | GOOD PRACTICE |
| --- | --- |
| Fit | Disposable coveralls should be: - fitted with an elasticated hood and elasticated cuffs - one size bigger than the worker would normally wear to minimise the risk of tearing at the seams. |
| Other considerations | Disposable coveralls should not have external pockets, Velcro fastenings, or other features that are difficult to decontaminate. Waterproof disposable coveralls may be needed if the work is carried out outdoors. |

[p137 | prose | 17.3 Coveralls]
TABLE 34: What to consider when choosing suitable disposable coveralls

[p137 | prose | 17.3 Coveralls]
FIGURE 41:

[p137 | prose | Important]
Important

[p137 | prose | Important]
Workers should never take disposable coveralls home.

[p137 | prose | Important]
Used disposable coveralls must be disposed of as asbestos waste. They should never be reused.

[p138 | prose | 17.4 Gloves]
17.4 Gloves

[p138 | prose | 17.4 Gloves]
Gloves create a protective barrier between a worker’s hands and potential contaminants, such as dust, fibres, and other harmful substances.

[p138 | prose | 17.4 Gloves]
They help to minimise the risk of asbestos fibres getting stuck under fingernails or getting into cuts and grazes, which can be difficult and painful to decontaminate.

[p138 | prose | 17.4 Gloves]
Single-use disposable gloves should be used for removal work. Gloves used during asbestos removal work must be disposed of as asbestos waste.

[p138 | prose | 17.4 Gloves]
Table 35 outlines examples of what to consider when choosing suitable gloves for asbestos removal work.

[p138 | table | 17.4 Gloves]
| WHAT TO CONSIDER | GOOD PRACTICE |
| --- | --- |
| Type | Disposable gloves suitable for working on or near asbestos are usually made of latex, vinyl, or nitrile. |
| Low protein (powder free) latex gloves may reduce the risk of workers developing a latex allergy or skin problems compared with standard latex gloves. Non-latex disposable gloves should always be provided to workers who have an allergy to latex. |  |
| Quality | Disposable gloves should be made of good quality material. Gloves that are too thin can be more likely to tear. |
| Fit | Disposable gloves should be reasonably easy to put on and take off. They should fit close to the hand. Arm cuffs of coveralls should be worn over the top of gloves, not tucked into the gloves. |
| Comfort | Gloves must be reasonably comfortable to wear - they should not be too tight. If they are too big, they can slip or make it hard to grip things. If they are too small, they can be uncomfortable to wear and could tear more easily. |

[p138 | prose | Important]
Important

[p138 | prose | Important]
Workers should never take disposable gloves home.

[p138 | prose | Important]
Used disposable gloves must be disposed of as asbestos waste. They should never be reused.

[p138 | prose | 17.5 Protective footwear]
17.5 Protective footwear

[p138 | prose | 17.5 Protective footwear]
Protective footwear is used to:

[p138 | prose | 17.5 Protective footwear]
– provide a barrier between a worker’s feet and potential contaminants

[p138 | prose | 17.5 Protective footwear]
– protect a worker’s feet from impacts (for example, things being dropped)

[p138 | prose | 17.5 Protective footwear]
– provide improved grip for walking on slippery or uneven surfaces.

[p138 | prose | 17.5 Protective footwear]
Table 36 outlines examples of what to consider when choosing suitable foot protection for asbestos removal work.

[p139 | table | 17.5 Protective footwear]
| WHAT TO CONSIDER | GOOD PRACTICE |
| --- | --- |
| Type | Protective footwear that may be suitable for asbestos removal includes: - gumboots - steel-capped shoes - rubber-soled shoes. |
| Gumboots are generally the best option because they do not have laces and can be easily decontaminated. Select the most appropriate safety footwear for the work that is being carried out. Protective footwear used for asbestos removal should not have laces or eyelets. Laces and eyelets can easily become contaminated with asbestos fibres and are difficult to decontaminate. |  |
|  |  |
| Quality | Protective footwear should be made of good quality, durable material. Good quality protective footwear is less likely to split, break down, or lose comfort over time. |
|  | They tight Footwear should have some space to allow for normal swelling of the feet and layers of insulation when needed (for example, thicker socks in cold weather or an insole to improve comfort). |
| Leg cuffs of coveralls should be worn over the top of protective footwear, not tucked into the top of the footwear. |  |
| Comfort | Protective footwear must be reasonably comfortable to wear. |
| Footwear that does not provide enough support can cause pain, blisters, and other foot problems. Each worker should have their own pair of protective footwear to minimise the risk of foot infections being spread. |  |

[p139 | prose | 17.5 Protective footwear]
TABLE 36: What to consider when choosing suitable protective footwear

[p139 | prose | 17.5 Protective footwear]
Protective footwear should be stored upside down when not in use to minimise the risk of asbestos contaminating the inside of shoes and boots. Provide storage that allows footwear to be stored in this way.

[p139 | prose | 17.5 Protective footwear]
Make sure protective footwear is maintained in good working order.

[p139 | prose | Disposable shoe covers]
Disposable shoe covers

[p139 | prose | Disposable shoe covers]
Disposable shoe covers are designed to fit over the top of shoes or boots to minimise contamination of shoes and boots (Figure 42). They should cover the whole shoe or boot, and should be secured above the ankle.

[p139 | prose | Disposable shoe covers]
Disposable shoe covers should have an anti-slip sole design.

[p139 | prose | Disposable shoe covers]
Gumboots (left) and disposable shoe covers covering gumboots (right)

[p140 | prose | Important]
Important

[p140 | prose | Important]
Workers should never take disposable shoe covers home.

[p140 | prose | Important]
Used disposable shoe covers must be disposed of as asbestos waste. They should never be reused.

[p140 | prose | Important]
Leg cuffs of coveralls should be worn over the top of shoe covers. They should not be tucked into the top of shoe covers.

[p140 | prose | Decontaminating reusable footwear]
Decontaminating reusable footwear

[p140 | prose | Decontaminating reusable footwear]
If it is not reasonably practicable to dispose of used footwear as asbestos waste, the footwear must be decontaminated in a decontamination area.

[p140 | prose | Decontaminating reusable footwear]
Decontaminating footwear should include:

[p140 | prose | Decontaminating reusable footwear]
– vacuuming using a Class H vacuum cleaner with a brush attachment, and

[p140 | prose | Decontaminating reusable footwear]
– wiping with wet rags or wet wipes.

[p140 | prose | Decontaminating reusable footwear]
The contents of the Class H vacuum cleaner and any used rags and wipes must be disposed of as asbestos waste.

[p140 | prose | Storing contaminated reusable footwear]
Storing contaminated reusable footwear

[p140 | prose | Storing contaminated reusable footwear]
If it is not reasonably practicable to dispose of contaminated reusable footwear, they must:

[p140 | prose | Storing contaminated reusable footwear]
– be kept in a sealed container that does not allow asbestos fibres to escape

[p140 | prose | Storing contaminated reusable footwear]
– be clearly marked as ‘ASBESTOS CONTAMINATED CLOTHING’.

[p140 | prose | Storing contaminated reusable footwear]
– be kept in the sealed container until they are reused for asbestos work.

[p140 | prose | 17.6 Respiratory protective equipment (RPE)]
17.6 Respiratory protective equipment (RPE)

[p140 | prose | 17.6 Respiratory protective equipment (RPE)]
RPE is a key control measure to prevent the inhalation of asbestos fibres while doing removal work. Workers should always wear RPE if respirable asbestos fibres are above trace levels.

[p140 | prose | 17.6 Respiratory protective equipment (RPE)]
The greater the risk of exposure to asbestos, the higher the level of protection the RPE should provide.

[p140 | prose | 17.6 Respiratory protective equipment (RPE)]
The information in the section below should be considered alongside advice from a suitably qualified and experienced health and safety professional (such as an occupational hygienist) who is knowledgeable of the working conditions and the limitations of RPE in relation to asbestos removal work.

[p140 | prose | Selecting the right type of RPE]
Selecting the right type of RPE

[p140 | prose | Selecting the right type of RPE]
RPE should be selected after doing a case-by-case risk assessment and consider the following:

[p140 | prose | Selecting the right type of RPE]
– the individual characteristics of the worker. See Selecting the most appropriate RPE for the worker, task, and environment in Protective clothing and equipment for working with or near asbestos

[p140 | prose | Selecting the right type of RPE]
– the type of removal work

[p140 | prose | Selecting the right type of RPE]
– the removal techniques that will be used

[p140 | prose | Selecting the right type of RPE]
– the duration the RPE will be worn for (for prolonged work, power-assisted RPE should be worn)

[p140 | prose | Selecting the right type of RPE]
– the expected level of exposure (Appendix 2: Asbestos levels associated with asbestos activities)

[p141 | prose | Selecting the right type of RPE]
Results from previous air monitoring for similar work tasks can help with the risk assessment.

[p141 | prose | Selecting the right type of RPE]
When selecting RPE, refer to the latest versions of:

[p141 | prose | Selecting the right type of RPE]
– AS/NZS 1715 Selection, use and maintenance of respiratory protective devices, and

[p141 | prose | Selecting the right type of RPE]
– AS/NZS 1716 Respiratory protective devices, or

[p141 | prose | Selecting the right type of RPE]
– SA/SNZ TS ISO 16975 Respiratory protective devices – Selection, use and maintenance

[p141 | prose | Selecting the right type of RPE]
Use information provided by RPE manufacturers or suppliers when selecting the correct type of respirator and filter.

[p141 | prose | Negative-pressure respirators]
Negative-pressure respirators

[p141 | prose | Negative-pressure respirators]
For lower-risk work, negative-pressure respirators may be appropriate.

[p141 | prose | Negative-pressure respirators]
Lower-risk work may include:

[p141 | prose | Negative-pressure respirators]
– work before removal has started

[p141 | prose | Negative-pressure respirators]
– work after removal has been completed and the area has been cleaned

[p141 | prose | Negative-pressure respirators]
– small-scale Class B removal

[p141 | prose | Negative-pressure respirators]
– unlicensed removal.

[p141 | prose | Negative-pressure respirators]
Negative-pressure respirators use one or more filters to purify the air that is breathed in. Inhaling creates negative pressure, which draws contaminated air through the filter. Examples of negative-pressure respirators are shown in Figure 43.

[p141 | prose | Negative-pressure respirators]
Examples of respirators suitable for lower risk asbestos work

[p141 | prose | Positive-pressure respirators]
Positive-pressure respirators

[p141 | prose | Positive-pressure respirators]
For higher-risk work full-facepiece positive-pressure respirators must be used. Higher risk work includes:

[p141 | prose | Positive-pressure respirators]
– All friable asbestos removal (Class A removal).

[p141 | prose | Positive-pressure respirators]
– Class B removal where there is expected to be elevated levels of airborne asbestos fibres. See Appendix 2: Asbestos levels associated with asbestos activities for more information.

[p141 | prose | Positive-pressure respirators]
If a proper seal cannot be achieved (for example, due to facial hair) individuals may need to use powered hoods or blouses.

[p141 | prose | Positive-pressure respirators]
Positive-pressure respirators use an independent supply of air that is suitable for breathing (for example, an air cylinder). They always maintain a positive pressure inside the facepiece. Examples of positive-pressure respirators are shown in Figure 44.

[p141 | prose | Positive-pressure respirators]
Positive-pressure respirators prevent contaminated air being inhaled even if there is a leak in the seal between the face and the facepiece (See Table 37). They are generally used where the risk of asbestos exposure is high.

[p142 | table | Positive-pressure respirators]
| TYPE | HOW THEY WORK |
| --- | --- |
| Powered air-purifying respirators (PAPRSs) | PAPRs use a battery-powered blower to draw air through the filters or cartridges and push it into the facepiece. |
| Supplied-air respirators (SARs) or air-line respirators | SARs (or air-line respirators) supply clean air from a tank of compressed air or through an air-line. |
| Self-contained breathing apparatus (SCBA) | SCBAs have their own air supply supplied from a high-pressure tank that is carried by the user. |

[p142 | prose | Positive-pressure respirators]
WorkSafe must be notified of any work that involves a worker breathing compressed air, or anything other than air.

[p142 | prose | Positive-pressure respirators]
To notify WorkSafe of work that involves a worker breathing compressed air, use the Notification Tool (Notify WorkSafe) and select Report Particular Hazardous Work.

[p142 | prose | Air-line respirators]
Air-line respirators

[p142 | prose | Air-line respirators]
Air-line respirators are used when removing friable asbestos. The air-line should include a belt-mounted back-up filter when in use. If the air supply system fails, workers must leave the asbestos removal area using normal decontamination procedures. The back-up filter will provide enough protection during this process.

[p142 | prose | Air-line respirators]
If too many workers are using air-line respirators inside an enclosure, the lines might get tangled. If this happens, the removalist should provide manifolds to prevent tangling and help workers move around.

[p142 | prose | Air-line respirators]
The compressor must be able to supply enough air for all the air-lines in use, and its intake should draw in clean, uncontaminated air. The air must be filtered before it reaches the respirator.

[p142 | prose | Air-line respirators]
TABLE 37:

[p142 | prose | Air-line respirators]
Types of positive- pressure respirators

[p142 | prose | Air-line respirators]
FIGURE 45:

[p142 | prose | Air-line respirators]
Full-face, positive- pressure demand air-line respirator

[p143 | prose | Filters]
Filters

[p143 | prose | Filters]
RPE must have an appropriate filter. Particle filters are classified into three groups (Table 38).

[p143 | table | Filters]
| TYPE | DETAILS |
| --- | --- |
| P1 | P1 filters are also known as low efficiency filters. They are not recommended for work on or near asbestos because they cannot effectively filter asbestos particles. |
| P2 | P2 filters are also known as medium efficiency. They stop up to 94% of airborne particles from being inhaled if they are used correctly. P2 filters do not provide the maximum protection for working with or on asbestos material. |
| P3 | P3 are also known as high efficiency filters. They stop up to 99.95% of airborne particles from being inhaled if they are used correctly. P3 filters provide the maximum protection for working with or on asbestos material. P3 filters should be used for licensed asbestos work. |

[p143 | prose | TABLE 38: Classifications of RPE filters]
TABLE 38: Classifications of RPE filters

[p143 | prose | TABLE 38: Classifications of RPE filters]
Filters must provide suitable protection for the work being carried out and work effectively in the work conditions.

[p143 | prose | TABLE 38: Classifications of RPE filters]
The length of time that a particulate filter can be used for work on or near asbestos depends on:

[p143 | prose | TABLE 38: Classifications of RPE filters]
– the quality of the filter

[p143 | prose | TABLE 38: Classifications of RPE filters]
– how well the filter is maintained

[p143 | prose | TABLE 38: Classifications of RPE filters]
– how often the filter is being used

[p143 | prose | TABLE 38: Classifications of RPE filters]
– the amount of particles the filter is filtering.

[p143 | prose | TABLE 38: Classifications of RPE filters]
Make sure that workers replace filters if they are damaged, or if they notice a change in their ability to breathe easily.

[p143 | prose | TABLE 38: Classifications of RPE filters]
There is no overall rule about when filters on respirators should be changed – each situation will be different. Ask the manufacturer or a competent person when and how filters should be changed.

[p143 | prose | TABLE 38: Classifications of RPE filters]
Filters should be maintained according to the manufacturer’s instructions.

[p143 | prose | Fit testing]
Fit testing

[p143 | prose | Fit testing]
RPE is only effective if it fits properly and is used according to the manufacturer’s guidelines. This includes positive-pressure systems, for example, PAPR or SARs. Tight fitting masks need to be fit tested in negative pressure mode. RPE should form a tight seal to the wearer’s skin.

[p143 | prose | Fit testing]
Facial differences mean that one kind of RPE is unlikely to fit all. These differences can be significant between people of different genders and of different ethnicities.

[p143 | prose | Fit testing]
Arrange for workers to have a fit test for RPE before they start removal work. Fit testing should be done in line with the recommendations set out in ISO 16975- 3. Consider contacting a specialist or the supplier of the RPE to fit test workers.

[p143 | prose | Fit testing]
There are two types of fit testing:

[p143 | prose | Fit testing]
– A quantitative fit test uses an instrument to measure the amount of air that leaks around the seal of RPE. This produces a result called a fit factor, which is a measure of how well the RPE fits the user.

[p143 | prose | Fit testing]
– A qualitative fit test uses a harmless substance (usually an aerosol) that the wearer can smell or taste. If the wearer can smell or taste the substance while wearing the RPE, this suggests that the RPE does not fit correctly and is letting unfiltered air in.

[p144 | prose | Fit testing]
17.0 Personal protective equipment (PPE) Only quantitative fit testing provides objective, accurate, and reliable results. Fit testing should be repeated: – at least every year – if a worker loses or gains weight – if a worker has a change to their facial features (for example, after dental work or facial surgery).

[p144 | prose | Seal checks]
Seal checks

[p144 | prose | Seal checks]
A seal check is a check to make sure that there is a good seal between the respirator and the face. Workers should do a seal check each time a respirator is put on. Always follow the manufacturer’s instructions when carrying out a seal check. Figures 46 and 47 show the seal check procedures for negative-pressure respirators and positive-pressure respirators.

[p144 | prose | Negative-pressure seal check]
Negative-pressure seal check

[p144 | prose | Negative-pressure seal check]
1. Block the filters with the palms of your hands. 2. Gently breath in and hold for about 10 seconds. 3. Check to see if the facepiece is collapsing slightly. 4. If the facepiece stays collapsed and there are no more leaks between the face and facepiece, the respirator is properly fitted. 5. If leaks are detected, the straps of the respirator should be adjusted, and the fit check should be repeated.

[p144 | prose | Positive-pressure seal check]
Positive-pressure seal check

[p144 | prose | Positive-pressure seal check]
1. Block the exhalation valve with the palm of your hand. 2. Gently breath out and hold for about 10 seconds. 3. Check to see if the facepiece is bulging slightly. 4. If the facepiece stays bulging and there are no more leaks between the face and facepiece, the respirator is properly fitted. 5. If leaks are detected, the straps of the respirator should be adjusted, and the seal check should be repeated.

[p145 | prose | Other pre-use checks for RPE]
Other pre-use checks for RPE

[p145 | prose | Other pre-use checks for RPE]
The RPE should be checked to make sure it is clean and in good condition before each use. The wearer should be trained to do this. Table 39 summarises the pre- use checks.

[p145 | table | Other pre-use checks for RPE]
| DISPOSABLE AND HALF-MASKS | FULL-PIECE POWERED RESPIRATORS (INCLUDING CHECKS FOR DISPOSABLE AND HALF-MASKS) |
| --- | --- |
| The facepiece is clean and in good physical condition with no damage or distortion to the face seal. | Visor, seals, gaskets. and ‘O’ rings are present and in good condition with components that can connect securely. All threaded connectors and seals are in good condition. |
| Head harness and anchorage points are undamaged and can hold the facepiece on the face correctly, securely, and comfortably | Battery charge/condition |
| All valves (especially exhale valves) are present, in good condition and correctly seated | Airflow rate for power-assisted and powered respirators has been checked and meets manufacturer’s specification |
| Filters are correct, in good condition, in date and securely fitted | The RPE is complete and correctly assembled |
| Any additional tests in accordance with the manufacturer’s instructions |  |

[p145 | prose | Other pre-use checks for RPE]
TABLE 39: Summary of pre-use checks when wearing RPE

[p145 | prose | Hygiene, cleaning and maintenance of RPE]
Hygiene, cleaning and maintenance of RPE

[p145 | prose | Hygiene, cleaning and maintenance of RPE]
Each worker should have their own RPE where practicable. Having individual RPE minimises the risk that respiratory infections (for example, COVID-19) will spread between workers.

[p145 | prose | Hygiene, cleaning and maintenance of RPE]
RPE should be labelled so it is easy to tell who it belongs to. This minimises the risk that a worker uses someone else’s RPE.

[p145 | prose | Hygiene, cleaning and maintenance of RPE]
After each use, RPE should be cleaned and disinfected (if needed) and stored in a sealable container in safe place away from areas contaminated with asbestos. Follow the manufacturer’s instructions for cleaning and storage.

[p145 | prose | Important]
Important

[p145 | prose | Important]
Never store clean RPE with contaminated RPE.

[p145 | prose | Important]
See Section 15: Personal decontamination for more information on decontamination

[p145 | prose | Important]
Follow the manufacturer’s instructions to clean, maintain, and perform additional checks and tests. Filters will need close monitoring. For example:

[p145 | prose | Important]
– damaged filters should be replaced following the manufacturer’s instructions.

[p145 | prose | Important]
– workers should replace filters if they are damaged or if breathing resistance increases.

[p145 | prose | Important]
– some filter brands may not be usable after exposure to conditions like a decontamination shower. The PCBU should check with the supplier about the filter’s effectiveness after such exposure.

[p145 | prose | Important]
Keep a record of inspections, maintenance, and any defects fixed for five years. Only use manufacturer-approved spare parts.

[p145 | prose | Important]
Never modify RPE without the manufacturer’s approval.

[p146 | prose | Medical conditions]
Medical conditions

[p146 | prose | Medical conditions]
If workers are likely to wear RPE regularly, encourage them to have a medical assessment. A medical assessment can help to determine if:

[p146 | prose | Medical conditions]
– a worker is fit to wear RPE long-term, and

[p146 | prose | Medical conditions]
– a worker is fit to wear RPE for long periods at a time.

[p146 | prose | Medical conditions]
Consider any medical conditions that workers may have when deciding on the most appropriate RPE.

[p146 | prose | Medical conditions]
Positive-pressure RPE may be more suitable for workers who have medical conditions that make negative-pressure RPE difficult to use.

[p146 | prose | Monitoring RPE use]
Monitoring RPE use

[p146 | prose | Monitoring RPE use]
Appoint a competent person to take responsibility for monitoring and recording all aspects of RPE use during removal work.

[p146 | prose | Monitoring RPE use]
Records of RPE are sometimes called a respiratory protection programme.

[p146 | prose | Monitoring RPE use]
A respiratory protection programme should be reviewed regularly to check that requirements are still being met. Keep track of any changes made as a result of reviews.

[p146 | prose | Monitoring RPE use]
Monitoring should be documented in writing, and include:

[p146 | prose | Monitoring RPE use]
– information about the risks workers may be exposed to

[p146 | prose | Monitoring RPE use]
– the information that has been used to select RPE for workers

[p146 | prose | Monitoring RPE use]
– records of RPE training for each worker

[p146 | prose | Monitoring RPE use]
– records of fit testing for each worker

[p146 | prose | Monitoring RPE use]
– information about fit testing procedures

[p146 | prose | Monitoring RPE use]
– information about maintenance, cleaning, and decontamination processes

[p146 | prose | Monitoring RPE use]
– information about replacement and repair processes

[p146 | prose | Monitoring RPE use]
– information about how the monitoring process is audited.

[p146 | prose | Training workers to use RPE]
Training workers to use RPE

[p146 | prose | Training workers to use RPE]
Workers should be given clear training, information, and instructions on:

[p146 | prose | Training workers to use RPE]
– why a particular type of RPE is selected, its limitations, and which filters to use

[p146 | prose | Training workers to use RPE]
– how to fit and use the RPE properly

[p146 | prose | Training workers to use RPE]
– the importance of wearing a tight-fitting facepiece correctly, the need for fit testing when selecting equipment, and how to check the face seal before each use

[p146 | prose | Training workers to use RPE]
– the risk of taking off or setting down RPE in a contaminated area, and what to do in a medical emergency

[p146 | prose | Training workers to use RPE]
– how to recognise airflow reduction (where relevant) and what to do if it happens

[p146 | prose | Training workers to use RPE]
– how to check the RPE before each use

[p146 | prose | Training workers to use RPE]
– the need for thorough examination and maintenance of the RPE

[p146 | prose | Training workers to use RPE]
– how to clean and decontaminate RPE after use and store it properly for reuse.

[p146 | prose | Training workers to use RPE]
Removal workers should get refresher training on how to use RPE at least once a year.

[p146 | prose | Training workers to use RPE]
PCBUs should not assume workers will always use RPE properly just because they have worn it before.

[p146 | prose | Training workers to use RPE]
For more guidance for businesses and workers and the respirator selector tool see WorkSafe’s webpage Respiratory Protective Equipment (RPE)

[p148 | prose | Health monitoring looks at whether a worker’s health is being harmed because of what they are being exposed to while working.]
Health monitoring looks at whether a worker’s health is being harmed because of what they are being exposed to while working.

[p148 | prose | 18.1 Purpose of health monitoring]
18.1 Purpose of health monitoring

[p148 | prose | 18.1 Purpose of health monitoring]
Health monitoring can be used to detect if workers are experiencing health effects from potential exposures.

[p148 | prose | 18.1 Purpose of health monitoring]
Asbestos-related diseases take years to appear, but doctors can run tests to monitor the health of people who work with asbestos. However, do not use these tests to assess asbestos control measures. Use asbestos air monitoring, especially personal air monitoring, to check if control measures are working.

[p148 | prose | 18.1 Purpose of health monitoring]
Working in hazardous conditions can adversely affect workers’ health – in both the short (acute) and long term (chronic). This includes when the work involves substances that are harmful to people’s health (substances hazardous to health).

[p148 | prose | 18.2 Who does health monitoring apply to?]
18.2 Who does health monitoring apply to?

[p148 | prose | 18.2 Who does health monitoring apply to?]
Workers must get health monitoring if they are at risk of asbestos exposure when doing any of the following:

[p148 | prose | 18.2 Who does health monitoring apply to?]
– Class A licensed asbestos removal work

[p148 | prose | 18.2 Who does health monitoring apply to?]
– Class B licensed asbestos removal work that lasts more than four weeks in any 12-month period

[p148 | prose | 18.2 Who does health monitoring apply to?]
– any other ongoing asbestos-related work or unlicensed asbestos removal where there is a risk of airborne asbestos exposure.

[p148 | prose | 18.2 Who does health monitoring apply to?]
Self-employed PCBUs should monitor their own health to comply with the Act.

[p148 | prose | 18.3 Who is responsible for making sure health monitoring is conducted?]
18.3 Who is responsible for making sure health monitoring is conducted?

[p148 | prose | 18.3 Who is responsible for making sure health monitoring is conducted?]
For asbestos removal work, it is the duty of the asbestos removalist (PCBU) to provide health monitoring for its removal workers.

[p148 | prose | 18.3 Who is responsible for making sure health monitoring is conducted?]
PCBUs should monitor their worker participation rates in health monitoring at regular periods, to make sure that they are fulfilling their duty to provide it.

[p149 | prose | 18.4 Informing workers about health monitoring]
18.4 Informing workers about health monitoring

[p149 | prose | 18.4 Informing workers about health monitoring]
The PCBU must tell its workers about any asbestos-related health monitoring requirements before they start any work that may expose them to asbestos.

[p149 | prose | 18.4 Informing workers about health monitoring]
The PCBUs must give those workers the following information:

[p149 | prose | 18.4 Informing workers about health monitoring]
– that the PCBU has a duty to carry out health monitoring

[p149 | prose | 18.4 Informing workers about health monitoring]
– what health hazard triggered the requirements for the health monitoring (in this case, asbestos)

[p149 | prose | 18.4 Informing workers about health monitoring]
– what the health monitoring will consist of and how it will be done

[p149 | prose | 18.4 Informing workers about health monitoring]
– the information that has to be given to the occupational health practitioner – that the PCBU has a duty to obtain a health monitoring report from the occupational health practitioner

[p149 | prose | 18.4 Informing workers about health monitoring]
– that the PCBU has a duty to notify the regulator and other relevant PCBUs

[p149 | prose | 18.4 Informing workers about health monitoring]
– how health monitoring reports will be kept, stored, and shared, including keeping confidentiality

[p149 | prose | 18.4 Informing workers about health monitoring]
– the reasons for health monitoring:

[p149 | prose | 18.4 Informing workers about health monitoring]
to help the PCBU reduce the risk of exposure to health hazards in the workplace

[p149 | prose | 18.4 Informing workers about health monitoring]
to enable the PCBU and other PCBUs in the workplace to take remedial action within the workplace to manage the health risk

[p149 | prose | 18.4 Informing workers about health monitoring]
to help with treating and protecting workers who are or were exposed to health hazards

[p149 | prose | 18.4 Informing workers about health monitoring]
– that they will inform WorkSafe if test results indicate a worker may have a disease, illness, or injury caused by the work that triggered the health monitoring. This helps WorkSafe carry out its functions under the Act.

[p149 | prose | 18.5 Components of health monitoring]
18.5 Components of health monitoring

[p149 | prose | 18.5 Components of health monitoring]
Health monitoring must include specific checks to monitor the health of workers at risk of asbestos exposure, unless a medical practitioner recommends otherwise. This is a called a ‘full asbestos medical’.

[p149 | prose | 18.5 Components of health monitoring]
Table 40 shows the checks needed to monitor a worker’s health in these situations.

[p149 | table | 18.5 Components of health monitoring]
| HEALTH MONITORING REQUIREMENT | DETAILS |
| --- | --- |
| Physical examination | This should focus on the respiratory system and include a lung function test (FEV1and FVC). |
| Note: A chest x-ray (PA and lateral) is no longer needed unless recommended by a specialist. |  |
| Worker’s history | Demographic, medical, and occupational history |
| Exposure records | Personal exposure to asbestos, including: - relevant risk assessment reports - air monitoring results - personal exposure monitoring results. Investigation reports if the airborne contamination standard for asbestos was exceeded. |

[p149 | prose | 18.5 Components of health monitoring]
TABLE 40: Components of a full asbestos medical for health monitoring

[p150 | prose | 18.6 When health monitoring occurs]
18.6 When health monitoring occurs

[p150 | prose | 18.6 When health monitoring occurs]
A full asbestos medical must be conducted every two years following the initial medical examination, which must occur within four weeks of the worker starting asbestos removal work

[p150 | prose | 18.6 When health monitoring occurs]
A full asbestos medical should be done every two years from when asbestos work starts, no matter when the worker started work with their current PCBU.

[p150 | prose | 18.6 When health monitoring occurs]
Table 41 shows the health monitoring calendar to use for licensed asbestos removal workers.

[p150 | table | 18.6 When health monitoring occurs]
| YEARS AFTER STARTING ASBESTOS REMOVAL WORK | PROCEDURE |
| --- | --- |
| Within four weeks of commencing work | Initial full asbestos medical |
| Two years | Full asbestos medical |
| Four years | Full asbestos medical |
| Every two years thereafter | Full asbestos medical |

[p150 | prose | 18.6 When health monitoring occurs]
TABLE 41:

[p150 | prose | 18.6 When health monitoring occurs]
Health monitoring calendar for licensed asbestos removal workers

[p150 | prose | 18.7 The people who carry out health monitoring]
18.7 The people who carry out health monitoring

[p150 | prose | 18.7 The people who carry out health monitoring]
An occupational health practitioner experienced in health monitoring must conduct or supervise the health monitoring.

[p150 | prose | 18.8 Paying for health monitoring]
18.8 Paying for health monitoring

[p150 | prose | 18.8 Paying for health monitoring]
The PCBU must pay all health monitoring costs for their workers. If multiple PCBUs are responsible for a worker’s health monitoring, they can agree that one PCBU will organise it. However, all PCBUs must share the costs equally unless they agree otherwise.

[p150 | prose | 18.9 Information for the occupational health practitioner]
18.9 Information for the occupational health practitioner

[p150 | prose | 18.9 Information for the occupational health practitioner]
The PCBU who commissions health monitoring must give the following information to the occupational health practitioner:

[p150 | prose | 18.9 Information for the occupational health practitioner]
– the PCBU’s name and address

[p150 | prose | 18.9 Information for the occupational health practitioner]
– each worker’s name and date of birth

[p150 | prose | 18.9 Information for the occupational health practitioner]
– a description of the type of work the workers are doing that triggered the need for health monitoring

[p150 | prose | 18.9 Information for the occupational health practitioner]
– if the workers have started the work involving asbestos, and how long have they been doing it.

[p150 | prose | 18.10 Health monitoring report details]
18.10 Health monitoring report details

[p150 | prose | 18.10 Health monitoring report details]
